air

GLADYS TABER 

Oliver Heron confronts a confused world, the world of today, and finds that his only solution lies in facing reality with personal courage. A sensitive, intelligent man, he has been swept under by the tidal wave of the depression. His past is a mystery; the future appears blank; and then he finds Helen. 

In their passionate, tragic love story it becomes evident that the world may change but love is forever the same. 

What happens to a man when he can't afford love? What does he do when he has no security to offer the woman he has dreamed of? Here is a problem which is by no means hypothetical in the present troubled state of existence, and here in "Tomorrow May Be Fair" is one of the answers. 

Mrs. Taber's first novel, "Late Climbs

(Continued from Front Flap) 

the Sun," dealt with the lost generation. 

In this new novel she is concerned with the men and women who reach maturity in a world of terrific pressure and economic deprivation. This study of contemporary life should interest people who are concerned with what is happening to people in the world today. 

The scene is laid principally in New 

York, with glimpses of Tammany, penthouse parties, literary agencies, subways, Fifth Avenue, home-relief bureaux, restaurants and shops. Contrasting with this an isolated farming section of Connecticut plays its part in the unravelling of Oliver Heron's destiny. The characters moving in and out of Oliver's life are varied, sophisticated New Yorkers, Lewisohn, the Jewish doctor, a rare and stimulating cynic, and Gilda, his mistress; politicians, playwrights, society women, job-seekers and the starving, petty officials, and the solid, earthy

Lithuanian farm folk in the hills of Connecticut. 2

HELEN HULL

PART ONE

* * ¶ When he saw her again, the blood thundered in his ears. His senses whirled like whirling leaves, then his mind stilled and in the light, clear as glass, he saw her coming toward him. "But of course you know Helen Carlyle!" cried 

Amy, "Helen, here's Oliver Heron." She waved her cigarette holder. "Isn't he the boy you knew that summer on the shore years ago?"

The voices died away. He saw her, not in this crowded 

New York apartment, not moving through a haze of dead cigarettes. He saw her, a young thin girl in a high-necked white sweater and dark plaid skirt, a velvet ribbon bound childishly around hair the clear color of amber. "Well, Oliver," she said.

He shook himself from the dream. He must perceive

her as she was. She was not a young girl. She must be, he remembered, about thirty-two or three. A tall, quiet woman, walking superbly. Her hair was darker now, the shade of sun-swept autumn leaves, but the eyes were the same, a curious indefinite color under the dark long lashes. She wore no sweater, she had a dress of some smooth stuff, cool as sea water, subtly cut, caught up on the shoulder with an ivory clasp. "It just shows you how small the world is," said Amy 

Wallace, "I always say you never can tell when you'll see somebody again. I never will forget, in Paris, seeing my history teacher at Napoleon's tomb. I got away, but what a turn it gave me!" "Well, Helen," said Oliver.

Her hand was cool, with fine sensitive fingers and a square palm. It was ringless, the hand he held, but on the other, the wedding ring and the engagement ring made a dazzling brilliance, too heavy, too elaborate. "Now you two find a place to sit down and chat quietly," advised Amy Wallace. "I'll drag the unsuccessful playwrights to the dining-room and clear the davenport." She clapped her hands together. "Come on, everybody, it's time to eat!"

There was a general movement through the open French doors toward the buffet, spread lavishly with 

Amy's newest discoveries in food. Silver and rock crystal, lace and candles, too many flowers, a movable chromium bar, and the smell of steaming food evidenced Amy's hospitality. Amy kept her pent-house, she hoped, like a true New Yorker, but a childhood of Methodist church suppers in Sac City, Iowa, betrayed her. There

were fifteen or twenty guests, and there was dinner for a whole congregation. "You just have a good talk," she said.

Thrown at each other abruptly, they had no words. 

Helen took a cigarette and moved to the window. Her presence made the other women seem suddenly indistinct. Oliver watched them go, flat chests, bony narrow arms, and a blur of painted faces alike as plates in a dinner set.

Modern. His three months in New York accustomed him to their tired brilliant eyes and high-keyed voices. 

But now they were strange again. Modern. He thought perhaps there was nothing modern about Helen. She belonged to an earlier age, with her smooth sweet breast and remote eyes and serene mouth. She made you think of Diana and Daphne and ships beating down the wind to Troy, not of saxophones and white lights and steel buildings. "I didn't expect to find you at Amy's." Helen smiled at him. "Nor I you." "But where did you come from?" "From Iowa," he said. "I had a letter to Amy. I'm tutoring her son Billy." 

She widened her eyes. "But Oliver you didn't ___” "No. The college closed. Half the students had to go back to the farm-to live on locusts and wild honey, I expect. A fourth of them, working their way through, lost their jobs. You know, these small colleges "I thought you were at the University." 

"Well,” he grinned at her. “I was. I quit. I had some

ideas of my own. They seemed important at the time," his voice died away. He lit a cigarette. “I had a chance to build up the department in English at the little place. 

As I wanted. It seemed a good thing—at the time. Of course, it wasn't. The depression "I'm sorry." 

He shrugged his shoulders. "How about you, Helen?" "We still live here. Brant hasn't suffered from the hard times. He's at a directors' conference now. 

That's why I he'll be along presently. Elizabeth and

Joan still go to a private school." A shadow went over her face. "We might have a cocktail," she said.

Oliver found the maid and came back with frosty glasses. "Let's get a breath of air," said Helen. She swept aside the heavy white satin draperies and moved to the terrace where an odd assortment of fashionable plants stood pale in the moonlight.

They leaned against the stone parapet. Far down, diminished, the Fifth Avenue traffic flung its noise and light against the dark. Sound came up to them shrill and thin. 

Little glazed eyes of light blinked at them. The dark bulk of Central Park lunged across the middle distance, and beyond, the million-windowed lights of Manhattan smote the sky. "A whole world,” said Helen, draining her glass. "A million worlds," said Oliver. He laughed suddenly.

Here he stood, with Helen, mysteriously evoked from cigarette smoke and the fumes of cocktails and highballs. "Is it a coincidence, seeing you here?” "No," she said thoughtfully, "in New York, sooner

or later, you meet everybody you ever knew. It might be next week or next month, but it's inevitable. I suppose it really is a number of small towns, and you know in a small town, anyone in the same set I'm afraid you couldn't help meeting me when you went to Iowa to teach. Are you sorry?" "What do you think?” "I think we should have another cocktail, before we eat." "This is my sixth, I believe," said Oliver, coming back. "And always at the seventh, I feel like a toast. It becomes important to drink to something." "What then?" Helen lifted her glass. Her hand curved softly around frosted crystal, she smiled her remote, shadowy smile. "To yesterday," he said.

They were silent, their minds reaching toward each other. Oliver remembered their last days together. A boy and girl on a summer night saying good-bye. Helen's mother, taking her away, had been entirely right, of course. Certainly he had never done anything since to make her regret snatching Helen from him while they were still almost children. Helen had made one of those brilliant marriages with a man whose sound financial security withstood the sterile air of the depression. She had everything. Helen's mother was certainly right. 

Even in those far away days, he hadn't been much of a social light. He didn't even have a family, a background. He was nameless and separate as a paper doll cut from a blank piece of paper. Just a young school boy in love with Helen.

"What are you thinking?" asked Helen. "Long, long thoughts," said Oliver, "but not the thoughts of a pent-house party."

So far as he knew, he had no family. That, in itself, prejudiced Helen's mother. We don't even know who his people were, he could hear her say in her sugared voice. His memory went back no farther than a school for very young boys, where he was the youngest and smallest. After that, he remembered an older boys' school, where nobody sent him candy and boxing gloves. 

Annually, a lawyer from the city came to arrange for his transportation to a summer camp and back again. 

The lawyer was a white-faced man with deep grey eyes.

His name was Merritt. When Oliver asked him questions, he averted his eyes and answered briefly that his father and mother were dead. Some sort of accident. 

That was all he knew, or would admit knowing. The insurance was enough to educate him, he said.

Mr. Merritt had a way of looking sharply at Oliver, with his lips thin, as if he hated to look at him. So far as he knew, Oliver never did anything very bad. A few minor escapades, like the one shooting the cap off a boy's head with a target pistol. Mr. Merritt had cut off his pocket money for six weeks as punishment. And the target pistol, which he had saved for, was taken away. 

Summer camps and boys' schools, and so up to college and university, with Mr. Merritt appearing and disappearing like a minor genie, or mailing a certified check for tuition and clothes and travelling expenses to some other camp and school. But where the money actually came from, Oliver never knew. There was somethinghe would learn after a while.

Of course, there had been an earlier life, before school. 

At first a cloudy consciousness of it lived in his dreams, elusive memories.

Sometimes, even after he was in college, he fancied he could remember odd little things from some other life. They swam lazily up like dark fish, there was a flash of a fin and they were gone in the stream. A woman in a blue dress under an appletree. A wooden rockinghorse with a missing eye. Little soldiers in faded blue coats marching single file above his head. 

He disliked walking in wet grass. He couldn't get over the silly notion that he would fall, the wet grass choking him. There were other little things-gradually they faded as he grew older. Even his vague curiosity as to where he had been born, faded. He accepted the deliberate screen drawn between him and his background. 

Merritt died when Oliver was the first year out of college. Whatever he might have revealed when Oliver was educated, died with him. The allowance stopped, and the office reported that the stocks standing in his name were no longer paying dividends. 

All right, all right, he thought impatiently, no use going back over that now. No use, ever, going back of today, nor forward to tomorrow. Only live, live in the moment, squeeze time like an orange. 

What was going on in Helen's head? Did she know how at the sight of her, the one brief summer when he had tasted ecstasy, came back with unbearable sweetness? Not even a whole summer. Helen's mother wasn't one to waste time. Helen, trembling on the verge of love, was whisked away before the season ripened into August. There was, there had been then, a kind of passive-

ness in Helen. She had gone away, that young girl in the white sweater and plaid skirt, vanished completely overnight. 

Was it only an echo of his desperation that held him now? He stared at his glass. He must remember that he was tutoring Billy Wallace, and that was his only source of income. He must remember also that it depended, naturally enough, not on the quality of his teaching, but on Amy's fondness for him. "Helen, I'm desperately poor," he said suddenly, staring at the wet empty glass. "It occurs to me that it's important you should know, immediately, how poor 

I am.' ,, "I suppose our entire generation," mused Helen, “will be conditioned by poverty. Either one is poor and afraid, or rich and still more afraid of being poor. All of us here keep trying to get away from it.” "Are you all through reminiscing?" Amy hovered in the doorway. "Do come and have some lobster patties before they're all gone. There are three poets here tonight, and you know how poets eat." She linked her hand in Oliver's arm. "Come in, darling, and let me feed you. Helen, you must try the canapes." She stood, smiling, blonde and blue-eyed, and carefully made-up, and quite definitely dressed in a youthful model, a trifle tight over the bosom, a shade too close under the hips. "Come along, Oliver," she repeated. She led him in, leaning against him to whisper, "I hope you behaved nicely. Helen's husband may be able to do something for you. He's on some very wealthy educational foundation. 

There's nothing," said Amy complacently, "like meeting the right people."

Oliver set his lips. He was completely himself again, the blood somber in his veins. He made his way through the crowd of right people, half of them dizzy with liquor, the men with tired eyes, the women raising their excited fatigued voices higher and higher. "You must talk to everybody," said Amy happily. "You need contacts. I've got an editor somewherethere, in the Russian corner under the icon. You might do an article for him. And there's Lessing-the one who paints those warped, naked people. He likes struggling young men. And Doctor David Lewisohn, he's terribly famous-babies, I think, and cutting people up. Gilda 

Ferris is beside him, she's his mistress. And the Rudolfs are at the bar. He writes plays."

Oliver's cool palm touched her bare arm. "Am I the only skeleton at the feast?" he asked idly. "Don't be silly." She pressed closer. "You are thinbut you'll soon be fat and sleek." Her voice was drowsy, possessive.

Somebody was about to sing. The noise ebbed a little, conversational ripples subsided. At the end of the drawing-room, above the carved elegance of the piano, Oliver saw a dark, ugly face. It was the face of a man violent with ambition, a typical hard-mouthed, bitter-eyed New Yorker.

The full mouth opened, the singer threw back his head and made a gesture with his hand, to be sure of attention. Then sweet and strong and vibrant with oriental passion, the tones beat into the room. 

Somewhere it was night, wide and still, and someone had made a bed of green boughs beside a dying fire. 

There was no moon, only a sky as deep as death, and the

woods, profound as sleep. On the bed of boughs lay a woman, her hair was a smoky flame above the broad brow and the long, glimmering limbs. And her mouth was the mouth of Helen. "I do like a little singing," said Amy, complacently, "but not too much. Do you like his voice? He's quite famous-but isn't he a little weak in the lower register?" 

Oliver lit a cigarette. "Let's turn on the radio," called Amy brightly. She hummed, "We'll make hay while the sun shines, We'll make love while it rains — "her voice was platinum, hard, bright, and flat-surfaced. She poked an olive between her lips, and nibbled the stone. "There comes Helen's husband," she said, "late train from Washington. I'll introduce you." "I've met him," said Oliver shortly. "I went to school with him." "You went to school with why didn't you say so before?" Amy pinched him. "You just like to be mysterious. It's a pose!"

Oliver nodded. His eyes devoured Helen's husband, Brant Carlyle. The successful Brant Carlyle, grown from a thin spindly youth to a long spare man with soft sensual lips and bitter greenish black eyes. His nose, angled sharply, gave the whole face a predatory look. 

He put out a long lean hand and spared a smile for Oliver. "Well, the Heron has flown in from the wilds!" he said.

He had known it for years, but now it must be admitted as a tangible thing, this marriage. His mind re-

jected it, he felt a bodily nausea. He fought it down. Smiled. "New York is something of a wilderness too," he said. "At least to me."

The full sullen mouth smiled. "It is apt to confuse strangers. وو "There's still something to eat," Amy told Brant. "Help yourself.” "Don't trouble yourself. I expected Helen would wait for me so I went round to the house first. That's the reason I'm so late." His voice was patient. "You said you weren't sure you would get back,” observed Helen mildly. "You might have waited. Not that I would keep you from anything." He smiled again. "Oh, do come and eat," said Amy impatiently. "It'll be cold."

Brant nodded briefly and strode toward the diningroom. "Helen!" he called over his shoulder. "Helen!" His sharp peremptory voice cut through the rising hum of conversation.

Oliver, taking refuge in another drink, watched Brant lean over her, speaking rapidly. Helen's head was bent, the long line of throat shadowed. Something was the matter with him. He felt suddenly a knife blade in his lungs. Loneliness wrapped around his throat, cold and strangling. Brant's hand closed on Helen's arm. 

She shrank away, turning her hand, palm upward in a curious helpless gesture.

The hot liquor eased Oliver's throat momentarily. He was drinking too much, that was the trouble. But why

not? What difference did it make? He could see himself dash down the glass suddenly and shout to the whole roomful see me! see me! What have I got for thirty-six years of life? Who wants a mind stuffed with ideas? Who wants a body alive as fire, hungry as hell?

Where shall I go, born in a world that has no place for me? "I'm much too young to be wasted," he said aloud, a little drunk. "Yes, much." Amy put a soft, soft hand on his hot wrist.

He went out past the wrought-iron Russian gate, into the bedroom where his hat and coat lay lifeless on the lavender spread. He sat down, heavily, resting his head in his hand, conscious that he was shaking. I must be ill, he told himself. I've gone mad, being alone so much, and hungry so often.

Over the dressing table was a carved Florentine panel, delicate with blue and gold virgins. The tallest virgin had a white flower in her hand. The hand was long, narrow, pale. I'd like to believe in you, said Oliver. I'd like to pray to you. But what should I pray for? It's too late for prayer. If you had been kind, you would never have led me here, you and your God, to see Helen.

All this time, he said, Helen has been asleep in my consciousness, and it was like the memory of someone dead, that young girl in a white sweater and plaid skirt, with amber hair blowing against the dusk. Now I see her, and all desire wakes up. Everything I haven't had -and the sterility, the lack of love, the futile ambitions are in me now like rotting swamp water. I see them, in

the light. Yes, and all dreams and loveliness are in Helen. 

Oh God, it isn't possible only to see her-and see her like this, and be destroyed by desire! This is insanity! 

He stood up and went to the bathroom. There, in the red white and blue splendor of Amy's nautical bath, he plunged his head in cold water. Over the row of scent bottles painted with still ships, he saw himself in the glass, oddly framed in twisted rope. Curiously he looked, as at a stranger. This fair-haired, grey-eyed man with thin sensitive mouth and hard narrow jaw-who was he? I look English, he thought wryly. For all I know,

I'm an illegitimate son of the Prince of Wales. Or is he old enough to have sired me? But English. One of the steady blonde Englishmen. I need a red coat and a pair of spotted coach dogs. I must go down to the sea again. She walks, the lady of my delight, a shepherdess of sheep. That man, out there, shares the same bathroom with her. He may even use her toothpaste. And what, in God's name, is that to me? 

He wiped his face on a dolphin, threw down the towel, tripped on the braided rope rug, slammed the door. I'll be sea-sick sure enough if I don't get out of the cabin, he said.

He moved carefully toward the diningroom, now empty, and found some black coffee. Man isn't so much, he reflected, dropping in two lumps, when a little something out of a glass can drown the mind. Amy came in, and whispered, "Stay a while they're all going now!"

Helen had a long dark wrap on. Her face, pale and tired, was framed in some soft black stuff of the collar.

Brant, lighting a long black cigar, stood impatiently waiting, chewing at his mouth. "Come along, Helen!" he said imperiously. 

Helen held her hand out to Oliver. "Come to dinner," she said. "How about tomorrow night?" "I have already made plans for us tomorrow night," said Brant. "Oh, I didn't know." "And Thursday night we're playing bridge with the Bernsteins." "I'll just drop in some day and call," said Oliver. "Well, I'll be in tomorrow afternoon," Brant took 

Helen's arm. "Come in about four for a cocktail. Talk over our college days." His laugh was thin and sharp. 

He turned to say something to Amy.

Helen leaned forward. "I take the children to the park at three," she said in her low quiet voice. "If you don't mind walking 

They were gone. They were all gone. The sound of the descending elevator died down the stone corridor. Amy pushed a button. The heavy carved doors folded shut. 

She opened the long windows. The white satin draperies leaned against the fresh air, blue smoke drifted out. 

Oliver sat down on the incredible softness of the divan. 

He felt suddenly so tired that nothing really mattered. 

Nothing. No, not even Helen, riding now through the canyoned night beside her husband. He wanted to rest, to sleep and cease thinking. But he must rouse himself to consider Amy. He had rent to pay tomorrow and the laundry man came at eight. It was the last day of June. 

Perhaps Amy would stop the lessons. He had to have

them! If he could be free from the struggle for bare life, for say, twenty four hours! If he could have a check book and sign one check-Oliver Heron. Just to write his name clean and clear on the paper, instead of digging a pinched bill from under the carpet. It ate a man's pride like corrosive acid.

He looked up, his somber eyes on Amy. She was like a peony, full blown, in her rich and elaborate garden. 

Helen was a water-lily. "Well, Amy?" He heard his own voice, level, casual. 

Amy came toward him. Hot sun in the garden, a bee heavy with pollen, a hop toad putting a small black hand on a dead fly, sweet rich petals, voluptuous and undesired, Oliver saw them all. "Wasn't it a lovely party?" said Amy. "I do like everybody to have a good time." "A lovely party," he echoed her.

She sat close to him, leaning forward so that he could smell the expensive odor of her hair. "You've been good to me," he said, with effort. His head was blank. "I 

He forced a stiff smile. expect I had better go, Amy." "I'm so lonely," sighed Amy. "Yes. You must miss your husband.” "He's not coming back," said Amy. "I thought you knew that, Oliver. I'm getting a divorce next month. 

A Mexican divorce. It's terribly simple. And so new! 

You pay two hundred dollars. Imagine how cheap for a divorce! And you do it all by mail." "That can't be legal." 

She laughed. "Don't be old-fashioned."

He lit a cigarette, the last Chesterfield from a crushed pack. Amy took an ivory tipped imported cigarette from a silver box on the table. "Well," he said, slowly, carefully. "I do you want to be congratulated?” 

Amy flung down the cigarette, stamped her gold slipper. "Don't be stupid!" she cried. "You know what I want! Oliver-have you been-playing with me this month?" "No, Amy. But I thought you thought you were being kind " he stumbled. "I 

She unfolded a petal. "I keep forgetting you don't know anything about women," she cried penitently. "she "Oliver -" her hands opened to him. you do 

There was defeat in his voice. "Amy, I————— I didn't dream you could care for me-really. You're toogood to me!"

He need never be alone again. He turned to her with a sudden gust of passion, but as her arms went around him with suffocating sweetness, he felt an anguish of loneliness, deep and profound. This beautiful body was no answer. The ripe full breasts were no harvest for his hunger. He stumbled to his feet, pushing her away. "Amy don't keep me now." His voice was harsh. "I'll come back tomorrow. You've got to be sure. You're so-beautiful-and you have everything —" 

Secure, assuaged, she smiled at him. "Tomorrow, then."

With his finger hard on the buzzer, he wished there might never be a tomorrow to trouble him. Of course he

was committed. Even the buttons on the elevator boy's coat glinted at him with wisdom. "I don' trus' dis weathah," said the boy. "Shuh am a breeder." "I don't trust it either," said Oliver grimly. "Good night." 

A blast of wind struck him, heavy with dust and dead paper from the gutters. A lean weathered cat peered at him from the safety of a spilling garbage can.

He wound his silk scarf, present from Amy, around his throat as he made for the subway, still sultry from the day.

Now is the time, he thought, dropping his worn nickel in the slot, when I ought to go home and plan the subsequent course of my life. This is the hour, to pack and go back home to my parents, and arrange a pattern for the rest of life. To go back to my beginnings and take a bearing. Make a plan for the rest of life.

* * ¶ Oliver woke the next morning conscious of very dry lungs and a sandy throat. Some unhappy, fateful thought prowled just at the edge of sleep, waiting to sink its fangs in him. As he sat up in bed, swinging his long legs to the floor, he felt it. Helen's children. But of course she had children. The whole structure of society was based on marriage, family life, children. Who grew up, married, had children. There it was, and outsiders like himself might like it or not.

That was another terrible mystery. People said so easily, "She has a child." Think of the darkness, the pain, the awful majesty of this bringing life out of the body. I daresay if one realized it, wholly, one would die from the burden of knowledge. The thing that saved people, he reflected, getting out of bed, was that they kept their minds dulled. Life would be impossible otherwise. They simply didn't let themselves know. 26

He progressed to the bathroom and turned on his bath. He was fortunate this month. What star he had, must be mildly in the ascendant. He was taking care of an apartment belonging to a couple of graduate students who had gone off to some coal field to watch a strike. 

They were writing dissertations for Teachers College on the cause and prevention of strikes. They knew the whole thing already, they said, but they would just take a look. One of them was a banker's son from Des 

Moines, Oliver had known him in Iowa, the other was the nephew of an oil king. But even with them, money was pinched, so they had modestly taken a three-room apartment at Columbia instead of a suite at the St. Moritz.

Oliver bathed and shaved, wondered idly if he had the well-tubbed look of English novels. Took in the milk bottle. He had a mild satisfaction in the Sheffield label. The child in the apartment across the court was a devotee of Uncle Don, and Oliver had heard the excellence of Borden's milk from six to six thirty every night until he had actually contemplated leaning out the window and shouting, "I take Sheffield!”

He indulged himself to the extent of one large spotted grade B egg from the A and P around the corner. I suppose I'm grade B myself, he thought, cracking the stiff shell. Funny how New York favored white eggs, you couldn't beat a good brown one. Being country-bred, at least to the point of summer camps and small town schools, he was amused at the elevation of white eggs. 

Somewhere at one of the camps, he had suffered a course in cooking and had developed a kind of bachelor efficiency in the kitchen. He scraped a potato and put it

to soak in a dented pan, nipped the ends from fifteen cents' worth of green beans from a push cart. But I couldn't get a job as a maid, he thought, pouring his coffee. Might get a chop, a loin chop to go with the beans for dinner. No, there are too many people in the world, and part of them have got to be eliminated. 

I belong to that part. The world is lousy with scholars. 

Stuffed like a gas-filled balloon with teachers. Education rots on the branch. Today I must get a job. Any kind of honest labor. Oh God, this idleness will destroy me.

It eats into a man's soul. There must be some small work for me.

He sat on the studio couch smoking his after-breakfast cigarette and reading the Herald Tribune, another heritage from the students. He found the stock market weak, conditions unstable in cotton and corn. The President was proclaiming the end of the depression, and the revival of national prosperity. Nice, neat words. Paris was emotionally excited over some insignificant spies. Mussolini was encouraging the Italians to breed like rabbits. Hitler put a premium on marriage to produce real Germans. And a Mrs. Walters had run over her husband, with deliberate intent, preferring the insurance to his company.

The buzzer sounded in the kitchen, and the dumb waiter came up for the rubbish. As he opened the door, damp air surged up and dirt eddied against his face. He laid his small parcel on the rickety shelf, leaned out to call down, "All right!" and the vile smell of the superintendent's cigar rose strong up the shaft. He must smoke cow dung, thought Oliver. The opposite door

opened, cutting the dark with a beam of white light. 

Across the well, Oliver looked at the face of a young girl, a tired thin face with smudged eyes. They stared at each other without speaking, three feet of space and a universe between them. There was a desperate quietness in her eyes and about her mouth. She was so very thin. 

In his own loneliness, Oliver longed suddenly to say, "Do come and have coffee with me. And a grade B egg, nourishing.' دو Her door closed softly, he shut his own, and wiped the black dust from his hands as the dumb waiter rocketed down the chute. Their rubbish was intimate, at any rate. Resolutely he returned to his paper, skipping the social page, the horses and dogs. There was still a piece of the world left for people who owned By Request out of Do Me A Favor, running six furlongs in so many seconds. The want page looked fat, but it was deceitful. Wanted, expert designer. Wanted, man with social connections and following for a Jewish camp. Wanted, experienced fur workers. Wanted, stenographic secretary. Experienced only. Wanted, advertising man. Must have at least five years' experience in cosmetics. Wanted, chemist with experience in dye stuffs. Wanted, business man with capital to invest 

He threw down the paper, and went to his typewriter. As he made a list of teacher's agencies, the tapping keys mocked him. What a lot of rot he had pushed through the rollers, about the early Elizabethans, about the Renascence in Italy, about the symbolism of Blake. 

About the use of consonants in Anglo Saxon poetry. 

About the influence of Rousseau. And how many poems

had fed themselves to the white receptive paper, to be fodder for the wastebasket. Where are they now? Who cared, in this time, about the world of ideas? 

With the list in his pocket, he turned back the rug and found his money cache. Under a lurid and indignant near-Chinese dragon, was his bank. Of course Amy hadn't paid him. He had thirty dollars a month from his vanished income. There were two folded five-dollar bills left, and one undersized dollar, thin and weak. His share of the apartment rent must be paid when Amy paid him. Meanwhile there was the laundry, shirts ten cents apiece, a sheet and pillow slip and a towel. A haircut he must have, seventy five cents to get rid of something he never asked for. And a man must eat. 

If he telephoned, he could eat with Amy. Not a chop and a potato and beans. A clear soup, jellied, a suggestion of fish rich with butter, a steak smothered in onions, and a crisp salad. But he wouldn't telephone. Not yet. Not until night. First he must decide. He could give Amy a kind of happiness. In return for security, peace. 

Wasn't that fair enough? In God's name, why not? He really couldn't afford masculine pride. He must compromise with life, and live. He could give Amy what she wanted. A man, companionship, conversation, kisses. 

He could be shown off at parties. Take her places. His inward melancholy, native to his mind, wouldn't show. 

If he took Amy, he would be what she wanted.

Only as a swimmer pauses at the top of the wet spring board, he paused, measuring the depth below. 

He was sick of the existence he had led for months. 

Sick of isolation, loneliness, and the gradual apathy

from hopeless seeking for work. He wanted something different. And there was Amy.

He locked the door, went out on the street. It was a still sultry day, dry as a leaf. The sky had the thin pallor of hot days, glazed like a shell. People on the street walked slowly. They had forgotten what heat felt like, there was a surprise in their eyes, they dragged their feet. 

At the corner, he had his hair cut. He saw himself in the glass, eyes blank, jaw-line hard. Too much curl in his hair. It resisted the slick stuff. 

The teacher's agencies were a dreary routine. Smith and Elbert and Empire and Allied. He went in, he waited, he leafed old Literary Digests. Nobody who kept an office ever discarded a Digest. The inner office, superior chill smiles across the desk. Dear God, the eyes said, here's another of those broken-down teachers. 

What can you do with them? Sorry, Mr. Heron, we're swamped with applications. And if it were any subject but English I have a call for a football coach in 

Vermont I don't suppose that's in your line? "Hardly," said Oliver drily. "I did play. But no coaching experience.' 

A bad heart had resulted from his college athletics.

He couldn't be a snow-shoveller. Or a truck man. "Sorry, Mr. Heron, we'll notify you as soon as anything turns up. Possibly by September lieve you owe us two dollars fee." and I be- "The high cost of unemployment." He grinned. "Naturally we have to maintain our offices. When you remember we must have a staff in دو "Chicago, San Francisco, St. Louis." He paid the two

dollars. "So far," he added, "it has cost me twenty five dollars to be unemployed.'

He had to force himself to the next move. It can't take long, he said. Come on, get it over. He dove into the subway. The Subway Sun advised him that Brooklyn children must be protected against diphtheria.

The car was full of morning shoppers, women. A row of abundant bosoms swayed with the swaying car. Blood red finger-nails, painted mouths, a glitter of cheap jewelry, and the smell of sweaty silk dresses. Two pale blonde girls in the center had the air of pressed flowers. 

One of them dropped a copy of Story when she went out. Oliver picked it up.

He walked with false briskness into the office of the state employment bureau, taking out his card, I, 167. 

Behind a wooden railing, the bureau staff twittered and rustled like so many birds in an aviary. Files and desks and typewriters and long benches filled every inch of space.

Outside, on other benches sat the hopeless and expectant. Unemployed. Oliver sat down, giving up his card. 

It was eleven thirty. He opened his magazine and inspected the contents with the eye of an English teacher. 

Idly with his fountain pen (gratefully to Oliver Heron from the glee club) he wrote the theme after the titles. 

Lust. Incest. Negro murder. Insanity. Rape of child. 

Suicide. Perversion. I should think Foley and Burnett would get full of it in time, he said bitterly. It's literary dung. My God, I'd rather read the Woman's Home, if it's got to be all one thing. Life is a whole orange, neither rind alone nor pulp and juice.

"They've got to take me next," said the woman beside him suddenly. "Well, perhaps they will." "How long have you been coming here?" her faded brown eyes looked at him vaguely. "As long as I can remember," he put away his pen. 

Her face was thin. Under a rusty hat a wisp of grey hair slid down. She had a worn purse the color of a dead mouse. She kept opening and shutting it, picking at the green glass catch with stubby fingers. "I've only got five cents left," she said. "What am I to do?" "Why don't you apply for home relief?" He regarded her with sympathy. "Oh, they won't give me that," she giggled faintly. "I been getting four a week in a girls' home. Board. And

I had to go to work and have a tooth out so I had an extra bill for five dollars."

He considered life on four dollars board. "I haven't any more stockings," she said. "I can't go on the street another time because I haven't got stockings to my feet." 

Oliver nodded. "It can't go on like this!" she cried suddenly. "They'll find out it can't go on like this! They keep on telling me to come back. Come back in a week!" up "Maybe you'll get something this time." "I joined a club down in the village. I'm going to sign for the whole thing if they don't give me a job. I just as soon fight." She lowered her voice. "The women in this country won't be starved forever."

"But it isn't exactly anybody's fault." "I read when they went to killing hawgs out west," she said dully. "And turning under crops. And I read where Miriam Hopkins gets seventy five thousand dollars for a picture out in Hollywood." "It's a cock-eyed world," agreed Oliver helplessly. "Well," she shut the purse. "I get nuts, sitting here, waiting." "Here's the secretary, you go on next." "Thanks." She got up and smoothed her dress. She put her limp little summer fur around her thin neck and passed through the wooden gate. Defeat was written all over her figure. Her purse hung quivering in her hand. Even the dead feather in her hat knew there was no hope.

I wish it didn't look so much like a cattle pen, thought 

Oliver. How soon will my own eyes take on that helpless liquid look? He was tired. The weight of human struggle and suffering corroded his bones. He wished he might look at someone who was happy. Someone who was tranquil. He closed his eyes and tried to think of something untouched, and lovely. The broken Venus in Rome with her fluent body, impassioned in marble. 

The splendid fallen columns in the Forum, drenched with golden light. A little snuff box inlaid with turquoise and pearl. But these were of the past. The clock struck one. 

At two thirty, he went through the wooden gate.

An undersized man with a white gardenia in his buttonhole received him. "What do you want a job for?" he said.

Oliver smiled. "To live." "Any income?" His voice had been dipped in oil. "Thirty dollars a month."

The beaked nose quivered. "Don't you know there are families in the tenements living on ten dollars a month?" He pushed a paper at Oliver with a scornful fingertip. "You should come here for a relief job when you have already thirty dollars a month?" "I only want a job, I'm not asking for charity." "How many dependents?" "None." "Women with ten children, and no income at all. 

You borrowed on your insurance?" "Not yet." "Paid your rent?" "Yes." "You're disqualified." 

Oliver went out.

He took a bus to get down to Helen's. Shutting the thought of seeing her away, he lit a cigarette and looked down from the height at the swarming life on the streets. 

New York was terribly alive, even now crippled by bad times. Shops burst with people, a solid stream flowed against the traffic lights, the elevated roared, the subway thundered. Even the leashed dogs pulled nervously from post to post as if every sniff might be the last. 

He wanted a dog. A little-girl spaniel, silver buff with onyx eyes and a flannel tongue. A man had a right to own a dog. But that was luxury. All right for tenement women to have ten children but ridiculous for a single man to have a dog.

Suddenly all the traffic halted. Horns blared. A policeman waved an angry arm. A street car ground to a stop. 

A milk truck screeched, missing a stream-lined limousine. Little dirty children ran to the gutter and diddled back and forth, screaming with delight. Oliver craned his neck and saw an old camel padding easily down the center line of stalled cars, a worn, ancient, moth-eaten beast with a fringe around its head and a sweating driver pulling along behind. Indolent, careless, the camel lounged past, with beautifully indifferent eyes inspecting the sky. The soft dark lips rolled, the feet padded, the hump swayed. It came from somewhere, it was going somewhere. The rest was nothing. New York waited for it. Then, when the driver crossed the street, the horns began again, the street car clanged, the children went about their play, and the bus lurched ahead, throwing Oliver violently against the Marlboro ad on the seat ahead.

He gave his name to the elevator man at Helen's apartment, and waited by the impossible artificial trees in the lobby until he heard the steel door swing open and the elevator come to a stop. 

In that moment, he felt again a strange dizziness, and a dark brooding premonition, not identified, but diffused in his blood. His heart beat with painful excitement, his hands came down hard on the gilt table behind him. The elevator boy stepped out, Helen stood there with a child clasping each hand. Her figure repeated itself in the mirror, tall and lovely, with proud lifted head and darker-than-amber hair. The little girls were a blur of pale yellow linen. 

She moved toward him, smiling her shadowy smile,

her wide deep eyes fixed on his, the long even lashes lifted. Ridiculously he leaned against the gilt table, motionless, a faint thunder in his veins, while his voice, far away, spoke for him. "Helen!"

* * ¶ Pigeons circled above their heads. The sanded walk had little fluttering groups of them. Their coral feet made patterns in the dust, their soft bright eyes looked up with impersonal curiosity. Oliver and Helen sat on a bench in the sun, with the pigeons wheeling above them. Elizabeth and Joan, the children, bounded away into the edge of the park. Helen took off her hat. She sat with her head bent a little, the sun on her clasped hands.

Around them, nursemaids and mothers in an endless tired stream, flowed around and over innumerable babies. A welter of balls and roller skates, prams and kiddie kars and scooters swept past them. Women screamed. Their worn harsh voices beat against the traffic noise. A pale boy in a torn shirt hawked The Saturday Evening Post. A pair of Scotties walked past, 38

insolent, whiskery. Three Pekingese with bashed-in noses bore a liveried chauffeur along. An Irish setter with melancholy brooding eyes came delicately down the path, briefly scattered the pigeons, and disappeared. 

Behind them, the trees of Central Park, rooted in dead city earth, made a brave crest of green lifting to the air what leaves survived the choking soot. And the tall buildings glittered in June sunlight.

In all the movement, Helen's presence made a stillness. She sat quietly, remote and lovely as an unrealized poem, Oliver thought, her hat crushed on her lap, her pale green dress smooth over her knees. Oliver, driven, tormented, wondered what word could reach her secret self. "I never expected to see you in New York," she said. "Are you staying here?” "I hardly know. I looked around for something." "Yes, I know that." "Helen, do you remember the day I tipped the canoe over?"

They both laughed, remembering. The paddles floated happily away, were caught in the reeds. They had dragged the canoe in finally. Helen with her hair dripping,

Oliver did not seem to breathe at all. The air grew golden over them, like a golden bubble. Then Helen's mouth trembled into a smile, not a shadowy semblance of a smile, but a swift brightness. "Oliver!" she said, "you are just the same, aren't you?" 

His eyes answered, I love you and his mouth said, "Yes. The same.'

His hand touched her dress. The stuff was soft in his fingers. He was here, in the world, with Helen beside

"Where do all the pigeons come from?" was all

Oliver could say, while his eyes searched hers with desperate question. 

She lit a cigarette. The smoke blew up against her straight decisive nose, half-closed eyes and loosened hair. When she opened her eyes, they were deep enough to be lost in. Clear and disturbing with the long level lashes and indefinite color, they met his confused driven look and took it into their depths. "I remember the seagulls on the water," she said. "You used to stand and stare up at them, waving your arms and shouting." 

He saw again the dark shore and the gulls, grey over the flat water, and Helen's young thin figure with windy skirts and blowing hair. "I envied their flight."

"And they ignored you." "I never went back to the shore again after you left." "Were you very lonely?" "You know I was! How could I forget?" "Did you never find out anything of your family?” "Oh, that," he cupped his hands around a match. "No. I gave up trying. It didn't matter any more. I didn't give a damn whether I was eligible, after you went."

The color came again to her cheeks. "I was lonely too," she said in a low voice. "Well, you didn't have to be lonely long." He was suddenly bitter, and then savagely he added, "I'm glad fifty percent of us made a success of life." 

He could sense her instant withdrawal. "Let's sit in the shade," she said. "Will you call the children?" 

He cursed himself. He shouldn't have betrayed his bitterness. Getting up, he walked to the park and called the children. He had forgotten there were any children. 

Now they entered his consciousness with acute reality. 

Yet not as part of Helen. They came up, two little girls in pale yellow linen, children in that stage where one wondered vaguely how old they were, and then dismissed it. Elizabeth, the taller, was a fair smooth blonde child with a colorless skin and a knowing air. She rolled a hoop before her with jaded superiority. Joan, the shorter, was thin and dark, with Brant's sullen mouth.

She flung herself at Helen like a small storm. "You've been telling secrets!" she cried passionately. "Where is your doll, Joan?" Helen asked quietly. "It's Elizabeth's fault! She made me lose it." "I did not,” Elizabeth spoke in a cold mature tone.

"Joan bashed Nellie's head in with a rock and then buried her, and forgot where she did it." "I didn't! I didn't!" screamed Joan. Her eyes dilated. "Never mind. Go and look again." "You come with me." Joan's lip trembled.

Elizabeth sat down neatly beside Oliver with an air of virtue. "It was a French doll," she said, with satisfaction. "A very expensive one.'

Helen and Joan disappeared. Oliver felt helpless. "What did she bash it's head for?" "You don't know Joan." Elizabeth smoothed her skirt. "Papa will thrash her." "Well-if it is her doll وو "Oh, Papa looks after everything, no matter whose it is. He says carelessness is a sin. He says where would he be now if he hadn't always been careful. We had to leave in the middle of the show, because silly Joan got to crying." 

A man in white trundled past with Good Humor ice cream. "Would you like some ice cream?" asked Oliver desperately. "Oh I don't mind," Elizabeth closed her eyes, bored. "What'll it be for my little lady?" asked the icecream man in the jovial tone reserved for children. 

Elizabeth opened her eyes. "Miss Carlyle to you," she

said. "Chocolate. And a cocoanut one too," she added listlessly.

The man moved on. "Joan will have a tantrum," she said, licking the frosted stick with a neat tongue. "Maybe you better eat them up before she gets back." "Oh, no. I never eat fast. I eat very slowly." 

Oliver pulled at his tie. "I suppose you like school," he ventured feebly. "Yes, indeed. I do very well at school. I take after Papa." 

Oliver wiped his face with his handkerchief. The sun was hot. He could see Helen coming back with Joan, Joan dragging at her mother's hand. The bashed doll hung limp in her hand. When they drew near, Joan's face darkened. "She has two Good Humors!" she said in an ominous tone. “Elizabeth Carlyle has ice cream, but Joan has no ice cream. 

Elizabeth swung her legs. "The cocoanut is delicious," she observed carelessly. 

Joan beat at her mother's dress. "She always gets everything! You give her everything! You like Elizabeth best!" 

Oliver looked away. These little girls weren't exactly like the children in the Golden Age. Their thin leggy selves were terrifying. They were raw humanity, untinctured by any softening influence. And what, in 

God's name, did they have to do with Helen? "I'll get you some ice cream presently," said Helen. 

She wiped Joan's flushed face.

The four of them proceeded along the path, Joan

now lapping up the remains of the cocoanut ice cream bar. Elizabeth carried on the conversation. Oliver was tired. He remembered wearily that he hadn't, as yet, called Amy. He couldn't seem to plan anything. 

At the door of the apartment building, he paused. "I'd better not come in." He hated to see Brant. "Do come up. We'll have cocktails." 

Joan stuck out her tongue at the elevator boy. "Ten, please," said Elizabeth smugly.

As the cage rushed upward, Oliver had a feeling that they were all flung violently from the solid earth toward an unknown void. Here with this woman, desire made tangible, and with the small animal creatures in some way part of her existence, rode Oliver Heron, dreamer, failure. They hung between pavement and sky, then were jarred out to a blank unfriendly corridor of imitation marble.

Now he knew this was unreal. The Carlyle apartment had nothing to do with Helen. Helen was out on the shore with him, dripping, laughing, running over the stones. He recoiled from the drawingroom. It was furnished with bleak magnificence, an unbreathing splendor. In such a place, red velvet ropes should be stretched across the chairs and silk curtains draped over the pictures. The furniture would have been adequate in a museum, the pictures, he thought, were pretty terrible. 

He sat down in a carved renaissance chair with his feet on a prayer rug and his eyes unavoidably engaged by the torso of a prize fighter with muscles and tendons exposed.

Helen left her hat and went down the hall. Elizabeth and Joan bounced away, already fighting again. Helen

had dropped her handkerchief. Oliver picked it up. 

There was more life, he reflected, in that soft little square than in the whole insistent formal room. Very small, exquisite, her name embroidered in the corner.

Helen. It smelled of lavender. It was ungarnished with lace. Beautifully the hem was plain.

Before she could speak, the door was flung open and 

Brant came in. "Ah, I see you didn't wait for me,” he said. His pale full lips smiled. He crossed the room and kissed Helen ostentatiously.

Oliver, taut, was conscious at once of a change in Helen. She said nothing, she smiled faintly, but she stood like a figure rigid in stone. The passive cheek she submitted to her husband was colorless. Nobody, even a less anxious observer than Oliver, could have mistaken her withdrawal. "And what have you and the children been up to today?"

from the tray. He laughed genially. "No," he said, wetting his lips. "No. I have to keep at the grindstone for my wife and children." "Lucky to have work to do," said Oliver shortly.

They sat down. Oliver thought in the curious room, their human figures seemed extraneous. All their little emotions and desires had no place among the massive carved pieces. Her skin shone warm as ivory, her hair had the luster of old copper. Dark in his mind too stood the other Helen, the thin girl in a white sweater. He had loved her then, he loved her now. 

Brant made a slight movement. Oliver turned, drew back from the dark brooding look in his eyes. Helen's hand fell, the spell was broken. He couldn't trust his eyes any more. He got up. "I must go." Oliver reddened. "She's warm-blooded," mused Brant. "So many women have gone all to mind lately. It's all one can do to find a natural woman. They've been spoiled by civilization. Helen came back. Oliver thought she might have been a nun in the close white dress she had put on with sleeves firm to the wrists and collar soft to the chin. But

Oliver came out of his dreams at once. Here was Amy and something definite to be done about her. Helen was in another world, he looked in through a pane of glass, but he wasn't in it. Oliver stared down at the twilight street. Rather nice to have money enough to be fussy about your whiskey.

* * The table was laid for four in the gloomy oak diningroom. Oliver, sitting down beside Amy, and across from

Helen. Amy, of course, played badly. She could never get over her early training as a Heart player. "Why, Oliver, I thought hearts were trumps," she kept saying, "where did I get that idea?”

Oliver managed a steady uninspired game, Helen played a dreamy hand, but Brant, his eyes gleaming, mouth set, played with fierce interest. When he lost, it was always Helen's fault. Doctor Lewisohn came in with his mistress, Gilda Ferris. He remembered Oliver, and spoke to him. He was a small man with a light soft voice, dark twinkling eyes and a child-like smile. He belonged, Amy said, to the most aristocratic of Jewish families, or if he didn't, he should have. Gilda was a made-over blonde, her hair was the color of brandy, her eyes golden brown, deepened by extra lashes, her mouth, naturally wide, was over-laid with a neat cupid bow in the new indelible shade. She hesitated, her eyes smiled. "I'll meet you at the 

Childs on Fifth Avenue-near the Squibb building." "What time?" 

Again she hesitated. "I'll try to get there by one. I 

can't be sure. You might have to wait for me.'

It was after one when he finally detached Amy. Most of the guests had gone, or were trying to go. In a small town, people got up and left at a decent hour, but here it seemed to involve endless discussion about who should go with whom, who should phone for taxis. There were final drinks, and final drinks. And then an aimless drifting to the elevator.

The living-room reeked of stale smoke and alcohol. 

Brant, smooth and genial, a little red around the eyelids, said good night. When he looked back, Oliver saw him standing beside Helen, his hand on her arm. The door would close on the last guest, and the two of them would be together again in the intimacy of married existence. Oliver put his hand to his forehead, and drew it away wet. Such a sickness welled up in his body that he leaned back in the taxi exhausted, while they racketed through the streets. It was a long way to Amy's apart-

ment. Amy, he knew, would have to pay the bill. Every male fibre in him loathed the idea. 

He went in with her. His mouth was desperate, he felt the hard pulse in his wrists. The absolute need of some human being to comfort his loneliness drew him, in spite of himself. He was tired.

And he couldn't lash his mind to the situation. Dimly he was conscious of Amy's silent apartment, of helping her with her wrap, smelling the rich scent of her clothes and hair. It had no meaning. He followed Amy down the long hall, his feet moving in a queer manner of their own, apart from the rest of him. Anything, anything at all, he thought, to numb this ache, to have rest. Because 

Helen is Brant's wife, I've got to find a peace in Amy. 

Nobody else belongs to me at all. I've got to be sane, I've got to forget. Most of life is a compromise with something.

He heard Amy in the bathroom. His feet moved, slow, to her bedroom. Her body would be soft and sweet.

Oblivion. Anything, anything in the world for peace, cried his mind. Helen was undressing, brushing her hair. Her feet, soft in slippers, walked to her bed. 

Brant was in that bed, with his greenish black eyes fixed on her, and his sullen mouth smiling.

Oliver ran. In Amy's bedroom there was a small lamp burning, held by a coquettish shepherdess. The bed was one of those low spineless affairs, just off the floor. A snapshot of himself in tennis flannels was already enthroned on the dressingtable. Oliver sat down on the bed, breathing heavily. He must summon all his resources now, and he must shut out the thought of Helen. 

Madness rose in him, black and thick as lava. Sick

of poverty, sick of loneliness, sick of struggle, Oliver opened and closed his hands. He would gather this peony. With a gesture of finality, but still fully dressed, he wrenched back the satin coverlet. "Amy! Amy! Come here!"

Amy appeared in the doorway, soft and seductive and glowing in a long black lace negligee under which her breasts gleamed rosy and her body shone white as pearl.

Her hair was down, she took a step toward him, the color deep in her cheeks, her eyes with a drowned look. "Here!" said Oliver, in a hard fierce tone. "Listen to me, Amy, I've got to have" he flung the cover violently on the soft rug. 

Under the cover was a small lump. The light struck it, he drew it out. Stared. It was a blue teddy bear, an old frayed bear with a single glass eye and worn paws. 

A small patch of stuffing rubbed through one helpless leg. It was the kind of bear a little girl takes to bed night after night to keep the dark away. Oliver held it, and looked back at Amy. "Oh, I forgot―Jimmy!" Amy flushed.

The bear dangled by a limp paw. "Is he-yours?" asked Oliver in a strained voice. "I-well, you see I-I've had him," said Amy helplessly.

She took him. The beady nose buried in the lace of her gown, she looked at Oliver, confused. "I—I've always had him." 

A teddy bear. "You'll think I'm silly," she said. She still held the bear. 

A little girl. In pigtails. Going to bed afraid of the

dark. That was Amy. Oliver's naked eyes saw that little girl and he groaned, turning away. "I can't do it," he muttered.

He covered his eyes with his hand. "Oliver-what is it?" 

He tried to speak, the words died on his lips. He couldn't violate that little girl. Oh God, said his mind, I can't stick it. All this-he muttered something utterly incoherent, he took her free hand and kissed it in a wild pitiful gesture, then stumbling, he ran from the room, down the corridor, away from Amy, away from all things except the hopeless pity. In the street he half-fell over a drunken man. "But not so drunk as I," thought 

Oliver, rushing away in the darkness. 

No, he couldn't do it. He couldn't betray that teddy bear with one eye. Oh God, he almost might have loved 

Amy! If only he could have loved her! 

The Brooklyn children died of diphtheria and he did not care. A blind beggar rattled a tin cup under his nose, and he did not care. The elevator in his building was out of order again, and he did not care. He climbed the interminable stairs smelling of garlic and fish, stumbled into the arid dark of the apartment and fell on the couch. "God what a cock-eyed world!" said Oliver.

* * ¶ He slept. Fully dressed, with the Herald Tribune crackling under his feet and a small wind creeping over the sooty sill to lick at his face, he slept. 

Curious unrelated visions troubled his sleep, darkness and light, a shadowy pale face, a white church with a square tower that leaned a little, a woman walking in a meadow in a blue dress. A rocking horse with a missing eye. There was a teddy bear in the same plight. And a frieze of toy soldiers marched along the wall in faded blue coats. When the ceiling sloped, they marched down hill.

He could almost see the face of the woman, he almost knew her name. But just as she emerged from the dark of his mind, as always, he awoke. 

The buzzing in his ears resolved itself into the dumb 58

waiter, insistent as a rasp. He went out and leaned down the well. "No trash today!"

The opposite door opened, the thin face of the girl looked across at him. Her wild dark eyes stared at his wild blue eyes, he could hear her breathe. The door swung shut. Oliver lighted the leaky gas stove for his coffee. Animals in a jungle, he said, measuring the coffee. 

Across the way, an apartment house was being remodeled. Six room apartments made over into two and three room apartments, smaller and smaller grew the cages, he thought. The wrecking machines were already at work. Unbearably sharp, the noise drilled his ear drums. 

He endured it while he shaved, then he went out. 

The only dog not on a leash, he prowled aimlessly. He bought a cake of gutta-percha soap from a poor old woman at the corner. His own vulnerability assailed him as he threw the soap in the gutter, He went up to the

Columbia library. The terrible gold figure established so firmly on the steps, stared at him. She never failed to shock him. She was like the Rogers groups in Iowa small town houses, or the boy with grapes. Beyond and to the right, he could see the Thinker, brooding in stained bronze. They were both in the same world, on the same campus, the gaunt naked immortal man and the terrible gilt female with her spread robes and maddening stare. 

But the books inside the library were also in the world. 

Oliver breathed the smell of dry glue and dust, he ran his hands over the solid strength of the encyclopaedia. 

Good reading that. Facts and facts, and none of your dreaming, none of your flights of fancy. The stuff men knew.

He watched the hands of the clock. Suddenly he decided to call Amy. He couldn't explain his rushing off in that mad way, and yet he must say something. 

He went out again to the bookstore, and wrestled with the dial phone, getting Amy's number at last. "Is Mrs. Wallace in?” "No, sir. Mrs. Wallace left this morning for Atlantic City." 

She was gone. And he was sorry. It was another failure on his part, a failure to compromise with some inner force. As he hung up the receiver, he wondered if the teddy bear had gone with her.

Visited with a flare of courage, he went down the street and stopped in every store to ask for a job. The worst of defeat, he thought, is the cumulative effect of failure. It eats into you. He wondered if Phi Beta Kappa keys could be sold as old gold. Not likely.

He passed the little church of Notre Dame, set on a slant corner on Morningside Drive. Again obeying his impulse, he went in. An old man was running a squeaky carpet sweeper over the altar carpet. Small red flares burned intermittently before St. John and Jesus and a number of other saints. The dim sweet smoke rose in the cool air. A woman knelt before St. John with tears streaming down her face. As Oliver tip-toed past, she leaned over and took St. John's robe in her hand. 

Above the altar, in a niche carved from rock, stood the virgin, our Lady of the Rocks. A buoyant cluster of red roses bloomed at her feet, she had a blue robe and a gilt crown, and her cheeks were pink. She looked down on Oliver, her hands folded, bisque-white and slender. 

He sat down, not thinking, not feeling, stilled for a

moment. His apathy was disturbed only by the scrape of the carpet sweeper. The silence and the incense and the wavering red flames made a kind of peace, even for the unbeliever. There was something here, something to hold to, yes, something in that virgin in the dark stone cavern, that he wanted.

It would be good to kneel down with faith and confess to that painted statue, to pour out his love and his hopelessness and be eased. Even to speak Helen's name, over and over and over, that would be a good thing. 

He stole out softly, not to break the peace, and waited for a bus. His heart began to beat painfully. He had not seen her for so many hours. Childs was full, it was one of the better Childs, embellished with oak tables and red checked cloths. It was jammed with shoppers and business men and little girls out with their mammas for the day. Oliver ranged up and down outside for half an hour, being early. 

She came. 

He ran to her. Ah, here she is, this is Helen! How much anguish is atoned for by the sight of her figure coming toward you. Inexplicable rapture had him by the throat. For the moment nothing mattered but that she came. Not that he was poor, jobless, without hope. 

Not that she did not, could not, belong to him. She came. She walked with her lovely light step in the midst of a thousand people, and she walked toward him! "Am I late?" "No, no, you're not late. I was so afraid you might not come!" He seized her hand, he drew her proudly into 

Childs, he got a table in a corner. 

Helen pulled off her gloves. Oliver moused through

the menu. They both made the little settling movements preparatory to dining. Some sort of order was given, snapped up by a scornful waitress with bangs and freckles.

Oliver removed the paper tulips to another table and leaned forward. He trembled with happiness.

She seemed to be wandering in a world of her own.

She was gone. A man idling on the curb moved past 

Oliver and also signalled a taxi. Then he asked Oliver for a match. Their eyes met. "The name is Heron," said Oliver suddenly, "in case 

Mr. Carlyle wants it."

The eyes did not flicker. But the man grinned. "Listen," he said, "I do what I'm paid for and no more. 

I saw the lady come out of Childs and take a taxi. I'll hand in a list of everything she buys and where she gets it. But you, I never see you at all. Hell,” he added, leaping into his taxi, "I been in love myself!"

He was borne away in the wake of the other taxi. 

Oliver, with a blank unseeing face, walked down Fifth 

Avenue. That vigorous street, glittering in the June day clattered and whistled and blew. A window of moonstones roused Oliver briefly. His eyes hungrily picked the moonstone for her hand, a pure soft color rather like the color in her eyes but not as clear, a stone with a drowned radiance. Set in silver, not in gold, and shining on that pale hand. He must think of things, he must shut out what he now knew. Feverishly he strode on, raking the windows with his wild dark gaze.

A sweep of velvet, no, chiffon velvet, arrested him. 

How soft it would be, falling down her long lovely limbs, how bright her hair above the deep smooth blue. 

The penniless Oliver Heron bought the two hundred dollar lounging robe and saw her in it, lovely as night. 

He plunged on. What right had this voluptuous city to tempt him with her wares? He collided heavily with a man, fell back. "Where's the fire?" asked an amused voice. "How do you do?" said Oliver. He recognized the famous Doctor, the Jew with the light voice and the mistress. "Rotten," said the Doctor. "Will you have a drink with me?"

They went into a speakeasy, now respectably serving liquor. "What are you doing, shopping?" "Window shopping," said Oliver bitterly. "It's all I can do. I'm broke.” "I know how that is." The little doctor ordered drinks and leaned back against a red plush seat. "I came up from the East Side myself." "You did?" "Sure." 

They drank. The doctor's twinkling little eyes, soft as a rabbit and sharp as a fox, went over Oliver. "You gentiles take love so hard," he said. "What an odd sickness it is!" "It's one medicine can't cure!"

An orchestra, hidden behind artificial palms began to play the hits of the season.

"Take me where the daisies," the violinist sang in a reedy tenor.

Lewisohn ordered more drinks. Oliver didn't even know what they were. He bit a salt potato chip, and drained his glass. He was warm, and slightly dizzy. "We'll make hay while the sun shines, We'll make love while it rains leered over his shiny fiddle. "I understand you're a teacher." "Was. Not now." "the violinist "You look like one of those calm Englishmen that suffer like hell over things." "Do I?" 

They had another drink. "I'm running away from myself today," confided the doctor. "Can you think of any reason why I shouldn't tell you about it?" "No. Not at the moment." "It's Gilda. We don't get on any more. I want to leave her and I can't. She wants to leave me too. We can't seem to break away." His eyes twinkled. "Now, isn't that a situation? Just as if we were married. The deuce of it is we're not. We haven't that excuse. "Every Saturday night we part for good. And every

Sunday morning I wake up and phone her to come right over! And she, hating it too, mind you, she gets in the subway and comes. Now what do you make of that?" "I don't understand it." "Well, I've got a theory." The Doctor smashed the ice in his glass. "When a man and woman live together long enough, they aren't just two people. No, there's a third identity. And it won't let go." He waved to the waiter.

"Two more, the same." He lit a thin cigar. "No, you can't get away from it," he went on. "Call it habitconvention-I don't know what it is." "Even if you're unhappy?" "Oh, yes. Take my family. Amy's probably told you

I am an English Jew-she likes to think so. I'm not. I was one of ten children in a tenement family. My mother was a real Jewess, head shaved and wore a wig. My father deserted regularly, came home once a year, broke. But she couldn't quit him. That third identity held her. Him too, in a way." He smiled, blowing the smoke up across his narrow cheeks. "Like all Jews, I talk too much," he said with his disarming smile. "Like all gentiles, you listen too well.” "There's not much hope then," said Oliver. "Oh, now and then, rare souls escape," he chuckled. "I almost did, one year. I got me another mistress. God, it was expensive. I couldn't stick it. Not that Gilda doesn't cost me a lot. But-well-it's different."

He spat out an olive pit and spread his hands in a typical Jewish gesture. "But how do you like New York?" he asked. "You're an old friend of Helen Carlyle's, I am told." "Yes." "Well, don't fall in love with her," advised the doctor, looking at the check. "She's a patient of mine. Brant's half mad and jealous as hell and she's going to die in his bed. She has one of those passive accepting naturesstand anything rather than blow up the ship. Just built to take it and take it." "I've got to go," Oliver rose abruptly. "See you again," said the doctor. "I feel better now.

It's a cathartic to tell your troubles to someone who doesn't give a God damn. Thanks."

He turned at the door and spoke again. "You after a job?" "Yes." "Well, I'll call up a few good Jewish friends," said the doctor. "Got a lot of patients with too damn much money. See if I can't land you a teaching job somewhere." 

Oliver thanked him.

They parted. Again he saw his companion drive off in a taxi. "Oh-by the way—what do you teach?" shouted the doctor as he drove away. 

And Oliver, shouting on the curb, called back. "English!"

*6* ¶ Stirred from the paralysis of unemployment, Oliver, with grim courage, went again through the mill, gradually grinding his self-respect. He went the rounds of the agents. He answered advertisements without number. 

Certainly it was not true that a man could rise above lack of a job. Insidiously it undermined personality. He began to doubt his own knowledge, to wonder if he could really do anything if he had it to do. Perhaps, without Helen, he might have sunk in the muddy water of apathy, dragged existence out like a starved tom cat. 

But he couldn't relinquish hope.

He had moments of seeing himself not as a man but as a class, rapidly becoming extinct. Gone out of style, as cab horses had gone. The world moved too fast. Silk gave place to rayon, lastex, which could stretch two ways 71

replaced the simplicity of rubber. Civilization wrapped itself in cellophane.

A feverish eagerness to succeed replaced the smug assurance of pre-depression days. The fabric of men wove tight and hard. The finer sensitized stuff of which Oliver and his like were composed simply didn't stand up. Run under Macy's testing machines, he felt it lacked toughness, would never compete with either the vigorous canvas of the laboring class nor the rigid warp of men like Brant.

Art had become an exhibit of so many miles in radio city. Music advertised herself by marrying Maxwell 

House coffee and Crazy Water Crystals. Literature had lost its fragrance, pared to the core, it pushed from page to page. Instead of Forsyte sagas the postman was now ringing twice.

Progress was a good, an excellent thing. Autos were better than horses. 

But now and then, men looked back. Eugene O'Neill permitted himself the nostalgia of Ah Wilderness. The old passion of queens lived in poetic drama. And women like Amy sat under a beauty mask reading about farm life.

Oliver thought about literary trends, as he prowled the streets after a job. He decided to interview the new head of the firm that handled his inheritance. Maybe he could dispose of everything, and get enough to live on for a year. Really live. He wrote a note stating he would call the next day.

All the while, his hunger grew. Hunger for Helen. 

The hours away from her were flat as a plate. How could he get through a whole life of it? In the park, with the

smug Elizabeth and jealous Joan, his hunger grew, feeding on itself. In drug stores, stealing a few minutes for coffee with her, his hunger grew against the background of syringes and soap and stacked toilet paper. The street radio blared, "I'll go home and get my panties, you go home and get your scanties." Love beat in him as profound as in any Elizabethan garden made sweet by lutes.

Touching her hand over the table at Childs, it grew in him, this wild deathless flower.

She was there, she lived. He could look at her eyes and lips, watch her hands, even in the midst of a crowd at her apartment. "There's going to be a revolution," said the Doctor. "I am thinking of buying a place in the country to hole in." "I'm thinking of a place myself," said Brant. "A summer in the country would be good for the children. And keep Helen out of mischief." He smiled. "The trouble is 

I haven't time to look. I can't get away. I have to work."

The emphasis on the pronoun was habitual. "Get an agent to scout for you," advised Lewisohn. "I didn't know you were planning on the country," said Helen. "Might surprise you one of these days.” "You couldn't surprise me," she said, without emphasis.

Brant flushed. He drew Oliver aside, rather pointedly. "You're no longer tutoring Amy's boy?” "No." "Have plenty of time on your hands?"

Oliver smoked thoughtfully. What was the man getting at?

"I'm job hunting, if that's what you mean," he said bluntly. "I may be able to do something for you. You wouldn't mind leaving New York? There's nothing to hold you here particularly, is there?" "Nothing at all," answered Oliver, with a voice bland as cream. "Drop round to my office in a day or so."

Oliver bit his lips. He was in no position to resent patronage. "Thank you. Shall I telephone for an appointment?” "Yes. I'm not always in. My business keeps me jumping these days." 

I know you're at the top, said Oliver to himself. But 

I won't choke on my idleness. As he gazed with his blue steady eyes at Brant, he had a strange sense of superiority. 

In spite of circumstances. "You're fortunate," he said.

Brant made a deprecating gesture. "I've done fairly well of late," he said. "Of course it's nothing to me. All

I want is to make Helen and the children happy." 

Oliver suppressed his smile. He wished he saw Brant with a little less clarity. وو "Almost any man would —" he checked the words. "Ah," Brant drew a long breath. His pale smiling face suddenly darkened. Oliver could see him prowling round and round the words, trying to make something of them. Because he was so devious, Brant read the world in the same medium. 

He suspected everything and everybody. He was always waiting to be taken in. He was constantly

cheated. Oliver had heard enough of it. The tailor cheated him, the barber slighted his hair-cut. He was like a rider rowelling his horse and injured when it tried to throw him. Now, seeing the brooding, sullen face, Oliver had a terrible moment of pity. What a way to live! Then his heart hardened to stone. What a life for 

Helen, pent in the darkness of such a man! "A summer in the country would be good for Helen,” 

Brant said. "Away from the distractions of the city. 

Don't you agree with me?"

Was he planning to isolate her? And how much did he guess of Oliver's feeling? Involved, against his will, in the tortuous indirections of the man, Oliver composed an answer. "The country is charming in summer." "If I can fix on the right place," Brant tilted his glass and drained it. "Cambridge, Connecticut," said Oliver without volition. "What's that?" "I don't know. I must have read about it somewhere -a long time ago. I haven't myself been to Connecticut. 

You know I'm fresh from the middle West." "Read about it? Where? In a guide book?" "I really can't say. I spoke without thinking," said 

Oliver vaguely. "Perhaps there isn't any such place. It just came to me, suddenly, there is the place to live! 

Curious, isn't it?" "What are you talking about?" Lewisohn came up. "Cambridge, Connecticut." "Yes, it's lovely there. A bit too isolated, if you are
