"The entire state of Connecticut is going to be a suburb of New York some day soon," commented Gilda. "Somebody will make money out of those gravel fields yet."

Money. Places made money too. Having a bit of dark steamy land in a certain spot meant money, no matter what you were or did. He wondered again where he had read or heard of Cambridge, Connecticut. Curious what the mind retained, picked up here and there and stowed away like a crow. "So what are you brooding over now?" asked Gilda. "I'm not brooding," said Oliver, irritated. "I wish you were. I need somebody to brood with. I've gained three pounds and I can't think how to take it off without worry. There's nothing like a little healthy worry to reduce one." "Try eating a little less," advised Brant, "or even drinking a little less. You notice I'm not fat." "People with dispositions like yours never are fat,” retorted Gilda. "What you need is a broken heart," said Hugo. “I knew a woman once lost a lover every six months to keep her weight down." "Now watch what you say," said the doctor, "I'm in a benign mood tonight. Or am I? I delivered a swell baby this afternoon."

"Too bad you couldn't deliver five like the Dionne's." 

Brant grinned. "That wouldn't be legal in this country. As the New 

Yorker says, they'd have to be ploughed under." "We need a code for gynecologists," Hugo said. 

Helen crossed the room. Oliver's eyes followed her. 

She moved so lightly, so steadily, her head lifted. It was a hot night and her arms and throat were bare. 

As always her presence made the other women stale, lifeless, like dancing puppets hung on a string. Her mouth, just quivering into a smile, suddenly filled Oliver with an aching sweetness. If they could be alone, now, at once, in the breathless night, not speaking, not touching, only alone. It seemed a small thing to ask, and a hopeless one.

In a blessed cessation of talk, there was music. He could look at her in silence. Somewhere, sometime, he had seen a pool of lotus. In the sunlight, she was a white waterlily, but tonight her skin had the golden softness of the lotus. He managed to touch her hand, lighting her cigarette. It was cool, as always. Her eyes, above the blowing smoke were mysterious, dark, with dilated pupils. "Helen." "Yes." She drew a long breath, awakening from some dream. "Is he going to send you away from me?" "I don't know." "I can't give you up." "No, Oliver. Not now." "Tomorrow?” "At the same time.”

"If only we could be alone." "Yes." 

He went out, taking his loneliness with him. Smiling, his arm linked in Helen's, Brant said good night.

When he got home, he found a letter from Amy. In it was a check for a hundred dollars. Without reading the letter, he held the check, his eyes bitter, his mouth hard. Money. The presence of it, the lack of it. How it conditioned life. How it checked the imagination, mastered the spirit.

Here was this woman, whom he had humiliated, in turn humiliating him beyond words. 

A hundred dollars. Not that he hadn't, in a measure, earned it. Well, she pays my poverty and not my will, he thought putting the check in his pocket. A month ago, he would have returned it. But now there was Helen. 

The letter, written in violet ink, breathed Amy's expensive scent and unhappy mind. 

Dear Oliver:

It will be at present impossible for the lessons to go on. I am taking the boy abroad. If you should want to reach me, the American Express in Paris. 

If only Amy.

He turned the letter in his hands, terribly shaken with pity. If only—if only 

He found some paper and began an answer. He must save her pride in some way. Saving the pride of a woman one has rejected by means of a note, he discovered

incredibly difficult. He couldn't assume that she had thrown herself at him. It was absurd to ignore it. 

My dear Amy, he wrote. Then he began again. 

Dear Amy, I wanted to see you. Your kindness I cannot speak of. If you find it hard to understand my situation, at least know that I cared too much to burden you with one with nothing to offer. But poor as I am, I give you my deepest thanks. Oliver.

He wiped the moisture from his face. For a moment he leaned back breathing rapidly. His heart beat with a bubbling sound. When it steadied again, he went out with the letter. Poor as it was, it had to do. 

After he posted the letter, he walked slowly back. 

The elevator had given out again and he had to mount slowly and more slowly, favoring his heart. If he had money, if he had real work important to somebody, if he had really any reason to live a long time, he might go to a specialist. The best he could do was to lean on the greasy rail, breathing hard, and look down the stair well. 

As he fumbled with the lock on his door, always out of order, the girl from the next apartment came climbing up the stair, clinging to the rail. Her pale thin face startled him, coming against the dark so silently. As she passed him, he smiled. "Hot tonight," he said. "Yes. Very." 

His door swung open. He hesitated. He might ask her

in and give her a drink, they might have a friendly cigarette and shut the world away briefly. "The elevator will be the death of us yet," he said. 

Her eyes widened. A little ghost of a smile flitted across her face, and vanished. "It seems to run down so many more times than it runs up," she said in a light tired voice.

Then she was turning the key in her own lock, and going in. This was absurd. Next time, he would certainly ask her in. There was no sense in not being neighborly, even in New York. She looked a nice girl with her dark blue dress and neat little hat.

Turning out the light, he sat at the window catching the stir of hot dead air. It seemed to him the conflict in himself had tired him until he couldn't endure it. 

His passion, forever unslaked, must be drying the blood in his veins. The feverish heat of his mind was turning his bone to ash.

For the first time he thought of death, as an end in itself, as a quietness rare and precious. The instinct for existence, any existence, the primal urge which kept the starved bodies alive in the fetid tenements, which sustained the breath of the crippled, diseased wretches trundling on carts down the street with their store of bad pencils, the will to live that beat in him, was it a blind device of nature to keep the world crawling with life? Or was it some superior essence of spirit, that must rise above the material?

He no longer was sure. He only felt that it would be a good thing to be still. To be so still that nothing could stir him more, not even hope.

After a long time, he rose and moved heavily to the

bedroom even more airless than the livingroom. As he bent to take off his shoes a folded bit of paper fell from his pocket. It was a shopping list of Helen's, written on half a Childs menu.

The sight of the fine slanting writing moved him profoundly. He saw her hand moving across the table, he saw the little shadowy veins at the wrist. Shoeshandkerchiefs-ribbon for Eliz.-candles-goblets-she had written sedately. Details from the life apart from him. But at the bottom, absently, her hand had written 

Oliver Heron. 

He wanted to live. For it might well be that death taking grief and pain and struggle in one hand, would also erase the memory of Helen. It was too high a price. 

Not even for peace would he lose remembrance of Helen.

The scornful hat girl allowed her fingertips to receive his hat. There was more scorn among New York maids and clerks and waitresses than sand in a sand dune, he reflected, his eyes crinkling. Part of the terrific competition, no doubt. 

He grinned at the girl. He was with Helen.

They sat at the bar on little red velvet stools drinking their old fashioneds. The place had been a glamorous speakeasy, now it was varnished with respectability. It was like a prostitute become a matron. The bartender had a grave manner. The manager assumed a ministerial

air. The jollity and secrecy had been laid away with the admittance cards. But it was quiet and the dinner still excellent.

I swear I'll not come 30 The bus jolted their bodies. She was so close he could smell the sweet elusive scent of her. Her hand, with its long slender fingers and generous firm palm, lay close to his lean hard one. His hand covered it. She did not stir, he could feel a little breath go through her. 

The utter impossibility of ever loosing his hold on that hand beat in his brain. "Helen. I can't let you go." "Ah- -" it was a breath, whether assent or dissent he couldn't say. 

A woman's hand. The sweetness and strength of

Helen. Was it the hand, the arrangement of bones under faintly golden skin, the length and proportion of the fingers, the subtle turn of the wrist? Or was it some essence informing the flesh? Where was Helen's self? He touched the hand-the living warmth. Blood from the heart under her breast beat in the firm fingertips. But what else was there?

When he dropped her hand and took the worn stair rail in his, the impression of her flesh did not leave him. 

He climbed the twisting stairs and got off the bus, turning to help her out. Her eyes looked down, unfathomable, deep. Was she there, behind the shadowy lashes? "I think you might come up with me-only for a minute."

A moment, another instant snatched from the jaws of time and stuffed in his hungry mouth. He rode up with her. 

He was stifled. No, love didn't stand still. His mouth grim, he waited while she took off her hat and put her hands to her hair, in the way women will. Something solid to stand on, in this morass. Did she care for himat all? A kind of strength was growing in him, a desperate honesty. He didn't want her to be sorry for him. 

He had no right to ask her anything. And yet, far down, a voice was crying in him. He did have a right. 

The right to love. It was utterly false to the manhood within him, to deny it since his new will to live was born of it. I love, I must live. So ran his strangely growing thought. "Helen-tell me what you feel about me!" 

Startled, she turned. Her hands fell from her hair. "If you were free-" he steadied his voice, "would you love me? Not as you did that summer, not with youth and glamour-but as you are now?"

She opened her lips to speak, but no sound came. The silence throbbed in their ears, the dark wings of an unknown brooded over them. 

And then, she swayed toward him. In her lips and eyes, in her throat and breasts, and in the hands that crept toward his, came the answer. The word was her body, swaying toward his. Broken, defeated by the force of her love, and so released into expression, her mouth and the breath of her mouth quivered against him.

But even as his hands went around her, time began again. The elevator stopped outside the door and a hand

fitted a key in the lock. Death and dissolution, decay and dust wrenched them back. "Well, well, how are you, Heron?" said Brant with a smooth smile. "Just come in? Helen, can't you make him feel at home?" his words mocked them, his eyes licked at them like flame. "I'm just going," said Oliver. "Come again! Come in often. Keeps Helen from being lonely. I'm so busy myself," said Brant genially. "Helen, I came to get you. I want you to help me pick out some new curtains for the bedroom." He smiled again, thin and cold as a snake, his smile glided. "When I wake up in the morning, I like to look at something soft and pretty. Don't you agree with me, Heron?"

Oliver leaned against the wall a moment outside, fists clenched, sweat standing on his forehead. 

Fortunately the sight of a single man plunging down the street could cause no comment in New York. 

Another ant, stepped on, perhaps, staggering dizzily in the ant hill, back and forth, without purpose, without meaning. Back and forth, Oliver, up and down. Try the park. Step over the derelicts asleep on old newspapers, avoid the old women selling hairpins. Try Broadway. 

Stagger with the blind, unplacarded. Avoid the lovers, linked shamelessly on bus tops, stop your ears to the street radios, dodge the taxis. Cross the street-what for? 

Cross again. Where are you going? Where are they all going?

Dazed with fatigue, he found himself at his own door after a time. The stuffy smell of gas greeted him. The stove must be leaking again. He threw up a window, fiddled with the lighter. The gas made his head ache.

He rang for the elevator. It creaked up, old and worn, with the old worn elevator-man inside. "The main pipe must be leaking in my kitchen,” said 

Oliver, "send up the superintendent, will you?"

He waited while the man rummaged in the kitchen. "Can't find anything wrong. I'll have a look down cellar. Must come up the dumb waiter."

Oliver sat down and drew a sheet of paper toward him. It mightn't be legal. The druggist at the corner was a notary. Witness and notary-it would have to do.

Oliver Heron. He went to the window and leaned out to clear his head of the persistent gas. A radio greeted him "A few words from Ambrosia-for many years fastidious women have used Ambrosia 

Hell. He shut the window. Fastidious women. Helen was that. But he would never know the intimate little things about her, whether she used Ambrosia

the business. Good God, in a world like this, what were a few years of existence more or less?

He had a little whiskey and when he folded up the paper, he drank it. God's greatest gift to man, he thought drily, wetting his mouth. He went out. He would hurry. 

No risking seeing her again and growing weak.

If he saw her again, he must have her. There wouldn't be any half-measures. The thing was to get out. Neatly, cleanly, and with honor. "I still smell gas," he said with vague annoyance, as he got in the elevator with his paper, and wondering what difference it made, he added, "you better check up on it. I'll have to report it." "Might be the main is bust loose somewheres," agreed the elevator boy, “ah'll git the supe again."

Oliver did his business at the drug store. It would pass. He better not mail it, that looked obvious. It must be casual. An old friend-nobody else to leave it to. Sell for cash. Sold out. Removal sale.

He walked around the block. The stars were out, little tinsel bits above the glitter of man-made lights. He went again into Whelan's drug store for a final cup of coffee. "Lightning before death," he murmured. Fatigue and the gas smell and his emotions merged now, his head was airy as a balloon. He had a feeling it would detach itself and float away easily. "Coffee," he said, perching on a counter stool. "Anything else, sir?" the clerk slopped the cup on the marble. "Not now," answered Oliver, with a grim smile.

No, there wasn't anything else. Not now. He stirred in the sugar.

The drug clerk had red hands and eyes set close together. What a lucky devil he was! He had work, regular work. He was secure, he could live. Well, it was good to live, for those who could afford it. Oliver stared dimly at the flamboyant Coca Cola girl pasted on the glass behind the counter. Times had changed. Once he had felt sorry for the men ground down to a routine like the drug clerk's. Now he envied them. They and their like had no future, they had a present. Security. They could cash their little checks, love their women, and raise young drug clerks with close-set eyes and red hands. An endless procession.

He went out and walked home. Light as a balloon, his head floated above his slow body. He would put the will in the desk and open the windows, get out his pajamas, and leave for the last time. Tell the elevator boy he wanted some aspirin. Not say goodnight, just be casual. 

It was an accident. Man run down shortly before dawn last night, name Oliver Heron, unemployed. Addressand so forth. Very brief. Very small. Another ant.

He came up to the building. Rang and rang, then climbed the stairs. Soot under his feet, dry hot dead air in his lungs. Dizzy, he ran on. His heart bubbled. He ignored it. It didn't need to last. Just a little change in the type. Man died of heart failure at-name Oliver Heron, unemployed teacher. At the top of the stairs he staggered, dizzy. Then he went on.

The superintendent stood in the hall with a wrench in his hand. "It's too late," he said. "She's gone." 

The door next his was open. "Give me a lift, will you?"

The elevator man, his face grey as dust, was running up the windows in the apartment. Gas, heavy and thick, poured over them. Oliver staggered after the superintendent.

Over Oliver the dark tide rolled, thick in his veins, paralyzing his mind. Blood beat in his eyes, he dimly felt the muscles knotted in his body. He never knew whether he uttered a sound, he seemed to hear a lost wild cry. He endured, he breathed, no more. "I had to see you," said Helen, like a child. 

When he touched her, she shivered, awaking from some frightful dream. On his knees, Oliver put his arms around that quivering body. Hate and agony, love and despair and a tenderness sharper than any sword drove through him.

So clinging, motionless, two drowned bodies flung against rocks that gave them again to the water, yet they clung.

When the sickness ebbed in him a little, Oliver laid her cold hands between his, rubbing a transitory warmth into them. There were, as yet, no words at all. After a time, she stirred, turning her palms up to his. 

Then he spoke. "Helen." And again, "Helen!" "Oliver!"

Let it be, Oliver. Don't go down the dark and devious way. Her eyes implored him. Leave me just a little, stripped as I am, said her eyes. Leave me the heavy secrets of this loveless marriage, because you love me. There are some things love itself can never reveal.

Whatever he did, he would never force her. She had enough of that. Without volition, never. "Promise me you'll wait a little," she said softly. "I really shouldn't have come like this. But I had such a sudden fear! Almost as if you were dead."

He looked at her queerly. He would keep his reserves also. That moment of ecstasy, of meeting spirit yesterday in the dark hall, was it gone forever? This hour of shared suffering, was it gone now? He set his mouth in a hard line. "All right," he said grimly. "As you wish, Helen." "You see there are the children," she said. 

Ah, yes, there they were, the little girls. You couldn't fight children. They destroyed your freedom, shackled you, they stood forever between you, and you couldn't fight children.

Helen wouldn't leave her children. He was only a man who loved her. He hadn't a single weapon. Even his love wasn't a weapon. It only made him vulnerable. "I am going to see what I can do," said Helen. "Oliver, I'll call you-in three days." 

She was gone. 

Wait, she had said. Wait and hold on. Was that the sum of wisdom? He had waited, he had held on. What for? To go on waiting and holding on. Inaction that sapped the fibre, that was the lesson of the period. 

He dressed and took a taxi to Brant's office. This was bigger than any civilized reason, his love had been violated and he was a man. Without any defense and with no words, he would batter Brant Carlyle to a pulp. Impetuous, burning, he ran down the marble corridor, found Brant's number and mounted in the elevator.

"I want Mr. Brant Carlyle, please." He threw down his hat and went past the office girl.

No cringing on benches this time, no giving his name. 

Past some kind of secretary strode Oliver, his eyes, now cold and deadly fixed on the farthest office, which must be, and was, Brant's. If you leave your وو Oliver brushed him aside and went out. Reaction set in. He walked to the subway, rode home. What good would it have done to smash Brant's bones? Would Helen have been happy? He doubted it. Would it improve their situation? Hardly. It would satisfy his ego. To hell with

his ego. It was his fundamental business now to keep his head.

There was a message from the Doctor when he got home. He was to come round for dinner-conversation, and a possible lead.

He had a sandwich and some coffee. He must keep his head, and wait. The word ground in his mind. Possibly, in time, waiting would be an end in itself. Thousands of men were waiting. Sitting on porches, on park benches, in stations, behind empty desks, waiting with fixed hopeless smiles. Something would turn up, this year, next year-never.

He went to the bank and asked if his check had cleared. The doorman, elegant in white gloves, supercilious in bearing, followed him to the window. There were fresh flowers on the desks, sheaves of engraved paper, spotless pens, and the customary poster with a ruddy young man pointing a finger at the words, "Have you made YOUR will?”

The doorman stood just at his elbow, as if Oliver might be a hold-up man. 

He noted, waiting at the window, that Christmas club payment number twenty six was due. The world moved along.

The cashier gave him his money, eyeing his suspiciously. Oliver counted it with equal suspicion. The doorman waved him out, his neat pistol twinkling in the fine holster. 

I don't trust you, a whit more than you trust me, said 

Oliver putting his money away. Upon this rock national prosperity had scattered its bleached bones. Shouldn't

wonder if it took a half century for people to rebuild their faith, Oliver mused over his cigarette. After all, the mere money wasn't the thing. Even he, down and out, knew it. It wasn't the closed banks, the actual destruction of property, the life savings swept away. No, it was deep and profound in the consciousness of the nation. The group faith, childish belief in the integrity of the banker-ah, that stock wouldn't pay dividends again in his day. No, it was a new era. Faith was gone. 

That doorman, that sleek cashier, the splendid veined marble columns, they represented the past along with cab horses and Oliver. Money itself, the actual symbol of confidence, of worth, was counterfeit five times out of ten. You couldn't even be casual about a dollar bill. 

Well, what should they believe in? Oliver, smiling at himself a little, went back to Keats, "the truth of the imagination and the holiness of the heart's affections.” 

He believed in his love for Helen. And he believed, tentatively, in something not quite trapped in the web of civilization. He didn't as yet know what it was. 

Punctually at seven, he rang the Doctor's bell. The bronze plate shone with fine luster, Doctor David Lewisohn, M.D. The maid took his hat. He was early, the Doctor sat at a grand piano in the livingroom, playing softly to himself, something old and mellow and infinitely sorrowful. "Come in," he said without getting up. "Be finished shortly," and he added with his charming smile, “doing my practising."

The room was long and low and comfortable, furnished without affectation. Oliver stretched his long legs on the davenport, and blew a feather of smoke in

the dusky air. The music made a mournful sweetness. It was like a golden bell rung over some shadowy ruined shrine. "Do you like it?" "Yes," said Oliver. "You play, too?" "The trouble with my surgery," the Doctor brought out a tray from a cabinet, "it interferes so with my music." He mixed cocktails, the ice clinked, a fine frosty rim rose on the glasses. "I was going to play in a concert," he said plaintively, "and some woman had an abortion on me." 

His fine dark eyes twinkled. "How many pounds have you dropped on account of this woman you can't have?" "How do you know it's a woman?" "My God, I know men and women," said the Doctor, "men like you-calm Nordic men don't brood. They get a weathered look. Have another?" "Thanks." "The thing is to get just hazy-nothing like it to simplify life. Have you ever tried writing?" "I've taught it, if that's what you mean." "No. It isn't. I mean doing fiction. It's good hard labor, and might lead to something. At any rate, I can send you to a friend of mine who's an editor. That's just one thing I thought of." "I wouldn't want to impose” "Nonsense. Nobody gets anything anywhere in New 

York without pull. You know somebody who knows somebody else who knows somebody else that's the system. Have a drink." "Well, I'll try anything," grinned Oliver, "of course

I've got stuff around. But I didn't expect to stick my neck out trying to sell it." "That's the gentile. With a Jew the first thought is, what's it worth in money?" "Are we really so different?" "Yes. And no. Take this business of being in love.

Emotion freezes up, feeds on itself in the typical gentile. 

With the Jew it simply shoots the works. You should fling your arms about and cry, my friend, and have sullen fits, and importune the stars. It helps." 

Oliver laughed. "Yes, even to laugh. Have a drink." The doctor rang a bell by the fireplace. "We're dining here. I got me a little slut from Harlem, but she does squab like a dream.” "I appreciate your asking me." "Yes and in your stiff way you wonder if it's charity. 

It's not. I get bored with New Yorkers. I've learned them by heart. You rest me.” 

They drank, and ate, and smoked. Politics— "You wouldn't mind a political job?” "No." "Well, if the fiction world won't have you, we'll try 

Tammany. I operated on the wife of the big shot, the power that turns the dynamo. You might be a secretary to some secretary-at three thousand a year." "I thought New York was cleaned up under the new administration." "You don't know New York. I voted for the new guy myself, secretly. But I'm fed up. The new graft is just a little worse than the old. I like organization even in robbery. They take less, by and large, and give you something for it. No," said the Doctor, "these little

squirts, Mussolini, Hitler-whole kit and boodle of them work so damn hard to make you notice them that they're a real menace.' دو "Is Hitler little, too?" "Well, you know my point." The Doctor lit a cigarette. "Back to Napoleon."

Oliver took his liqueur. He was shutting Helen behind a screen, digging a conversational trench between him and the forest fire. Where was she? Where was Brant? 

What hope was there? Helen, Helen, cried his mind. "I'm expecting a few people in shortly," said the Doctor. "One always is. Either you go out or somebody comes in. New Yorkers are so afraid of being alone. 

They don't dare. D'you play contract?" 

Oliver made a wry face. "Under compulsion." "I've got some fancy new gambling equipment. 

Would you like roulette?" ee ee Anything." "Agreeable cuss. Nothing matters, does it?" 

Oliver nodded. "Only one thing, right now." "Men and women," the Doctor lit a long thin cigar, discarding his cigarette. The phone rang. He answered, holding the cigar between his lips. "Bleeding?" his voice changed, dry and clipped. "I'll be along later. Call me back in an hour. At the house." وو 

He came back, sighed. "There you are. These women!" 

He brooded over it. "That girl's an opera singer. Just coming to the top. One of those fire and blood women. 

What does she do? Goes off and gets pregnant with her manager who has two wives already. Tries an abortion. 

I save her. "I tell her a few of the facts of life not out of Rigo-

The maid carried the dinner table away, folded it up. "Well, you can entertain my guests," said the Doctor, "while I go down and hold her hand and tell her she's O.K." 

Oliver said nothing. 

The Doctor sat down. "I'll be out all night," he said. "You can sleep here, if you want to. Then we'll have breakfast together and I'll run you around to that agent and introduce you personally." "But-why?" "God damn it, because I like you," answered the 

Doctor, "and because she loves you!"

There was a little silence. So he too "Well, there it is," said Oliver. "I've never known anybody like her," said the doctor. "So cool and remote-well, let's have a drink. Now that 

I'm drunk, I don't mind telling you I tried to make her myself. It's no go. But I'll do anything in God's world to help her get away from our mutual friend, Brant Carlyle." 

Now he understood. A thin smile tightened Oliver's lips. "Women," said the doctor. He drank a straight drink of whiskey, poured another and drank that. He wiped his lips. "She's a good sport," he said suddenly. "I operated on her once. I'll not forget it. God, what a nerve! Well,

she was terribly set on going out. If you can make her want to live, I'll back you." "She wants to live." Oliver saw his hand, steady with the match. 

The doctor clapped on his hat. "Guess I'll go along," he said. "There might still be something. I can't make women out!" He waved toward the chromium bar. "Be a host," he said. "Who's coming? It hardly matters. All they want is to drink enough to forget themselves and their troubles. The Bergs always come first so they can drink most. Now there's something." He sat down, his hat cocked on his head, his cigar making a long ash. "She's another thing. In love with a girl friend. Gets analyzed out of it and married a man. Supports him, endures his beastly temper. He won't work, won't have children. She might be in Italy now with the girl friend sitting at a cafe drinking coffee and watching the world go by. Or dipping her feet in the Mediterranean. Secure and free and independent. Instead she's grubbing in an office at twenty dollars a week supporting a big hulk who abuses her. All very beautiful and moral because he's a male. A victory for analysis and morality, what? How do you like life, my Nordic friend?" "So little that I tried to check out this week," answered Oliver, "but something changed my mind. 

There's something 

"Yes. There is." The doctor opened the door. "That's why I'm going off. If Gilda calls, tell her I'm out with another woman! See you later!"

The door swung shut behind his brisk figure. 

Oliver went to the window and stared at the lights below. Helen-Helen-Helen-Helen —

He moved away, found the bathroom, decided there was time for a shower since he was already hazy with drink. The cold stinging water whipped him back to reality. His haggard face with the blonde hair dripping eyed him grimly in the glass. The new hard line of jaw made him look strange to himself. He tied his tie. Trim, steadied, he saw himself, and wondered who he was, what he was going toward.

* * ¶ pierre dubois, fashionably uncapitalized, agreeably French, profitably in the literary business, received Oliver. He was lacquer-smooth, his smile was brilliant, he radiated success.

Oliver, still waiting, had become thinner and harder in the interminable week. Desperate, he had walked the streets in front of Helen's, learning at last that she had gone to the shore with Brant for a few days. Well, what did it mean? Who could say? A patching up, or a breaking away. Removed from him, what was Helen doing and feeling? How reaching Brant? Or was she, at this moment of crisis, only withdrawing into herself? 

Perhaps she didn't love him. But she didn't love Brant. 

What of the children? Ah, that was another thing. "And while they have a certain literary ability," dubois was saying smoothly. 109

Oliver jerked back to the present. Just like him to go off at an important moment. "Yes?" he said. "I doubt whether they're salable. Why not write something more in line with the popular demand?" Oliver's eyes were mirthful. "Not what you call adventure," he said. "I just missed suicide and chance prevented me from murder, but no-not adventure. 

Good day." 

Optimism. That was the thing. Light love. A pretty

girl with a fast line, an irate wealthy father, and a young football hero. Smart sophisticated dialogue and a neat final clinch. He knew the formula. But he had a horrid feeling that you had to believe in it to make it sound right. You had to have a forget-me-not mind and a heliotrope style. His would be bitter almonds. 

Now for Tammany. From one of these blind alleys something might yet creep toward him. Well versed now in procedure, he bought a paper and a magazine. He looked briefly at Esquire, the magazine with guts. Guts came high. Fifty cents. He satisfied himself with Redbook at half the price. He must indeed skim some of the stories, notably those by smart women writers. 

Women had the knack for this honey-coated romance. 

God, to have money! He strode down the street, a thin loosely knit figure in a neat if uninspired blue suit, one among many, and no one to see the burden of love and longing behind those level eyes or the passion in leash behind the taut lips.

The shrine of Tammany was a law office. As a shrine should be, it was private, hidden. A blank door opened on a lobby done heavily in red leather and presided over by a bland bust of Jefferson. Three doors opened from this lobby, behind these inner and more secret doors gave forth at intervals men who rabbited from office to office carrying papers.

Oliver gave his name to a secretary and showed his letter from the doctor. The man he asked to see did not have his name on the door, had no telephone, was as inaccessible as a priest should be. 

Oliver sat down in a red leather chair and pulled an ash tray toward him. He composed himself for a long

wait, the lobby was packed with men. Every five minutes the elevator disgorged more men. 

The stench of rank tobacco and sweaty bodies and extravagant perfume went up as incense before the altar. 

There were men of all ages and conditions. Ruddy 

Irishmen full of bone with small bright blue eyes and big hands. Dark Italians with skin the color of green peaches, hair carefully valsparred and long gleaming fingernails. Gentlemen crooks with gardenias in their buttonholes and common-garden crooks with black blunt hands and bulldog jaws.

Two men, near Oliver, kept their hands moving all the time. "And when he asks you, you say, you were promised the contract." "Hell, I will, and then what?" "By God, he's got to give it to you." "You can wait inside," said the secretary, sidling up to Oliver. He spoke in a professional whisper. Instantly he was beset by a buzz of voices. "Where is he? When can I see him? Say, listen, he said " 

Smooth as a canoe, the secretary paddled Oliver past them.

The second sanctum was very quiet. The big shots, the personal friends sat here, drinking straight whiskey, wetting their lips. The air was like a gun barrel, smooth and silent.

Next to Oliver sat a killer, his eyes bright, his hands at home in his pocket. A man farther on, trembling slightly, was speaking to him in a low voice. "Don't worry, grandpa, it isn't your turn to take

the rap," laughed the gunman. "Take it easy." He spoke with a pleasant cool drawl.

Suddenly there fell a silence again. A man had come to the door, a quiet man with blue-grey eyes and a smile. "Hello boys," said the soft silken voice.

The first man, the killer, went to him. He took orders and slid out, grinning. Two more men crowded up, a third edged nearer, the whole group, breaking up, surged toward the high priest. 

The quiet man made a gesture. He smiled. "Jesus 

Christ," said the soft voice, "you'd think we won this election! And my God what a beating we got!" He bent his ear to the first man. The eyes lost the blue, became steel. "I never promise anything," he said softly. "If anybody promised you anything he was a God damned liar. Get out!"

A phone rang. Still talking, the man lifted the receiver. "All I'm asking is five minutes of your time, Judge. 

Christ, no. This is a lady. Give her five minutes!"

He walked quietly to Oliver. "And what can I do for you?" said the soft silken voice. 

The blue grey eyes surveyed him. Oliver, looking at power, raw, tangible power, smiled. "I just want to live," he said. 99 The quiet man ran over the letter. "That bitch is costing a lot," he said gently, "one way and another. Jesus 

Christ, the education racket is hard. You want to teach? 

How long you been in New York? Well, I'll see.' 

He scribbled a name on a pad. "I'll do what I can. I can't promise. I may get a shot in the back any day." He smiled. "Come back next month if you haven't landed anything," he said gently. "If I'm still alive." 

At the door, Oliver looked back. "Jesus Christ," said the quiet man, "I don't promise anything!"

Oliver went out past the bland Jefferson, into the warm air of the street. A man brushed against him, hurried past. A second man moved after him, silent and sure. The first man bounded into a taxi, the second stood at the curb, his hand in his pocket. There was a sound like the backfire of the taxi motor, the man inside gave a strangled scream, the taxi driver shouted, the ants, momentarily disturbed in their business swarmed up on every side. The killer made quick work of his assignment. He vanished.

Ten stories up, his boss was pulling the strings to get work for a scholar, influencing the course of education.

Oliver went to Helen's, and was admitted by the maid. 

The Carlyles might be back tomorrow. The children were in, would he like to see them?

Acting on an inexplicable impulse, he offered to take them to the park. The maid was obviously relieved. "I won't wear my hat," said Joan, asserting herself at once. "Well, I shall wear mine," Elizabeth said smugly. 

Hatted and unhatted, they went out. "What have you been doing to amuse yourself?" asked Oliver. "I am reading a life of Queen Victoria," Elizabeth answered. "Joan's been reading Little Man, What Now." "Whatever for?" "Because Mamma's away and Mamma told her not to," 

Elizabeth explained. 

Oliver changed the subject. "Do you play games?" "One can't play games with Joan. She always has to win." "I do not! I do not!" "Don't scream, Joan." "I'm not screaming!" "If she can't win any other way, she cheats," said 

Elizabeth. And added with dry wisdom. "Like Papa." "Would you like a soda?" "A chocolate frappé."

They went into a convenient Loft's and after a brief argument about whose turn it was to order first, they settled down over their eating. They looked, then, like two little girls absorbed happily. Oliver relaxed. He just wasn't used to children, that was it.

"Are you planning to pay for the sodas?" asked Joan suddenly. "Of course. Why not?" "Papa says you haven't got a nickel.” 

Oliver flushed. "He says he's going to get you a job," pursued Joan, thoughtful over her chocolate syrup. "Oh, is he? Why should he?" 

Elizabeth laughed. "So you can't see Mamma." "I see." "They went away so we couldn't hear what they were talking about," Elizabeth's eyes were amused. "We can always hear. Have you ever tried listening by a radiator pipe?" "No. I wouldn't listen to what I wasn't wanted to hear." "How else would you find out anything?" Her eyes were round. 

He gave it up. "I could eat another frappé," stated Elizabeth, "if you are sure you can pay for it.' 

Oliver laid the money on the glassy table top. "Would you like to go to the country?" "No. It would be dull." "Something's going to happen," said Joan lifting her shut, secret face. "We're either going to the country or away to school or to Europe. Papa says whatever Mamma decides." "He always says that. Then she has to decide what he wants. 

"I'm afraid I'll have to take you home." Oliver rose hastily.

They walked back to the apartment. "Would you like some balloons?" 

Both little girls laughed. "Thank you, no.” And Joan said, "We got over childish things like that, long ago."

But when they approached the balloon man, they hesitated. 

They consulted each other with their eyes. "Papa isn't home," murmured Joan.

Sedately they left him, Elizabeth stiffly holding her flame balloon, Joan squeezing a green one so close to her thin chest that it burst. Elizabeth came back. "Thank you, Oliver," she said. He saw, for the first time, a look of Helen in her grave eyes. 

Oliver sat on a bench, smoking. Elizabeth and Joan.

A double bulwark against his love. Helen, where are you? In the very marrow of my bones, I desire you.

This is an ancient thing, and there is no place in today's world for it.

Aphrodite rises no more through the foam with breasts of pearl and heavy-lidded eyes, Daphne glimmers no longer in the reeds, and Bacchus is translated to three parts dry gin and a shot of seltzer. But my love has nothing to do with time.

He went home and wrote a light love story, his tongue between his teeth. At midnight he mailed the manuscript and went to bed. He slept. 

There was Helen, come from a violet sea, her body cool as pearl. Helen or Aphrodite? The beach stretched white as jade, deep and mysterious lay the green forests behind. Within those secret depths lovely in the moon, was the secret place. A flight of worn marble steps led to the altar of a god whose face he could not see. Be-

fore that shrine he must make a living sacrifice. The moon climbed, the scent of lotus blossomed on the breast of night. And the shadow of Helen fell across him.

And the bell rang to announce the laundry man and his clean shirts.

And yet you ask me to wait to wait this clock is ticking life away, minute by minute. Here we are, now-tomorrow we are gone."

I saw you. And the need for happiness went like a sword in me. I was dead. Now I'm alive-I see you, I hear your voice, I touch you, now and then-and the rest of the time I'm in torment. "No. No. I pity us both!" 99 "You are mine. And not mine. I have no part in you. 

Nothing of yours is mine. There's no small place in all the world that is ours. I couldn't even have this hour alone with you without getting a hotel room.' "We have this hour. Nothing can take it away." "Forgive me, darling. I only make it difficult for you." "After all, we are not children, Oliver.” "Why do you tremble, Helen? Are you afraid?” "Ah-no-not any longer." "This much is mine!" 

This much is yours, then, to drink an elixir after which thirst may never be quenched, to behold the heart of fire after which all lesser fires are ash, to die into life.

To reach, at last, the peace of passion, the release of flesh and bone and the death of time. To know. Body and spirit fused in a complete one, saying to that other body and spirit in one, "I know you."

Magnificently released from the bonds of flesh by flesh. There is no past, there is no tomorrow, and there is no fear. Now, in this consummation breathes immortality.

And so, slowly and painfully, climb back to circumstance, leave godhead, open the dazzled eyes on dull daylight sifting through a torn shade. 

Wrench your arms away from her, Oliver. Turn from the softness of her breasts and the ineffable sweetness of her closed lids. Ah, yes, now learn what it is to tear yourself from her, because you must leave her.

As the ecstasy, so the anguish.

Unendurable, yet endured. 

Too late, now, the cup has been at your mouth, and not while the breath labors in your lungs shall you forget this wine. Move ahead, Oliver Heron, to whatever destiny awaits with whatever of courage you possess. 

And know, in the midst of the suffering, that you would not, if you could, draw the sword from your heart.

PART TWO

* * ¶ Oliver got off the train at Danbury. It had a familiar look; perhaps because it resembled a middle western town. He stood a moment like a swimmer at the edge of an unknown lake, his typewriter and worn suitcase at his feet. It was Saturday afternoon, a bad time to come. The station was crowded with hurrying men. A line of cars jammed the curb, commuters' wives and children and dogs. There was an air of week-end excitement, the crackling of parcels, squeals from the children, frantic barking of the dogs, as the husbands were reclaimed from the city.

It made Oliver feel a special nostalgia. For an instant he saw Helen beckoning from a small car, and a golden little girl spaniel making hello noises. His face, already thin, hardened, as he picked up his typewriter. No good to dream. 127

He was tired. A few hours of rapture with Helen interspersed with long periods of utter starvation, had worn him out. He was like a clock, he thought wearily, the pendulum of emotion swinging from bliss to despair had finally exhausted him. Then, one morning Helen had telephoned to say she could not meet him for lunch. 

Joan had measles. Doctor Lewisohn had just left, he was coming round to see Oliver on business. 

The little Doctor arrived, professional with his bag. "Heard anything yet from Tammany? How's the literary racket? Fix me some coffee, I've been up all night delivering a baby. Helen's all right. That Joan! 

Her father all over. Too bad. Elizabeth-I don't know. God, you look terrible. I can't stand the heat, Oliver. Put some whiskey in that coffee, half a cup. 

What kind of lease have you here? Lousy little place.” "I may sell a story," said Oliver, answering part of the questions. "God, I'm fed up. Listen, Oliver, I've got to get away from women and work. Helen's going to be hanging over her offspring for a week-well, too, Elizabeth will catch it, if just for spite. How would you like to take on a job for me?" "Doing what?" "Scouting for a place in Connecticut. Run down a few and phone me. I want an old farm, you know—oldfashioned floor boards and old-fashioned cracks. Come from the tenements. Like to get hold of a real old place -off somewhere, I can't look myself, I've got to do a bunch of operations this week. How does it strike you?"

Oliver poured in the whiskey. "I can see you wearing a crown in heaven.” 

The Doctor waved him away. "I'm selfish. I wouldn't give a cent to any charity. But Helen-I asked her. 

She liked the idea. Listen, you damned Nordic, take this check and cash it and get up to Connecticut and find me a place. If I buy it, I'll give you a chance to live there until I can come out. You know-mend the leaks and fix the roofing and see if the water poisons you." "Do you care where it is?" "Well," the Doctor grinned, wiping his mouth, "something isolated so my particular burden can't follow me. I'm getting old, I can't operate all day and be good in bed all night." "I'll go to Cambridge," said Oliver. "My family came from there, I think. I'll look and see. I don't need all this money." "Well, go to a good hotel, if there are any."

The Doctor turned at the door. "I can't make it out," he said. He picked up his bag, shifted it to the other hand. "I never expected, at my time of life to be a fairy godmother," he complained. "Well, get thee to the country, Oliver, you need it." 

Oliver had stared after him. What did he know? 

What was in that clever strange head? What had Helen told him? These men that spoke obliquely, scoffing-one couldn't tell.

But he had come. When the flurry of husbands and wives had subsided, he asked the station agent about

the bus. Then with a glass of beer and a sandwich inside him, he rode jerkily on into Connecticut. The hills and valleys, dim with the gold of evening, fell away before him. The sky was a quiet arc, soft as the hollow of 

Helen's throat. And the air had a haunting quality, a drifting sweetness, vaguely familiar.

The world was unbelievably green and still after the stone and steel brilliance of New York. The bus stopped to let a file of cows pass. They smelled of sweet grass and warm milk and their limpid indifferent eyes regarded the intruder softly. At a road stand, the driver got out for gas. A tinny victrola was playing, Let Me Call You

Sweetheart. The smell of roasting frankfurters and hot coffee filled the air. 

Oliver got out in a nameless little town, dreamy in the dusk. An old white tavern received him. He crossed the lawn, feeling the dew on his feet, and took a room for the night. He was near Cambridge. He would walk around and make inquiries in the morning. 

In an old four poster, he stretched his tired body. No sound at all came through the open window, only the smell of fresh hay, rich and warm. The ruffled curtains stirred. A moth with soft blundering body struck blindly at the light. Oliver put it out and lay listening to the silence.

* * ¶ He woke in the morning, opened his eyes vaguely on a demure girl in needlepoint who advised him with urgent finger that, THE FEAR OF GOD IS THE GREATEST TREASURE OF THE HEART. She was neatly pantaletted and bonneted, and a wreath of roses and forget-me-nots hung over her head. Oliver rolled over. The fear of God is the greatest treasure of the heart. Fear.

He had, he reflected, fear of everything but God. Fear of losing Helen, fear of never having her, a thousand fears blossoming daily in his fevered mind. Poverty, disaster, and deep down, the fear of himself. He, Oliver, did he have enough courage left to get through? A kind of fatal expectancy had wakened with him this morning, but of what, he had no idea. 

The sounds of day came to him as he shaved. A thou- 131

sand little country birds sharpened their voices outside, a cow mooed, farther away a hen cackled with the perpetual surprise of having laid an egg. Then a bell, sweet and solemn, made the air rich and died upon it. It was Sunday.

Oliver telephoned the real estate agent. She would show him the places after dinner. Church, she said thinly, would be over at twelve. She was sure she had just what he was looking for. Had he a car? Then she would get one and call for him at one. Or better, he could come to her house.

He went out. The August sun laid a fine glaze of gold over the hills, but the little town was cool with shade. 

Tranquil lawns stretched back to houses composed and gracious, white with green blinds and ruffled curtains. 

Instead of street numbers, the houses wore their dates, 1645, 1732, 1729. Oliver remembered with a smile, the houses in Glendale, Iowa. Modern, cut to patterns, decorated with fancy railings, or better, violent with bright stucco. The oldest of them had towers and cupolas, like girlish old ladies with false curls. These houses, here they stood, here they would stand, unchanged. The little school house, sturdy red brick, was the same, to a window, as it had been in 1738.

They were real, these houses, they endured, they were not afraid of being behind the times. They had a quality of timelessness-like Helen. Tranquil and fine, they opened their wide doors on yesterday and tomorrow.

Smoking his cigarette, he moved down the street, stopped at the old town hall. The door stood open, there was a bronze plate on it inviting the public to visit the museum. The caretaker, old and solid, advised him the

museum was closed Sunday morning-but he might look in since he was a stranger. The Indian relics were on the second floor. The D. A. R. room was downstairs but the Green Mountain chapter was redecorating it. 

Oliver climbed past the line of portraits. Solemn bearded men, their faces a little cracked by the years, the blue uniforms and the gold braid a little faded, but the mouths and eyes indomitable. As he stopped at the landing to quiet his heart, he looked at the face nearest him, hung aslant beside the window. What a tall haggard young man leaning on his painted rifle! Stirred strangely, Oliver gazed at the blue defiant eyes, and the thin straight mouth. The artist, busy happily with the medals and fringes and the tasseled background, had inadvertently caught the expression, proud and wild and desperately resolved. The name 'plate was gone, Oliver stared at the strange young man. If he had time, he would find out who he was, what story lay sealed under the bright breast of the uniform.

His mind divided in layers of thought, he went on. 

Always the deep part of him was filled with Helen. His need, her body, her little trick of bending her head and looking up, half-smiling. But the top of his mind, like the crest of a wave, folded over and over, turning what he saw into his consciousness. In some curious way, it was because he loved her so, that the very shadow on the scarred floor was distinct.

Here was another world. A doll with pink cheeks and gentian eyes, stiff and prim in dimity. "This doll belonged to Selena Hatch. It was used to carry news across the line during the Civil War." Portrait of a young girl in a bullet-riddled case, found on an unidentified soldier

after the battle of Manassas. An old door, hacked by tomahawks. From the bloody massacre. 

The scream of musket balls, the galloping feet of horses, the scream of the dying, the bright blood poured, all gone now. Leaving behind a doll, musty leather, rotted silks, a broken door. He bent over a bronze tablet. 

Sacred to the memory of Reverend Josiah Herne, Eunice 

Herne, and their eight children, Felicity, Thankful, Deborah, Increase, Barnabas, Praise God, Isaiah, and

Joanna. Carried into captivity by the Indians. All returned but Joanna, who married an Indian Chief. Back to the parish after what wild adventures, safe. All but Joanna. 

Smiling at the thought of Joanna, Oliver went past the stern young man, and out again to the sunshine. Did she prefer bright beads and blankets to a grey gown?

No more long sermons for Joanna. He could see her, with her wild dark face, her long hair thrust through with a scarlet feather.

The little white church looked familiar, with its sagging spire and fine lawn. He turned in. The old square stalls were fresh with white paint, the hymnals and palm leaf fans lay neatly. There were subscription envelopes too. God himself needed money and it is more blessed to give than to receive, yet I say unto you it is harder for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God than for a camel to pass through a needle's eye.

Oliver took up his hymnal and sat still, he hadn't been in a church since he left Iowa, he felt alien, and was afraid he looked it.

The church was filled. Neat spare men and worn

placid women. The Sunday school class on the front rows, wriggling like puppies, dropping and retrieving pennies. Behind them an old man in blue serge, his face eroded by time into deep hollows and stark bones, his old hands thin on the hymnal. A few young girls with fresh faces and round arms. One old gaunt colored woman, grey-black, neat in white cotton gloves.

The minister, a young earnest man with a flat voice, opened the service while Oliver was yet wondering why he was there. The reed organ quivered into a hymn. 

Clear in the clear air rose the old music. The children scuffled for their pennies, the old man raised a worn note like the echo of a voice, not the voice itself.

Oliver sang, too. It eased him, for no reason that he could see. Oh, love that wilt not let me go, I raise my weary heart to thee-I lay in dust life's glory dead, and from the grave there blossoms red, life that shall richer be.

Helen, shadowy, silent, a water-lily in a deep pool. 

The Lamb of God. Oh, love that wilt not let me goah, from the grave blossoms what? A bisque doll. A scarred door. A bit of faded calico. All returned but 

Joanna. What an epitaph! Oliver Heron-love will not let him go.

He roused himself. The minister was praying. "We know we are sinners. We come full of sin. Therefore we praise thee, Lord God." There was an idea. I love her. What is my sin? That I have ravished another man's wife, the mother of two children? Or that I do not take her away? Sin against love. Sin against self. 

There's the sin. Therefore, should I praise God?

"Here in this peaceful Connecticut valley let us realize we have a mission, we are blessed in the midst of trouble, let us not fail to exalt thy name.' "Praise God from whom all blessings flow!"

Praise God for the starving millions, for rape and murder, for grief and bitterness? For life? Praise him because the woman I love can never be mine? Suddenly 

Oliver remembered the wide deep gaze of Helen, he saw her eyes fixed on his, clear and grave. He saw, he felt, the smooth hollow between her breasts, the warm quick beat of her heart under his hand. It was his.

Neither God, if there were a God, nor the devil, if there were a devil, nor man, so much of both, could diminish that memory. 

And Oliver, opening to the responsive reading, heard his own voice most strangely with the rest, "From whence cometh my help? My help cometh from the hills."

Leaving church, he shook hands with the young minister. He was aware of a special kind of loneliness, he alone did not belong. A trifle wistfully he lingered, watching the people scatter and climb in old Fords and new Buicks and rattle or snort away to family tables and the conjugal chicken.

He ate his own dinner, and walked to Miss Bramble's house. Curious business, hunting a house for another man. Miss Bramble opened the door. "Dr. Lewisohn?" she said. "No. But Dr. Lewisohn sent me. My name is Heron." "Ah," she said. "You-you aren't familiar with these parts?" "No. I've never been here."

She looked at him vaguely. "I've such a poor head for names."

She was a small slight person with fluttering hands and a soft fluttering voice. "I have such a hard time with my papers," she confided. "I'm not used to-I had some things listed — "Mr. Wells will be here with a car soon. He's my partner," she said nervously. "I'm sure I had___” "I wanted especially to see something in or around 

Cambridge," said Oliver. No need to say why. If there were something-go carefully. "That's a little far off. Do you know anything about feeding owls?" she asked plaintively. "I have an owl.

He won't eat. All my life I've wanted an owl and now 

I have one. And he won't eat.' "Try raw meat," Oliver advised. "Ah, here they are. There's a little place on Poverty

Mr. Wells. Thank goodness." She fluttered to the door. "I'll look at what you have near Cambridge first,' said Oliver. 99 "We'll swing around." Mr. Wells chewed his cigar stub. "You told him about the places, Letitia?" 

They exchanged glances. "I haven't told him much.” "Well, well, nothing like looking." Mr. Wells was hearty. "That last place-get your rubbers, Letitia, it's swampy there!"

At the top of the hill, they inspected the first place. "Of course it needs cleaning up," said Mr. Wells, "family of Polacks live here. Nice old place though.

Dam up the brook and make a dandy little lake." 

Letitia put her nose in her handkerchief. They waded through the filth in the yard. Children and chickens scattered before them. A slack-bosomed woman stood in the kitchen. She nodded. The stench rose in Oliver's nostrils, he lit a cigarette. "This would make a study," Mr. Wells was puffing.

Beneath the fine old mantel a broken sofa stretched. 

On the sofa lay half a slaughtered calf, dried in its own blood. The table was propped with an old box. Rags stuffed in the windows sent greasy shadows over the floor. "Let's get out," said Oliver, pinching his nose. "Of course it needs cleaning," said Mr. Wells dubiously as they picked their way back. "But it's a fine old house."

It was a fine old house, or rather it was the disintegrated bony structure of past life. Oliver made a note of it, adding half the price for rebuilding. Lewisohn couldn't move in there this season, that was evident. "Are you selling much?" asked Oliver. "All the time," said Mr. Wells. "City people. They're a terrible nuisance. Run around getting the water in little bottles and getting it tested. Water's been drunk round here for a hundred years. You aren't city are you?" "Well-no. I've been around," Oliver said absently. "Sold a place near this one last month," Mr. Wells

waved his arm. "What a time we had! Woman sent a truckload of furniture up from Virginia and it got lost. 

Letitia and I were up all night chasing it. About twothirty a truck come along from Kentucky. Letitia here was sure that was the one. Got out and insisted on it. 

Opened the door and there was a horse! I'm going home, I said, and get some sleep." He turned in a driveway. "Just got my collar off when Letitia phoned. The woman hunting the horse found our truck in Roxbury!" He laughed. "We was unloading up till four A. M. 

Eyah, it's a great business. Like this place?" "No," said Oliver. "It's too pretentious. Is this all you have?" "The place in Cambridge is a bargain.” "You see," said Letitia hurriedly, "it hasn't been occupied since—not for some time. So it's very cheap." "We'll look at it anyway," Mr. Wells said heartily. 

His eyes twinkled. "Take a look anyway. Might not interest you. It's old all right.” "It has the loveliest view," said Letitia, "and all the old hardware." She looked at Mr. Wells. In the rearview mirror their eyes met. He shook his head slightly. 

They took the road winding through the hills. "It's beautiful," said Oliver suddenly, "such a familiar kind of beauty. It's as if one remembered it." "That's why it's nicer than Switzerland," Letitia said quickly. "Exactly what a woman said last week. 

She said Switzerland startled her."

The hills fell away, curving to a valley green as a dream and soft as sleep. In the late afternoon sun, they took a narrow rutted road that fled away before them

through a wooded slope. Oliver sat forward in his seat. 

Letitia sneezed in a delicate tribute to the goldenrod making a golden haze at the edge of the road. "There it is!" she fluttered, waving her small handkerchief and at once burying her nose in it again. 

A still meadow sloped down and away and up again smooth as a sea. On a little rise of ground stood the house, square and quiet at the end of the road. It had always been there, time out of mind, with its white clapboards and sagging roofline, and small many-paned windows. Serene and strong and self-contained it stood, had so stood while the trees grew tall above it, overshadowed it, while time fell like leaves in the yard. A hundred and fifty-two hundred years had gone down the wind before it, and it might well bear two hundred more. "Take me there," said Oliver.

There were five appletrees in the back yard. There was the brook singing a tuneless little tune in the meadow. And the mossy well-house-little boys mustn't go near the well. "Take me there," said Oliver, breathing hard. "Who owns this place?" "It's for sale through the probate court," answered

Mr. Wells. "A man who owned it never lived in it. He died in one of those aeroplane wrecks.” "What was his name? Who was he?" "The last owner? His name was Smith," said Mr. 

Wells succinctly. "Of course he never moved in.” "Nobody has. Nobody would" Letitia sneezed again, muffled herself and was silent. "That's a Lithuanian farmer lives across the road,"

said Mr. Wells hastily. "Name's Paul. Dropped off the poni or the ski or whatever it was. Nice neighbors, good solid people. Litvaks. Been there thirty years—maybe forty. Here we are, get out the key, Letitia." 

Across the deep grass Oliver followed them.

It was a small house with steeply slanting roof and a low doorway. Oliver stooped to enter, then took a deep breath, smelling the damp dead air of a long-closed house. Mr. Wells led the way, Miss Bramble hesitated outside, then gathering up her skirts tiptoed after them. "And the furniture goes with it," said Mr. Wells, his voice loud as a drum in the silent place. "Why is that?" "Well you see it belonged to the other ownerhere's the livingroom!” "They haven't taken anything away," murmured Letitia.

There were five rooms downstairs, low-ceiled, with wide old floors, white batten doors, and faded wallpapers. Fireplaces in every room, and in the center room a great hearth blackened and cracked, a dutch oven and a rusty crane. The furniture was old, genuine, dusty and chipped a little but worth a small fortune, Oliver thought.

People didn't walk away from places like this leaving the shelves full of ancient books, candles in the candlesticks, and a linen cloth on the dining table. Climbing the steep stairs, Oliver felt a little dizzy, as if he had drunk too much. There was a quality of stillness here -the house in its very quietness was saying something to him. There was something he must know-must discover-

He turned to ask Mr. Wells, then closed his lips. 

Whatever the house meant, these two wouldn't tell him. 

There was a conspiracy of silence between them. 

Miss Bramble did not go into the big bedroom. Mr. 

Wells crossed it, and dilated on the glories of a modern bathroom, "right in this genuine old colonial." But

Oliver pushed past a spinning wheel and threw open the window. Something oppressive was at his throat, a nameless darkness. Leaning on the sill, he breathed the sweet air and looked to the meadow rippling like water in the summer wind. That maple bed, massive and ancient, that candle stand with the melted wax on the mellow surface-the mirror with a shadowy meadow in it "I'll wait downstairs," said Miss Bramble, creaking down the steps. "And there are three more bedrooms," boomed Mr.

Wells. "It isn't such a small house as it looks from the front."

Under the bed lay a pair of ladies' bedroom slippers, silvery with dust. Mice had nested in the mattress, bits of wadding stuck through the stained ticking. 

Oliver left the room. The heat was affecting him. His head felt light and his feet heavy. Fact and fancy fused in his mind, he couldn't identify one from the other. 

This was his house, his and Helen's. It was still as death, because she was not in it. In her was life. 

He moved warily to the small room at the end of the hall. From it you could see directly into the big room if the bathroom doors were open. There was a crib in the little room, a rocking horse with an empty eyesocket leaned against it, tail rigid and nostrils breathing

still a faded fire. A frieze of faded toy soldiers marched steadily downhill where the roof slanted, came up again. Carefully he touched the little blue rocker. Yes, that was real, solid. But the little boy who sat rocking there, that was a dream. Was it Helen's child and his? 

He must get over these ridiculous fancies.

As he looked out again, he saw Miss Bramble in earnest conversation with the tall gaunt farmer, at the edge of the farmer's yard. She was shaking her head like a small determined bird. The farmer shook his own in answer, shrugged his shoulders, finally nodded and shuffled away. Once he paused and looked back at the house, then he shrugged his shoulders and disappeared. 

Mr. Wells had his head up the attic stair. "Plenty of room up here too," he said. "Good roof on the house and the chimney's in fine condition. Fine." "Come on down," said Oliver. "I want to talk business with you."

Mr. Wells descended, bumping his head on the beam and muttering under his breath. His face was red. "Dandy little place here,” he repeated, wiping his hands.

Within an hour Oliver had rented the house, pending the purchase by Dr. Lewisohn, had paid down two weeks' rent and promised the rest if the house remained unsold another two weeks. He telephoned the doctor. "Well, when you move you move quick," said the doctor. "My God, I can't come up for a week yet, or ten days. You only been up there a day yourself! However, if it's the stuff, better grab off an option and get things under weigh. I wouldn't put it past Brant Carlyle to buy it out from under my nose if he knew it was a good buy. You think it's a bargain? Hell, I don't know.

You use your own head, it's all right. If I don't like it, we'll not close the deal. That's simple. Hire somebody to clean up and move in, I may bring a guest or two when I come. Ring off now, Heron, this is costing money." 

It was almost too smooth, too easy. If I were superstitious, thought Oliver, paying his hotel bill, I would be sure this was destiny. But it can't be. Odd that the place has enough furniture so I can get in it! But even 

I have to have a break now and then, I expect. 

Small threads winding-the doctor's anxiety to have a place the name of Cambridge-the lawyer's wordsthe measles—the strange dark feeling of the house impelling him—there was no grand gesture of fate here, not at all. Only a winding of small natural threads, thought Oliver, snapping his typewriter down and lighting a cigarette.

He ordered a few groceries, bought a blanket at the Postoffice and meat market, and walked the three miles to the house carrying his typewriter and bag. It was now Monday morning. Sunday he had leased a house, Monday he moved down the road, bright and sweet with summer lights and scents.

* * ¶ Time had not exactly ceased, it had not yet begun. 

Oliver, sitting in the golden evening before the house, felt the hush, the suspension of beginning. The roof was mended, the house clean, a carton of bedding and china from Macy's put away. The doctor had made one flying trip and gone back with a bottle of well water, after the manner of city folk. "Stay here," he said, "and get the floors waxed for me. You might as well, there's nothing for you in town this week. A favor to me, can't be bothered. Herehere's a note from Helen."

Oliver held it now, the fine slanting lines stirred him бeyond all content. Dear:- 

I will let you know when I can see you. It won't be long now. Helen. 145

Underneath it was the comment on his last manuscript. "Unfortunately it is too somber for our use, was the consensus of opinion. I do hope you will let us see something else soon. We are badly in need of short stories."

He tore the letter up and the pieces sifted in the uncut grass. Helen's note went back in his pocket. 

As the evening deepened, fireflies flickered across the meadow. The farmer's cows came down the road. Oliver stretched out his long legs and approached the farmer. 

Hemmed in by the cows, the fellow couldn't disappear behind the barn, as he usually did. He came on, swinging his stick. "Good evening," said Oliver pleasantly.

Mr. Paul swung his stick. His small blue eyes gave a quick glance at Oliver, a slight quiver passed across the leathery face. "Could I have a pint of milk?” 

Mr. Paul jerked his head toward the house. "You goin' to stay there?" He spoke in a queer guttural, making the words sound foreign. "For a little while." 

Mr. Paul shook his head. "I give you some milk if you want him," he said briefly. "You've lived in America a long time," Oliver offered him a cigarette. "Forty year." "Then you knew the people who lived here in this house?"

Over the face went a sudden look, watchful, suspicious. Then it was blank as a shuttered house.

"I'm a good neighbor," said Mr. Paul. "I mind my own business. Other people they mind theirs. So nobody get wrong. دو "I'd like to meet Mrs. Paul," said Oliver. "She don't talk English much. Litvak only. Go to back door and she will give you milk." "I'd like some every day-as long as I'm here." "You thinking of buying the house?" "No. Not for myself."

Mr. Paul hesitated. The bright blue eyes peered at him. Suddenly he drew a step nearer and put his old brown face close to Oliver. "Trouble goes with that house," he said in a hoarse whisper. Then he backed away, swung his stick and shouted at the cows. In a flurry of dust, cows and man swung into the barnyard, leaving Oliver at the road's edge staring after them. 

Oliver looked back at it, with a little silver moon curved over the chimney and the fading luster of evening behind it. Strange the feeling of possession he had for it. As he possessed Helen, so this place. Another man's house, another man's wife. What could he possess himself? 

He crossed the farmyard. The chickens scattered. A cat with a broken back sidled past, curiously out of gear. In the barn the work horses stamped softly. Mr. 

Paul's eldest boy bent over a rusty wheel by the sagging steps. "Fixing something?" asked Oliver. "Making a radio," said the boy, "we found the stuff in the junk pile." 

Mrs. Paul waddled out in a dirty apron. She wiped her

red, cheerful face. "Benn hot today. I think like it rain.” "Could I have some milk?" "Sure. Nize fresh milk. I think more better tonight's milk." Her little deep eyes twinkled. "You stay in house by yourself?" "Yes." "You no afraid?” "What of?" 

She shrugged her shoulders. "Sure. No afraid." She beamed. "I think no like afraid. You wait. I bring him milk in nize clean bottle." Oliver sat down on an upturned wash tub. He would learn more if he were not curious, he decided. So he said idly, "There's a radio over in my house. But there's no aerial." 

"I know. I'll put it up. It goes to the walnut tree.'

They knew a lot about the place. Oliver walked catlike around his questions. Was it just that Litvaks were tight mouthed? Or was there something special? Mamma looked at him queerly. Or was it his imagination? Was there really a conspiracy of silence? Or were the agents trying to make a cleaning up on a hapless New Yorker?
