The two other boys came up, nodded shyly at him, and bent over the dry bones of the radio. They were all fair, tall and brown, with big strong bodies and quick blue eyes. They were compact, quiet, their hands were strong and supple, under their torn shirts tawny shoulders and coppery chests showed. Lennie, the oldest, had the little close-set eyes, George had wide brown eyes,

Vince looked like a Russian with a hawklike nose and narrow lids. Lennie had a slow voice, heavy with authority. He was the heir. The heir to fourteen hours a day over a hand hoe, to pitching hay loads in the dusty sun, to butchering the calves and hauling the ice. 

George had a voice like music with a lilt and lift and a pagan gaiety. He was laughing, bending his curly head over the radio.

Vince was shy, and said nothing. 

Oliver had a sudden vision of the white-lidded city boys hanging to subway straps, their mouths slack and tired, their shoulders thin under the natty suits. Life carved with a generous hand bodies like these, he thought, farmers died young, he had read, but their bodies were vital while they lived. "You come in kitchen," said Mamma, waddling back from the milk house. She carried a dripping pail and a tin dipper. Milk flowed generously over her generous self. "Nize clean bottle."

The cat trailed after her. Oliver followed the cat. 

Five small kittens materialized from under the house and walked delicately after him, eyes blue and wide and whiskers quivering. George whipped one up against his neck, his sensuous voice caressed it. He laughed. “You old kitten!” he said in his gay soft voice.

Mamma fished up a dubious bottle and measured the milk. "I wash him clean," she assured Oliver. He grinned. "I expect it keeps you busy," he assented, watching her fat thumb in the milk. 

Mamma wiped her hands on the apron. "Oh God I work all time," she said in her rich deep voice. "All

time work. I tired." She sat down, spreading her legs apart and heaving a long sigh. "I tired." 

Oliver ventured to sit down too. "You have a big family to take care of." 

Mamma rolled her eyes. "Some time I think I go in box pretty soon," she said. "All time wash-washwash-that Lennie-wash him stockings every day. His feet stink," said Mamma displaying her best English. "Has the house across the road been empty long?" asked Oliver, lighting a cigarette. 

Mamma's eyes sparkled. "My Tessie she live in Waterbury," she said, "maybe some twenty year-ten yearmy Tessie work for the poor lady when he live there."

She leaned forward, her face beaming with excitement. "My Tessie," she breathed, "My Tessie work for him.” "For whom?” 

Mamma broke into gutturals. "I cry," she said at length. "Nize lady. Such nize lady. And pretty! He was pretty!"

Mr. Paul stood in the doorway, a dark look on his face. In Litvak he addressed his wife. She answered shrilly. He pounded his fist on the table. Mamma shrugged. "Some time you like nize little hen maybe?" she said brightly. 

Oliver rose. "Some time," he said taking his bottle of milk.

Mr. Paul showed him out. "Old man bring rain tomorrow," he said, "bring rain for sure." دو Oliver crossed the road and went in. Running a paper in his typewriter, he lit a cigarette and stared at the keys in their senseless pattern on the keyboard. Words, and

letters. And the profundity of life. And light romance, please, if you would write. He began a new story. 

But under the words ran another script. Where was 

Helen now? Would she like this place? What had the old house known? Who slept in the maple bed, whose eyes followed the ride of John Gilpin on the faded wallpaper in the livingroom? All across the wall galloped

John Gilpin in his dim scarlet coat. "Stop, stop, John Gilpin, here's the house," 

They all at once did cry; "The dinner waits, and we are tired." 

Said Gilpin, "So am I."

The wallpaper repeated the poem in a pattern. But was there any pattern in existence? Or a meaningless jumble?

He pushed aside the typewriter and went out. No use to sit with his hands frozen to the keyboard while his mind went round in circles. Dew was falling. He couldn't walk through dew. There was something about running in wet grass-and being afraid. He sat on the stone coping of the terrace and lit a cigarette. Fireflies brightened over the meadow. Far away, under the hard white lights of the city, was Helen, and their lives were going on while they were apart, just as separate as if each lay in a well-buried coffin. It was too much to be endured, and it was endured. 

Elizabeth, Joan, and Brant were her life. He was outside, only Oliver Heron, who loved her.

Helen was there, with her eyes remote, her hands clasped, and the smooth hair quiet. Sleeping. Or waking.

Sleeping or waking, not with him. His loneliness rose in him dark as a tide. "And so live ever, or else swoon to death." 

Keats had died. For what end? Was it enough that his passion lived between Morocco pages in every library? That schoolboys memorized his dates and labored through the Grecian Urn? Keats, scornful, a dense ghost, mocked him. Would he not give all this immortality on paper for a night on Fanny's breast?

Little simple Fanny, a child with a star in her hand. 

Did Helen sleep? Or wake? His hands went out to the empty air and closed on it. A bird in the darkness cried. A moth blundered against his smouldering cigarette. 

Oliver went in, turned up all the lights, and drank a pint of whisky, dulling his senses so that he could sleep. The bed swayed like a boat. The floor rose and fell, uneven, dark. Night came softly against the screen and sighed there. Drunk, he could take the world in his hands and break it open like a ripe orange, and drink the juice. Drunk, he could hold her in his embrace and take her breath into his dry lungs and be a God. 

But when morning came he was only a man puttering around the yard, desperately manufacturing fiction that might sell and buy him bread and continued existence.

And waiting for a destiny that darkened the air like leaves in Autumn.

× × ¶ A day of rain, as Mr. Paul had predicted. Oliver went round and round the house, prying into corners, rummaging in cupboards. He could feel the house resisting him, silent, withdrawn, lonely. The annoying thing was the sense of familiarity. Almost he knew, almost the fog in his mind lifted to reveal the past, then it closed in again. Did he really remember a shadowy woman in a blue dress, or was it a trick of firelight on the wall that suggested it?

Desperately he sent his thoughts back, the past must be there. Everything that ever happened left an imprint on something. Kneeling before the bookshelves, he ran his hands behind the books. Where the boards failed to meet, he felt papers. 

Better put them back. 

No, better discover.

A sudden drench of rain against the window dark- 153

ened the room so that he turned on the light. Pushing aside the Waterbury American he rubbed the dust from his fingers. The first paper was a seed catalogue. An order blank at the rear, filled out in a fine feminine hand. March 29-no year. Asters, roses, two dozen pansy plants, larkspur, delphinium.

Somebody had ordered flowers, and never sent the order. Had it been forgotten? A stamped envelope addressed to the seed company lay beside the order. The catalogue itself was partially torn, the date was missing. 

The hand that wrote the order never picked the larkspur and delphinium. And here it was, preserved, the spring planning. He knew she had decided on asters, roses, pansies, larkspur and delphinium. 

What else did he know? Well, people didn't order such things as roses unless they expected to be there. 

Pansies were for a season, but roses were for years. He went back to the roses, Jacqueminot, Rambler, Yellow 

Tea Rose, American Beauty. The writing was firm, young. Not spidery and uncertain like the script of age.

The t's crossed with a bold stroke, the g's falling swiftly below the line.

The second paper was a letter, dated March 31st, New 

York. There was no envelope.

Dear Bunny:- 

I wanted to see you before I went to South 

America. If I hadn't been afraid of your family turning me out, I would have come up. Do you ever remember good luck to you. Yours, Edgar.

Two names. Bunny and Edgar. That was all he could make of it. Edgar had gone to South America. What should Bunny remember? Was Bunny the asters and roses?

The third paper, in a third hand, had a single scrawl on it. "For Laurie. Would we not shatter it to bits and then 

Remold it nearer to the heart's desire?" 

It was the fly leaf of a book.

The fourth paper was part of a cook book, and there was a name in it, written in the same hand as the flower order. Oliver bent over it. Diana G. Patterson.

He thrust the papers back and went out in the rain. 

Diana. Laurie. Bunny and Edgar. Three of the names woke no response, but Laurie-he had heard that name in his dreams. There was a Laurie. Oh God, to remember!

But now all he could think of was the rocking horse with a missing eye.

The wrack of storm clouds piled above him, the rain came down, trees bent to the rush of water, and the rutted road flowed in a stream. 

What will it mean to me when I do find out? asked

Oliver, striding past the dripping hedges. Will it clarify my own life? If I knew from what I came, would I then-but what is the mystery of that place? 

Head bent, he strode along, coming at last to the village. He turned in at the roadstand and ordered coffee. The radio was going. Beatrice Lillie was singing, 

"Mad Dogs and Englishmen Stay Out in the Midday 

Sun." "Staying around here?” "Yes," said Oliver, then he added vaguely, "I'm look-

ing around. They tell me the old place at the crossroads is for sale. The Smith place." "Oh I wouldn't spend a night under that roof," said the waitress impulsively. "Why not?" "After what happened there?" 

She shook her head. "You don't know? Oh, well, I wouldn't say anything to spoil a sale. It's a nice place, I guess." She eyed him shrewdly, "I always say what folks don't know can't hurt." 

Oliver pushed his cup aside impatiently. "Was it Mr. 

Smith's getting killed that ruined the place?" "Mr. Smith. Oh, no. He never lived there. He only just bought it." She turned to wait on a truck driver. 

Mr. Smith, then, was an intervening name, nothing more. Oliver went back along the muddy road. The trick was to be disinterested, not to be connected with the place. And not to betray ignorance. He stopped at the barber shop. This time he was wary. "I suppose you have lots of business here in summer," he said settling in the chair. "Yes. Lots of city people." The barber stropped a razor. "Must be quiet in winter." "Eyah. Quiet." 

Oliver wanted to cry out, "For God's sake tell me the truth about the Smith house!" And he said carelessly, "Too bad the old Smith place doesn't sell." "Well-it's not likely to." He soaped Oliver's thin face. "You mean the agent's against a tough proposition selling that."

"On account of what happened," said Oliver softly. "Eyah. Funny thing, I give him a hair cut just two days before it happened. Let's see, that was around thirty years ago. I was just startin' business. He had blonde hair, real wavy. I cut it too close. I can see him yet, standing in the door cursing. Eyah, he cursed pretty good. You want a shave too?" 

Oliver nodded. "He used to ride a big brown horse. Come tearing down, sitting that curious English way. She had a white horse. Well, you can't tell what will happen in this world. Did I cut you?" "No. No." "She had pretty hair too, kind of brownish gold, the only woman in these parts who had short hair. Sort of waved around. Funny how women took up with cutting their hair. Makes a lot of business for barbers. I always like to see a girl with real long hair; my wife says I married her because she wore three switches." "What was her first name?" "Amanda Jenkins. She was a school teacher. I met her at the Baptist Young People's corn husking bee." 

Oliver choked on the shaving suds. "I didn't mean your wife's name," he said. "Oh, her. Her name was Diana. But he always called her Laurie. He called all his girls Laurie, they said. Eyah, he called them Laurie always. You want some tonic?

This here Lucky Tiger is about the best. But speaking of hair, I got a tar solution you put on and sleep in for baldness, wrop a newspaper around. Then you wash it out in the morning. It's awful powerful. If you can

sleep for the smell. My wife says she'd just as lieve have a polecat on the next pillow. But I tell her it's bad business for a barber to be bald. It don't look right." 

Oliver choked again. "Take it easy, I'm about done.” The barber wiped his face. "You got a good head of hair. Ever troubled with dandruff? I give a series of treatments for five dollars. 

Tones up the scalp. You going to be around long?" "No. Not long." 

Oliver went out to find the sky clearing. His precious bits of information, he stored in his mind. He was getting something.

Lennie was measuring wire in the yard when he got back. "Had to quit haying on account of the rain," he said. "So I thought I'd fix this radio." "I haven't bought anything for it." "Oh, well, I'll pick up enough stuff around. Can I use that old wheel hub?" "What for?" "Hang on the wire so when it storms it'll swing loose. 

I cut off the barn wires. You ain't keeping cows are you?" "No." "That's a good thing," said Lennie. "Less you was born and raised on a farm, you better not try to farm." "Lots of people are nowadays." "They don't know the tricks." Lennie wound the wire in a noose around his neck, tied the twenty-pound weight to his waist. He hooked pliers in his hip pocket, and a hammer in his belt. "When you farm you got to do everything. You can't just buy seeds and put 'em in.

Man come up here last year, going to raise cabbage for a sauerkraut place. Put in every cent in them cabbages. Cut worms ate 'em all. Funny." He squinted at the sky. "I'll climb that maple." "Where's the ladder?" "I don't need no ladder.” Lennie put his arms around the ancient trunk. Hammer, pliers, wire and iron went with him to the first branch a good fifteen feet from the ground. Leaves and sticks fell. Pliant and sure as a monkey, he went on, up out of sight. At the top of the tree, higher than the ridgepole of the house, his face appeared framed in wet leaves. "Catch this when it swings down, will you?”

Hips braced against the trunk, one hand holding a branch, he looped and knotted the wire, grappled the weight, dropped lightly to the lower branches while the wire snaked down to Oliver. "I couldn't do that," said Oliver ruefully. "Climbin's nothin'." Lennie spliced the wire, pulled it to the house, swung himself to the well house roof and the next moment was at the peak of the ridgepole. "There," he said, "turn her on and see can you get

Bridgeport. We got Cuba over on our junk pile radio." 

He fished out a cigarette and came down with it in his mouth. "That all?" "Thanks," said Oliver. "I got to butcher a calf before supper." "How about a drink first?” "Oh, sure." 

They went in. Oliver mixed a highball. "So you don't advise me to take up farming?" "No."

"I guess it's hard to make a farm go nowadays." "Well, we get along. Course we don't get much for stuff any more. But we can live.” "How big is your family?" "Well, there's Tessie, she's in Waterbury. She's fortyseven or so, and Mary, she's in New York working out, and Les, he's the one in Danbury. Then there was Bill, he got burned up when the barn burned down, and Bennie that the bull gored. So there's nobody home now but me and the two young kids. The farm's mine, half of it." "Have another?" "I don't mind." Lennie wiped his mouth with his hand. "Most of the old stuff in this house came from ours," he said. "Oh, did it?" 

Lennie blinked. "Where do you come from?" he asked suddenly. 

So they were curious too. "From Iowa," said Oliver with sudden decision. "Oh, Iowa," Lennie's small eyes were on him, bright, weighing something. "Then you haven't been round here before?" "No." He knew enough not to add, perhaps I was. 

Oliver lit a cigarette. "What a terrible thing to happen," he said. "H'm," said Lennie. Then he added suddenly, "Ask

Tessie when she comes up. I don't know anything. I mind my own business. Besides I was a baby. Mamma was giving me whisky for colic. Tessie was the one." 

He got up. "You don't want some fresh veal, do you? 

I got to kill that calf.”

"Are you going to sell it?" Oliver got up too. 

Patience, patience. "Well, we could give you a piece. You doing your cooking?" 

Oliver nodded. "We generally can what we don't eat. Killed a bull last winter," Lennie paused idly in the doorway, "weighed seven hundred pounds. Canned him." "My God-seven hundred pounds!” "Sure. We don't buy no meat. We sold the hide, though. We don't buy nothing much. A farm don't pay if you go to town every week," said Lennie seriously. "Christmas time during the fast, we don't buy fish even.

We eat mushrooms. The old woman picks 'em and dries 'em. Well, thanks. Good night." 

Oliver boiled an egg and made toast. The self-sufficient farm. Lennie and his family didn't read, but they lived, they survived. They were a compact little world, wonderful to behold. The hope of the future might be in this solid peasant stock. Perhaps the wheel had turned. From the refinements of civilization-back to subsistence every family in itself. It was solid. It was solid.

After his meal he washed the plate, cup and saucer. 

The last ray of sun was coming through a crested wave of storm cloud. Cool and fresh as water the air lay over the fields. He could see the cabbage field in the soft apricot light and George and Vince were hoeing, by hand, rows of soft green-blue cabbages. As he moved up the hill, George straightened and picked up a rifle. There was a brief putt-putt, a ball of smoke. When he came close, he could see a rabbit, twitching warm legs.

"These ole jackrabbits," said George in his joyous voice. "They eat the cabbage."

The rabbit rolled over once, and lay still. "He won't never eat no more cabbage," said George, taking up his hoe again. "I see him creep along." 

Death was as simple as that. A beating heart, swift motion, then a sharp sound, and warm blood spurting on the earth. Oliver bent over the rabbit. For this small furry thing, the mystery had been accomplished. Where had the life gone? Did it vanish or change? It was there and it was not there. And George, instrument of fate for the jackrabbit, was now busily giving life to the cabbages which in turn would give their life to human beings. So that in the end, all things might die. 

He walked down the cabbage rows. This was the real mystery, growth. From the earth these round green plants took something, took vitality, took the essence of life, the mysterious life-force. Rain and sun and the dark loam under the sun, and out of them, life. He took up a handful of dirt. Impassive it lay in his palm, and yet how much life slept in these particles. Men should not get so far away from this growing. 

The cows were winding down the road, wet from the recent rain. Oliver dropped over the brow of the hill, nodding at George and Vince, and fell in with 

Mr. Paul. "You were right about the rain," he said. 

Mr. Paul grinned. "She rain some more tonight."

Oliver could think of nothing to say. He moved along in silence and was rewarded by Mr. Paul's saying, "You stay alone here all time?"

"I'm expecting Dr. Lewisohn soon." "He good neighbor like yourself?” "He's the man who is buying the house. Yes, he'll be a good neighbor." "Well, I like see light in house again. Real light." He nodded. "You not scared of nothing?" "What should I be scared of?" وو "People scared sometimes-old house. Fonny, I scare when I see furst firefly. I come to this country and I walk out in field at night and I see firefly everywhere.' 

He spread his hands. "I think, devil is get me for sure. 

But I think I go along and see. Then I catch him and he just little bug." He laughed, deep and slow. "I think devil get me for sure this time!"

The cows went their solemn way to the barn. "It's generally what we don't know about scares us,' said Oliver, turning aside for his milk. دو 

Mamma was slicing onions in a pan. A hot sour smell rose from the shut inner room and fought to a standstill the reek of onions. Oliver made a fan of smoke over his nose. "I get milk," she said. "Find 'em nize milk." "Could I get some eggs also?" 

She considered him. "Some time lay 'em in nest. I go catch him," she assented. Taking a pan, she waddled out, her apron a sail before her. Oliver, on the stone step, saw her suddenly in the newly washed air of twilight as if she were earth itself, solid, fertile, unhurried, with a tranquillity deeper than human feeling. She might, conceivably suffer loss and grief, might weep, but the enduring, patient spirit would not change. Like the earth

her heavy squat figure, the earth materialized in human form, and standing against a sky of pearl.

The old red barn darkened in the ebbing light, but the hill above glimmered like a green clear sea, the junk piled in the yard took on shadowy shape and it too became significant. Men and the machines they made, thought Oliver-and tomorrow the power is gone and iron rusts back to earth, bone goes to lime, to sleep a while in the dark unstirred womb, then to live again, in the raspberry paw of a kitten or the slim brave blade of a newer tower.

For a moment, he too, merged with the darkening scene. His troubled, fevered mind grew still. A little cooling air breathed on his mouth. Presently he would take into himself the meaning of every rusted nail, every dark smooth leaf, every broken pebble ground small from some ancient titanic granite. He hovered on the edge of it, he breathed, it was gone. The quickened senses alone, the perception, the feeling-with the world were not, and never could be, enough to cross the bridge between finite man and infinite reality. 

Only in Helen, the word was made flesh. If he could turn, in this illumined instant, and see her walking across the dark yard, then at once he could pull the meaning of life as he now pulled a tangle of knot grass from the soil. "I find him nize eggs," said Mamma Paul, sucking in her breath pleasantly. "Small, yes, but small. You no like small?" "An egg," said Oliver, "is an egg.'

* * ¶ The curious sense of held time, and the actual isolation of his life took hold of Oliver like an illness, and gave him, finally, a fear of going back to the world. He didn't want to ask questions, he wanted to lie in the grass and smell the sun. After the frenzied activity of job-getting in a jobless world, he now wondered idly if the same Queen Anne's Lace grew in the same spot every year, if the fat paunchy spider came back to his web, if the pattern of life was perhaps in the material world, and the current of human emotion only a wind blowing over it without pattern. Or did the wind, too, have a pattern?

Shortly he must walk ahead into the dark room of the future, now he stood on the lintel, with undesirous eyes. So that the jogging sound of a car coming up his road shook his nerves like an earthquake. With a blue 165

trail of smoke and the panting of the exhaust, the car huffed into the Pauls' yard. Mamma came out, apron at full spread, and face gleaming like a moon. Papa, sucking hard on his stinking pipe, shambled behind her. 

Lennie and George and Vince appeared, walking with the curious light and slow gait of men who feel the earth solid beneath them. The cat with a broken back swayed out, and the chickens set up a soft chatter. 

Oliver, standing on his side of the road with his legs apart and his cigarette unlighted in his mouth, drew a long breath, half-fearful. 

My Tessie had come from Waterbury.

My Tessie had worked for the lady, and My Tessie could tell him as much as she would. 

Oliver went into the house and took out his little trove of papers. Whose hand, living and warm, made the flower order? And who was Bunny? And what could 

Tessie tell him about himself, Oliver Heron? 

Oh God, not to make a false step, not to over reach, but this time, finally, to know. In a sudden madness he took the phone and asked for long distance. There was nothing for it, he must hear her voice. It was very important, at this moment, to hear Helen speak, though he couldn't have explained why. His compulsion was obscure but violent.

All five of the families on the wire having been talked off the wire temporarily and all being satisfied that Mr. 

Heron was telephoning New York City, and having hung up just long enough to permit the connection to be made, Oliver heard, at last, Helen's voice with the cool little note of surprise running through it.

"Ah-Oliver!"

Then, absurdly, he could not speak. She was there, real and breathing at the end of a wire. As remote as a dream, and as close as his own ears. "Helen! Are you—all right?” "Why, yes, of course." "I just wanted to know if—if you and the children were all right!" "Yes. All right." "Helen, I think I'm going to find out about my father and mother." "Oh," she said softly. How quickly she caught the tension, how swiftly her mind ran to meet his in the word. "Oh!"

Suddenly time began again. Blood ran in slack veins and his heart beat heavy and rapid. "Yes. I am sure," he said. "I hope so." "Helen, can you hear me?" "Not too well." "Helen, listen, is it-important to you to know about my family?” "It matters to you. "Are you alone?" 99 "Yes. But you must not call me again. I've beennever mind. I will see you soon now. The children are almost well." "Helen. Whatever I am, I love you." "And I you." "Good-bye. How soon can I come?" "I will write you. Good-bye, Oliver."

The receiver hung on the hook. Life departed from the thing of metal. He was alone. The connection broken, the wire employed in some other use. He put the telephone back on the stand. His hand was shaking.

Divided, isolated, they were together. 

Now he had courage, and went forward. He took out his typewriter and finished the story, wrote to the doctor, and sent an inquiry to all the agencies. Then anxious to be at something tangible, he pulled the ragweed in the yard and dug up the burdock by the steps. 

Its strong vigorous leaves came hardily from the stem, there was life in the weed also.

Quite steady now, he took up a milk bottle as excuse, and crossed the road to see Tessie. 

Words rang in his mind. Odd bits of verse, lost quotations, the flotsam of a life in books. "By whose direction found'st thou out this place -By love that first did prompt me to inquire." "Not in thyself but in this lady's love. The woodspurge has a cup of three. Is there no pity sitting in the clouds that sees into the bottom of my grief? But what may be, we know not." "I've come for the milk," said Oliver in his best voice. 

The kitchen was thick with smoke. Where the red heat of the stove glowed hottest, stood Mamma stirring a beefy stew. Lennie, by the window, had the rusty entrails of a clock spread before him. The joyous George bent over the wood box, his arms full of split stove lengths. Hesitating in the smoke haze, Oliver looked to the farthest corner of the room. There was nobody there. 

Sick with disappointment, he lingered, sweating in the heat. 

Mamma, with a flourish, wiped his milk bottle with

her apron, and capped it with a firm thumb. "Nize fresh milk," she said.

He went out, avoiding the broken floor board at the threshold. Then as he passed the cellarway, he looked in and saw her, bending over a stone crock. The damp earthy opening was like a cave, the darkness framed her tall square figure. "Good evening," said Oliver. "Aren't you the other daughter?" It was all he could think of to say, and speak he must.

The figure straightened. Oliver stepped in the dusky well, his face hidden, as she stepped toward the light and lifted her face. He could see her blue eyes and silky fair hair and the strong cheek bones. "Yes. I'm Tessie." 

The milk bottle clinked against the cold steaming stone. He steadied it. Soft and rich, the voice assailed him with remembrance. Like a Delphic oracle, it came from the dark cellarway, and like a priestess stood Tessie, peering at him in the shadows. "I thought I heard you drive up," said Oliver, helpless. "I come up from Waterbury," she said slowly. "You're the man who stays over there?" "Yes." 

She wiped her hands on her blue apron. "I been making pickles," she said. The crock was full of cucumbers, she was washing them in cold water. A limp pile of dill lay on a clean board beside her. The smell of the cucumbers lay green and cool, mingled with the damp cellar smell. "You-used to work there," said Oliver.

"Yes. I was a girl. Just fifteen. I worked there." Her voice went back, far down the years. "I took care of the little baby boy. He was cute." "How old was he?" "Just running around. And after, I took care of him awhile until he was sent away." She pushed back her hair. "I carried him over here that night. They took him away pretty soon, so he wouldn't remember. They were scared he might, sleeping in the next room, and all. The door open, too. I slept with him." She sighed. "We never know what's coming in this world," she said. She sighed again. "She was so pretty. And afraid of nothing. He used to leave her and go to New York, even at first. Then he would call her up from the station as soon as he got in and tell her he was coming back and she would get out the car and drive clear to town in the middle of the night and get him. I'd sit up with the boy until they come in, two or three o'clock. I'd hear them, they had the first car round."

Oliver held his breath. He bit back the questions and stood quiet, hearing her speak above the roar in his blood. "She always come in laughing but Bunny · "Bunny!" 29 "Everybody called him that. Bunny would just sit, staring at her, drinking, and staring up under his eyebrows in a way he had. He had a funny look." "Did they quarrel?" "Oh, no. Not that. It wasn't that way," said Tessie vaguely. "There was something else. He just did queer things. One time he was gone and a man came to see her. I was ironing. I said to her, "There's a man to see you, a Mr. Merritt." And she got up and went right out

the back door and stayed off in the woods till he went away. After a while he did go, and Bunny came home. 

Seems he saw him going down the road. He come in, quiet, and said, 'Has that man been here?' And she said, 'I haven't seen him.' Well, then he did a funny thing. He went out and run the car right in the middle of the swamp and left it there. He had his supper, same as usual, and never said a word. Took Lennie and Pa and two horses a whole day to get it out. She sat on the hill and watched them, and then right in the middle of dinner she said, "You won't keep me that way.' She helped herself to string beans as natural and he said, easy as ever, 'You'll never leave me, Laurie.' I couldn't make anything of it. I just let the coffee boil over, looking at them. But when she leaned across the table, I went out and shut the door, not to be in the way." 

Oliver's mind harried the words. He was afraid of losing her. Was that it? Then did she run away finally with the Mr. Merritt. Or did Bunny leave her? Patience, Oliver, wind the reel gently. What was behind it all?

What had Tessie felt that now, in her dim way, she was unable to voice? He couldn't betray ignorance, asking where they were, what had happened. دو "I don't know what makes me think about it again,' said Tessie. "It was all so long ago, and now it might be yesterday. You see I went away afterward. I couldn't stay around here, right across from the house. I went out to work in Waterbury and got married and had my own children." "Did you ever see-the little boy again?” "No. The lawyer thought I better not. They was always careful not to let him see a picture of her, for

fear he would remember. There's a car coming up your drive. You expecting company?"

Oliver swore under his breath. Now, just when he was so close! "How long are you going to stay here?" "Oh, a week or so. I always help Ma with her canning." "Then I'll be sure to see you again.” "Why, yes," she said with faint surprise.

Oliver ran across the road. The doctor was just getting out of his car. "Just brought some company for the week end," he said cheerfully. "Get out, Gilda. You're too big to be carried. Come on Rudolf. This way, Rachel." "I can't move," said a plaintive voice. "Rudolf is sitting on me, fat as he is!" "I'll open up and turn on the lights," said Oliver.

The shrill voices pursued him into the house. Auto doors slammed, the doctor said something in Yiddish, and the group advanced to the house. 

Into the quiet living-room they swarmed and over its ancient dignity laid their personalities like varnish over old wood. They seemed to be all over the house at once. 

Not a cranny was secret enough to withstand their eyes. 

Oliver took the bottles the doctor gave him, found glasses, and mixed their drinks. 

Hugo Weiss, the banker, sent a hawk-like eye into the cellar, prowled to the attic. "Of course it's awfully crude," he said. "Better take out this wall here, and this, and make an extra bath here-and add a new mantel there" he gestured with his cigar. "Put a swimming pool on one side and tennis courts on the

other. You can make quite a little summer resort here!" "I want a golf course!" cried Gilda, "I'm sick of bridge all the time. Give me a golf course, big boy!" Her narrow-lidded eyes lifted at Oliver, the full mouth pouted, she put up a ringed hand to smooth the brandycolored hair. "Shouldn't I have a golf course?" she demanded. "It's good for the hips."

The banker's wife, Rachel, had already curled on the sofa, her lazy bright eyes sliding over Oliver, her head twisting from side to side. Her smooth tight body relaxed. "Cigarette, Oliver," she said wearily, "and more whisky." 

Oliver waited on her, ignoring the lazy insolent voice. 

Rudolf, the playwright, lolled in the arm chair. "How are you?" he said to Oliver. "I hear you're in the writing game. I'll look over some of your stuff and criticize it tomorrow." "Thanks," said Oliver shortly. "How's your work going?" the doctor asked Rudolf. "Don't ask him," said Rudolf's wife. "I've got hold of something really big," Rudolf lit a cigarette. She went in and sat on the edge of the maple bed. 

She was a small brown quiet woman, and she looked at Oliver with a thoughtful wisdom, setting himself and her apart to look at the place.

Oliver moved away. Avoiding the noise in the livingroom, he made his way to the kitchen and began to clear up the glasses. It was two o'clock before the game ended, the squabbling over money was finished, and the company started to bed. Gilda, quite drunk, was shunted up the stairs like a freight car, the doctor puffing and pushing like a vigorous engine behind. Rachel decided to sleep on the davenport, saving herself the effort of rising. Oliver found her a pillow and a blanket. Her cold sleepy eyes glittered at him, she reached a languid hand and he withdrew his too hastily.

The doctor and Hugo went outside, restless and awake,

they prowled round and round the house, their long cigars trailing a dark smell. Oliver was dead tired. The excessive vitality of these people wearied him. Every gentile sense cried for quiet. He sneaked out the back door and took refuge in the barn. The rickety old loft was half-full of hay, Papa Paul was making use of it. 

Oliver stretched his long body in the hay and looked up through the ragged roof at the stars, serene and bright after the rain. What else would My Tessie tell him? How soon could he go to Helen? His body and mind were dry, drained of strength. The desert of days since he had held her in his arms stretched behind him, arid. His mouth was full of sand. Now even to see her, to catch the turn of her head, to look at her from a great distance, would be the cup of water to the man dying of thirst. Soon, it must be soon. He told himself he would be content with a crust now, but in his heart he knew he would not. Perverse human spirit, never really fed. 

Rolling over, he buried his face in his arms, and slept.

* * ¶"You could tear up the old floors and lay good new oak," said Hugo. "Or I tell you what, slap a coat of fresh paint on the place, put some blue spruce in front, add a bath as I suggested, and re-paper with a snappy modern wallpaper, then sell the place for double what you pay and build yourself a colonial up on the hill." They had come in from dinner, eaten with gusto and noise at an Inn in the village. They were swarming over the yard, restless, excited over nothing, and just ready to be bored. The terrible dread of boredom keyed their voices even higher, made their opinions blatant. 177

Their little belongings gradually collected themselves.

Lipsticks, vanity cases, cigarette holders, cards, handkerchiefs, a scarf, a pair of diamond dice, all the trivia attendant on their lives, finally departed with them. 

They had flung themselves stridently at the house, possessed it, ravished it, now they piled out to the car, laughing and chattering like a flight of alien birds. The gear grated. The motor purred. The car moved away, caught the ruts, and lurched a trifle.

As it went down the road, faster and faster, Gilda leaned out to wave. Then she began to sing in her voluptuous voice. The song came back to him. "We'll make hay while the sun shines, We'll make love while it rains.”

Around the curve, sound and lights diminished, leaving the road dark and still. Oliver went into the house and swept up the cigarette stubs, put up the card table, threw the old newspapers in the fireplace, added Hugo's forgotten walking stick and an odd white mesh glove, soiled.

* * ¶ Directly after breakfast, Oliver crossed the road. It was a grey day with a brooding sky soft as a dove, and a slight feather of wind. George was carrying manure from the cow shed, surrounded by the strong reek of ammonia he was like a young god coming from Avernus. "I'd like to get some eggs," called Oliver. "Already?" George was surprised.

Oliver reflected that eggs as a pretense would soon fail, he must think of something else. How many dozen had he purchased in the past week? "Ask Tessie. She's in the barn," called George.

The rich warm smell of hay came from the open door as Oliver stepped in. A small defiant rooster rustled in the hay under the very nose of a somnolent cat. The air was drowsy with the sweet heaviness of hay. Oliver walked on it, feeling it soft under him. 187

Tessie bent over a box in the far corner, pulling out a fresh egg to add to the eggs in her pan. It was an old grey enamel pan with a hole in it, rust around the hole. 

Oliver didn't want to have the details etched on his mind, he was filled with the issue, but he found the mousy stir of the cat's whiskers and the tarnished gleam of the rooster's breast so vivid to his senses that he closed his eyes a moment. He could see the grasses that made the hay, with a scattering of withered Queen Anne's 

Lace, and the stalks of dried daisies. "Could I get some eggs?" he repeated his password.

God, why couldn't human beings speak to one another. 

Why not say, Tessie, tell me the story of those two, at once! I must know! "I guess," she said. Kneeling in the hay, she looked up at him. His back was toward the light, she could not see his face, but her eyes blinked, trying to catch his features. "I suppose, in a way, it was a good thing to take the little boy across the road away somewhere?"

That was the best he could do. He had to start somewhere. "Oh," she said. "Him?" She sat back in the hay. "Yes, of course. I always said if she only would have taken him away herself. I told her she better. Seems like she couldn't make up her mind to go, until at the last minute." "Oh," he said, "I wish I could understand," and the unguarded remark touched a response in Tessie. "I felt like that, too. If ever two people were crazy about each other, it was them. But I guess he got so she couldn't stand it, and maybe she said so. Maybe he drove

her to it. I do know that other man, he was jealous of. 

Sometimes he'd say, 'Well, Laurie, don't you wish you had him, the nice quiet Sunday school boy?' And she would look at him in such a funny way, and say, 'God knows.' Now what do you make of that?" "I don't know." "He was always so polite to me, soft as butter. Even when he got drunk and threw half the dishes in the fire, he was polite. 'Just a little left-over from the past,' he said, 'fuel to the fire, Tessie,' and he threw in the blue cups and saucers like lightning. I hated to see them go. 

And she come out, all dressed up in a new blue dress, and stood there looking at him with her head back, and then she said, 'Memory doesn't burn out.' I always remembered her saying it, it was such a queer thing. You'd think she would have carried on about him burning her best dishes and then she just stood there and said, 'Memory doesn't burn out.' Well, I don't know," said Tessie, "I don't know." "She meant he couldn't destroy the memory," said 

Oliver. Ah, he knew. Poor Bunny! For the first time a kind of aching sympathy went through him. 

No, the blue dishes never could be burned up. Whoever had given them to her, the other man, whom she had not taken, or someone she had loved, whoever it had been, that Bunny feared and hated, was permanent. "Then what happened?" asked Oliver. "Then he made a kind of groan, and she said, 'After all, you also have blue dishes in your past! Why didn't you go to South America with Edgar?" " 

Edgar! Ah, there it was. Slowly, slowly, a bit here and a thread there.

"Then what?" "I can't remember. They said such funny things," explained Tessie. "I only remembered a few, and it's been so long. I shouldn't remember any, except when things happened like the dishes that upset me so, I kept thinking about what they said for a long time." She got up with the eggs. "And of course, afterward, I went back and went back, trying to figure it out so I could understand." "How long-did you work there?" "About a year. Maybe more. It was my first job.

Mama let me go out because it was just acrost the road and I could always come home every day a while. I could go over with the baby and let him run around in our yard and see my folks. It made it nice. Did you want a dozen?” "Yes," said Oliver helplessly. Then he had a really splendid idea. "I don't suppose you could come over, Tessie, and clean the house for me?" "Why, yes, I guess. I might come tomorrow, not today. 

I have to clean for Mama today."

Oliver hurried back to the house. Another day, and he must know the whole truth. Excitement beat in him, he was snuffing the tracked prey.

With a sense of finality, Oliver arranged his manuscripts, mailed several, sent a note to the agencies, and telephoned Miss Bramble. The abstract would be delivered by the middle of the week, she assured him. Was the doctor ready to make the deposit and the cash payment at that time? The judge for the court had found

the price satisfactory. Such a sweet place, and the electricity alone had cost eighteen hundred dollars. The extension. She hoped he hadn't been-lonely. "No, I haven't seen any ghosts," said Oliver lightly. 

She hung up.

Another week, and what little of the place was his, would be his no longer. His sense of possession stronger than ever, he would have to go back to town and find a room somewhere, and begin again the waiting, walking the streets, waiting, walking the streets. Haunting the corners where Helen might pass, looking for her face, listening with taut nerves for her step, smiling a tight smile at the shadow that followed her.

He saw himself consumed by both love and hate. The passion of hate for Brant, growing in him with the growth of the passion of love for Helen. The more he loved her, the deeper the hatred for Brant. The image of Brant hung over him like a vulture, the beak dipped in his own blood. Someday he would even the account, before he died. But how much was his hatred of the man who made her suffer, and how much was his own personal jealousy? Who could measure such imponderables? It was enough that he hated him.

Alone in the house, he prowled restlessly, up and down, up and down. He went through all the books, leafing the yellow pages and wondering what hands leafed them last. Again he made a search for any scrap that might tell him something. The impress of what happened was here, somewhere, if he could find it. 

There must be something!

In the maple bedroom he found it. It was only an-

other order, neglected because it, too, was in the back of a catalogue, an old Sears, Roebuck catalogue. 

In the same fine hand, half finished, and with no date, there was the order: 

One dozen yards viyella flannel 

Two dozen yards birdseye no 1 

One half dozen slips number 345 

One dozen sheets size A 

One dozen binders

He stared at it. In the name of God, what did that mean? He carried the catalogue downstairs and propped it before him on the table in the kitchen while he fried two of the omnipresent eggs and made coffee. Viyella flannel! Birdseye! Binders! Did it have something to do with seed planting? Slips. Slips of what? Rose cuttings?

Sheets. Sheets size A. 

He walked up and down the yard, after his lunch, smoking and thinking. Then he had an idea. He rushed in the house again and frantically retrieved the catalogue from the ashes in the fireplace. There was no index. Half the leaves were gone. There was nothing for it but to begin at the beginning. 

So Oliver read the Sears, Roebuck catalogue, through farm implements, past bathrooms, beyond house furnishings, toys, and machinery. After an hour and a half, he poured himself a drink of whisky and went back again. He learned the price of cultivators, the cost of bicycles. Then he turned to a page decorated with infants floating in the catalogue fashion all over the page. 

There was a fat bonneted baby, a naked baby, a baby`

sucking a large ball, and there in the center of the page, a "Suggested List Order For Layette." 

One dozen yards viyella flannel 

Two dozen yards birdseye no 1 

One half dozen

He read it all, even to the rubber sheeting which she had not had time to list.

Then he sat back, sweat standing out on his forehead. 

For whom was this? These things were for a new-born baby, not for a little boy already running around. His mind leaped ahead. There had been another child! 

But wait! Not so fast, Oliver, not so fast! The order lay yellowing in the catalogue. No baby had kicked and squalled in the viyella flannel unless she had changed her mind, and shopped somewhere else. Had she gone away with that baby? 

But then what had happened to the little boy? How had he been left behind in the flight? He beat his closed fist against the catalogue, striving to penetrate the meaning.

She had expected a garden, she had expected a baby. 

Did the baby die? Those two-Laurie and Bunny, and the child! What darkness drew across them? Once again, he must wait. Tessie would tell him everything when she came to clean. She would remember, in the house, and she would talk to him. He would follow, very quiet, and listen, and piece together the pattern. 

He laid the layette order with the flower order and the letter from Edgar and the bit of verse, scanty gleanings from a house so full of memories. Then he went out

and walked in the woods with time walking beside him. 

Tomorrow and tomorrow and tomorrow. Barren, sterile tomorrows treading the path ahead of him and turning blind faces back to his face. 

For what could he hope? That Brant would turn fairy godmother and give him Helen wrapped in stars?

That he might consent to take care of the children and give her freedom, without recrimination, without venom? That the lion with paws red on the fallen deer, would lose appetite and retire to the shadowy jungle that bore him? Scarcely likely. 

Or that he, Oliver Heron, miraculously bathed in success should step forward and play the conquering hero? Snatch Helen and the children and fly to Australia or Africa and live in a golden era?

He laughed, and the sound of his laughter was not good in his ears. Walking faster, and still not leaving his thoughts behind, he went farther in the wood than he had gone before, came out suddenly on a narrow path. An old wooden finger pointed ahead, the words unintelligible as the mutterings of an old man. 

Oliver mounted the slope and walked on. He was no judge of distance but in his mind he went far forward. 

He was conscious that the sun had come out and that it was an evening sun, low and intimate in a soft sky. 

This whole day now was with yesterday. He made a turn and came through a shadowy little copse to a clear place, an old cemetery such as one found on every road. 

Across the short grass, cropped by cows, he went, coming to the broken ranks of gravestones that leaned toward the ground, or lay flat in final resignation to the wind and rain.

Scarred and worn, the stone angels blew their persistent trumpets over soundless ears, and the grinning death's heads tilted over the fleshless skulls below. A squirrel scampered away from the nearest tomb, rolling a nut in a stuffed small mouth. Two white butterflies flickered, fragile and alive, in the still air. 

Oliver walked up and down, looking at the graves. 

Wild myrtle lifted a small purple flower from the sod, and a clump of woolly everlasting stood sturdily here and there. Most of the inscriptions were muted to a whisper by the grey lichen. A name here-a date thereand part of a verse. Higher up the slope, a few stones were better preserved, cut from more resistant stone, or protected by the slant of the land. Oliver lay flat on his stomach and deciphered the verse on a stone embellished with a very paunchy baby with some kind of wand in its hand. An inspired stone cutter had carved a wreath of bones above the baby, and the winged death's head was yet visible up to the neck in grass. 

Oliver read the verse. "Within the cold and silent tomb 

Lonesome and dark I find a room 

My husband and my children leave 

And take my lodging in the grave."

God in Heaven, what manner of husband paid for that stone? His mind sheered away from it. Lodging in the grave. There were a number of them here, mouldering under the grass while he walked alive with the butterflies and the squirrel. Why should he not lie there, too, his problems resolved by a definite end? He turned over,

and a shaft of sun struck the next stone. Half the name was on it, struggling above the lichen. Oliver. Oliver. 

A straggle of moss, and then, aged 27. He held his hand to let a shadow fall on the verse, which he made out with difficulty supplying the vanished letters. "Departed shade, forgot, unknown

Accept the tribute of this stone 

Tho' science fair enrich'd thy mind

With tender sympathies refined It nought avail'd death's cruel stroke 

The golden bowl of life has broke; 

Lost in manhood's early bloom 

Thy blossoms wither in the tomb." "You studying history?" 

Oliver's head jerked up. A brown old face was above him, ripe as a nut. 

"I seed you coming up here when I wuz taking my last hay load in. This old cemetery is on my prop'ty.' "I didn't mean to trespass." "That's all right as long as you ain't after the pheasants. Matter of fact, folks come up here every once and so often. I jest thought I'd come up to see you hadn't no gun. I don't allow huntin'." "I was just interested in the old graves." Oliver offered the old man a cigarette. "Thanks. I don't use them little things wropped in paper. Last person to come up here was a lady. She had a ancestor buried here and she wuz getting herself in the D.A.R., so she wanted to read off the tomb." He

chuckled. "Warn't a word on her'n. She felt bad, coming so far. Where you from? Cambridge?" "Yes." "That's a pretty verse, that there. And take my lodging in the grave. Kind of neat, ain't it? Nobody can make up pretty pomes like that nowadays. When my wife passed over yonder five year back, I had Mr. Pettigrew copy that there verse. She's buried up to Tranquillity in her family lot. She said she wasn't going to come out here and be grazed over by Bessie and Edna. I don't know as it matters much." "You don't know about this stone, do you? This 

Oliver stone?"

The old man bent to peer at it. "Yes I do. That's the 

Herne stone. That's the young feller hangin' in the museum at the head of the stairs-you been in there?" "Yes, I have. I saw it. I know the one you mean. 

There's no name on the portrait." "That's him. Tho' science fair enrich'd thy mind. He wuz the son of that Joanna and her Indian chief. So they said, anyway. And he went to England to a school somewhere. Science fair. That certainly is a good verse, ain't it? True to life. But he come back here and got in love with the minister's wife. His Indian blood didn't rightly get educated out of him.” "What happened?" Oliver heard the sharpness in his voice. "Well, nobody knows. The minister preached a sermon, they say, denouncing the serpent in the nest and young Oliver stood up in the meeting house-that's the old white one with the tower that kind of leans off,-and he said, 'You answer for your own sins, I'll attend

to mine!' But it turned out the Lord was on the side of the righteous because the very next day Oliver Herne was drowned in the river when his horse stumbled. The golden bowl of life has broke. Lost in manhood's early bloom. They put up just as good a stone for him as if he'd walked in the way of the Lord." "What happened to the minister's wife?" "She got prayed back to the fold." "Then the-Herne line died out, didn't it?”

The old man made a clucking sound. "No, it didn't. 

It come out young Oliver had a son by an English lady. I was a boy when he was brought over, but I don't remember that. The rest of the story was kind of country history, you might say, like the bloody massacre tales and all. There was always one of 'em. Up till the last. But I hear now the place is for sale. Some New 

York man is going to buy it. There's a man stopped. He peered with sudden interest at Oliver. He got out an old pipe and bit the stem. "Are you the feller that's staying over there?" "Yes." "He "Well, that's a nice piece of land." The old man started to walk away. "Hope you like it." 

Oliver cursed to himself. "I'm interested in whatever you can tell me about it." "Me? Oh, I don't know nothing. I mind my own business and never get in trouble with the real estate agents. Might want 'em to sell this old graveyard for me someday for a golf course!" He laughed.

Oliver accepted defeat with the best grace he could. 

He leaned against the stone marked Oliver, and watched the old man shuffle rapidly away.

* * Tessie did not come to the front door, and wait to be admitted, rather, as if falling into an old habit, she walked quietly into the kitchen and tied on her apron and began to make a fire in the old range. Oliver heard her. He was upstairs shaving. He called down. "Good morning, Tessie!" "Good morning." She came to the stair well and looked up. Always afterward when he thought of Tessie, he saw her looking up at him from the cellarway, from the barnfloor, up the stair. Oliver sharply.

He brought her a drink, and she got up, holding the glass firmly. "There isn't anything to tell," she said stubbornly. "Why should there be?" 

He made a gesture of despair. "I must know. I must!"

She seemed to consider the urgency in his tone. "How long are you going to stay in this house?" "Only until the deed is delivered and the doctor takes it. Why?" She tightened her lips. "Not today. It would be better if you would let it all alone, the way I said. You must be sure you have to know." "I am sure. I must know!"

Tessie went back downstairs without another word. 

He watched the firm broad shoulders and the wide back. 

When he heard her in the kitchen, he finished shaving. 

It seemed to him the face reflected was changed, the bones stood out sharply but there was something different around the mouth, a determined set, and he was getting brown. He moved to the little room where the faded battalion of soldiers marched along. He stood and stared for a long time, then he stepped in and laid his hand on the rockinghorse, disturbing the dust. His.

To come again, briefly, into his kingdom. His own place. Out of the void past, a little room with faded soldiers, a chair, and a rockinghorse.

He pushed his hand against the dappled neck and the horse moved, backward, forward, galloping from the darkness of yesterday to a glittering tomorrow. The curious stretched grin peculiar to such steeds and the solitary rolling eye were for Oliver to admire, and the rusted stirrups swung in the rhythm of a little boy's feet.

"Gid-dap," said Oliver softly.

Tessie had come back upstairs and was making cleaning sounds in the maple bedroom. Presently he would run in and find a woman in a blue dress whose name was Laurie. He would drag her back to watch him gallop so valiantly, waving his arms and shouting, not touching the reins, and then Tessie would come, too, and there was a puppy under the bed, a golden spaniel whose name was Moon.

Oliver went downstairs. There was nobody there. He and Tessie remained in the sad grown-up world and everything else was a dream.

Nothing more to be got from Tessie today, at any rate. He took a package of cigarettes and went out. It was a cold clear day, one of those summer days with the seed of Autumn ripening in it. The air blew fresh, cool as water and the sky had a scud of clouds. A little wet kitten, one of the endless tribe of the Pauls', crouched on a still damp stone, very bleak and cold, licking her chest.

Oliver swung down the road to the mailbox, there was the customary moment of excitement, the customary flattening of spirits. Nothing from Helen. A card from the doctor.

Oliver walked on to the village in the quick clear air. 

What a jade the weather was! A day like this promised life and hope in the very rapidity of the clouds, windy galleons on a blue, blue sea. Something good must comewell, some small good had come, he reminded himself.

Oliver walked down the street to the museum. The homely stir of living died away as he mounted the stair. 

The price of sugar, the death of a baby, war and the romance of a grocer's clerk, all faded. In the dusty stillness, he came to the landing and looked at the portrait of the young man with bitter eyes. Answer for your own sins, I'll attend to mine! Oh unregenerate son of the reckless Joanna and the dark savage, how much of your blood dried in your bones, how much was a heritage for future men? Did you sleep with her, the

timid little minister's wife? Did your passion burn away for a little, her morality? Nevertheless, she was prayed back to repentance. Did she burn the lock of fair hair hidden under her stays, and burn the memory of your kisses? Or did she, secretly, haunt the green glade, her heart tumultuous under grey merino? Did she lie beside her righteous lord, still lost?

Oliver, accustomed now all too well to ferret out bits of information, went over the cases, one by one, and was rewarded by an old curious pistol in the case of firearms, to which a paper called attention that it "supposed to belong to Oliver Herne, presented to the Green Mountain chapter by Diana and Oliver Heron, for loan exhibit."

Oliver hunted up the caretaker and asked to examine it. The case was unlocked, after some argument, and the pistol lay in his hand, feeling very much to his touch as it must have felt to Oliver Herne, with the exception of rust and dust. It was firm, tangible, a record. Oliver played with the idea that a bullet from it might well have gone home to the minister's heart. They might have run away, those two. Joanna's son should have known something of his boyhood. Enough to live in the wilderness. Well, it hardly mattered now. They were all dead. 

Nothing of them remained but Oliver's own mind harrying them from the silence.

He put the pistol back and with another long look at the young man on the landing, went out again and turned home. Did he have nothing to do but hover over ghosts? Better get back to the typewriter at least and see if another seventy five dollars was hidden in the keyboard, somewhere between asdf and vbnm. It would

keep him alive, keep him going, but as for being a writer -a popular creator of fiction-he laughed at the thought. He remembered he had had neither breakfast nor lunch, because of Tessie's arrival. No doubt the curious rejection of popular fiction in his stomach was partly hunger for food.

Poor old D. H. Lawrence, dividing man up in several selves and setting them at loggerheads to fight it out, should have been told that man is a unit. Let him be one piece of life, stomach and loins and brain and emotions a whole. Perhaps some surgeon someday could operate on the emotions, do an appendectomy worth doing, but so far, it seemed impossible. 

At the roadstand Oliver ate a hamburger sandwich and drank coffee. His stomach accepted the offering most gratefully. Perhaps man was fundamentally stomach. The maintenance of it was frightfully vital to civilization, in fact, made civilization. "How about a piece of home made pie?" asked the girl. "Thanks." He bit into rich juicy crust. "How do you like it up here?" "I never liked any place so much." "It's nice in winter too. When the snow is deep, we go tobogganing on Long Hill." "Well, winter's a long way off." "I don't know. Seems as if summer goes fast." "Yes, summer goes fast." "I always say after the first of August, it's winter before I can turn around."

He took the long way home. Tessie would be gone. 

He could go home and get through another day somehow. Split wood, or rake the yard. Build a fire and stand

before it drinking his last whiskey, feeling the hot fire on his legs, the hot liquor in his throat, and the intenser heat in his mind.

The thing was, he must give up hope. Think of her as Helen Carlyle, Brant's wife. Reduce the whole thing to a dream. The warmth of her presence had made an illusion, he had believed that she belonged to him because he loved her. 

Not to feel. That was the answer. To lose the heat, let the limbs go numb. Perhaps even in time the thought of her would no longer move him, nor desire drink up his blood. Even to longing, comes an end. 

The conflict of his disparate selves, brought him, worn and somber, to the old orchard and the meadow. Mr. Paul was cutting the meadow grass, the horses stood patient against the sky, the clouds went over, went over, and the bent figure of the man straightened suddenly with a forkful of hay. Oliver waved, and Mr. Paul waved back, and flicked at the horses with a whip. They moved on, in their slow rhythm and the clouds moved on in theirs and Oliver, in his absence of accord with a rhythm on land or in the sky, moved on also.

He came up to the front door and strode in taking his bitter desperate face from the light into the shadows. 

Helen sat by the fire, which burned red in the shadowy room. Her face was turned toward him, she did not move, her hands were clasped in front of her loosely, in the way she had, the firelight shone on her soft gold and dusky hair, and on her dress, which was blue. 

Oliver, stricken, gazed at her blindly. It was another trick, and he had died and should no longer be subject to tricks that drained the blood and consumed the bone.

Even as his eyes rejected the sight of her, she assailed his ears. "I've come, Oliver." 

His ears deceived him too.

Two glasses. Not one, but two. A glass to be held in her hand and lifted to her mouth. "I ought to die now," he thought, "now while I am chopping ice in this kitchen. Some kind God should send a bolt through me now."

Never mind. Never mind anything but that she was there, across the road, in his house. Not for an hour, not in a restaurant, not in a crowded drugstore. Alone with the fire-and the Waterbury American, Helen with hair the color of autumn leaves and quiet eyes and-and Helen! 

He ran back, cradling the sweet smelling tomatoes in his hands. They were still warm from the sun, and dusted with earth, the lettuce leaves had a faint smell too, not like the city lettuce. She was still there and everything in the room was yet alive with her presence. 

He got supper, cooking like a man with a great clashing of pans and of sizzling skillets. Every dish was soiled when he finished and plopped hurriedly in the sink. With all the ardour of a cave man killing a lion for his mate, Oliver wrestled with the veal chops, and sliced the tomatoes.

And she was still there when he brought the little table in and set it by the fire. Two plates furnished forth with broiled tomatoes, garden lettuce, fat irregular chops, and several slices of very startled bacon popped

into a pan smoking hot, and then forgotten while the coffee steamed. "It isn't much," he said, wiping his damp forehead.

In the windless evening, they went outside and sat on the grass, while the fireflies brightened over the meadows and a casual star slipped over the appletrees. It was warm again, the air mellow as a ripened field. The earth breathed. Locusts made a constant chattering sound, reedy and shrill. The sound of summer, Helen said. 

It was an hour to be at peace. They said little, not to break the stillness. As the dark came over them, the cool lonely note of frogs came from the swamps. All the night creatures woke to their little lives with rustlings and cheepings and soft flutings. 

The stars hung low and the moon, lotus-covered, just tipped the orchard with growing luster. 

They smoked, their cigarettes glowing in the dark. "I wish I could tell you what you are to me," said Oliver. "I almost know." "I would try, but it sounds unreal in words. Yet there must be words, there must be speech, that's part of it.

"Or tease me, if you will. You see I don't even know myself, much less you. I feel as if I were walking around in myself as one walks in a strange wood. I don't know what lives here!" "I'll never know all of you! It's odd how the hunger grows and grows. An hour ago, it was enough to kiss you, to hear you. Now I torture myself thinking how to follow the flight of your thoughts." I mean-what has been in your lifecan you forget that part? I couldn't have you, except willingly." "I was afraid. There is, I think in women, a kind of fear of being made so vulnerable to suffering. All the way up today I looked out of the window and thought

how afraid I was. And when I saw you, Oliver, when I saw you, I only loved you. There isn't room for anything else in me now."

Leaning against him, she walked to the house, serene, timeless, the figure of love itself passing over the moonbright grass toward an immortal consummation with life. Oliver shut out the night. This was no hasty passion, no rapid hour, this was the ultimate meaning. It was his house and she was his, and for the hours until dawn, he would be untroubled by any ghost of her past or his. No spectre of the future should affright him. 

In the bedroom, very simply, she let down her short hair. Her movements were slow, child-like, and her eyes were deep and dreamy. When he buried his face in her hair, she relaxed against him. "Do I make you happy, Oliver?" 

Speechless, he found her mouth with his. 

All beauty, all ecstasy, yes, and all pain, the remembrance of every star and the sound of all music, the sterility of a life without her and the unbearable sweetness of her breasts, all these and more became the blood in his veins, the marrow of his bones. As his body found hers in the moonlit darkness, he heard the thunder of his heart and knew death was helpless to still it in that hour.

* * ¶ In the first grey light, Oliver knew another change. 

He couldn't, any longer, challenge death so proudly with the dawn invading the room and the knowledge cold in his mind that the night was over, nothing had stayed it. For a moment it all seemed incredible. Had he actually possessed her? Where was the secret rapture? When she woke, how would she look at him? 

Would she, perhaps, turn away? Passion was, he thought, a violation of some stern self sitting in the mind. Was it fundamentally decent to give oneself to such rapture? It made one so vulnerable to suffering, later. 

He touched her. This warm glowing flesh was tangible in his hand. Her hair, tumbled on the pillow, took a rich amber light into itself, her closed eyes gave her face a child-like look. Her eye-lids were very lovely and the lashes, even on her cheek, made a darkness of their own. 223

Lovers all over the world assailed by dawn must be turning like this and looking, in the aloof hour of waking after rapture. Night was a dream. His body and hers, sundered by sleep, were no longer one white fire.

The thunder in his blood slept, too, and he had a childish desire to wake her and be assured that she had loved him, loved him still. "I wish you didn't have to sleep, darling," he said softly.

The dawn brightened. He could see, by bending his head, the rising mist on the meadow, silversoft and strange. The whole world was veiled, or rather, not veiled, submersed, flooded with a silver sea, that brimmed the hollows level and lapped without sound at the breasts of the hills. The sky was the color of a shell, very quiet.

Reaching stealthily to the table, Oliver took a cigarette and lying on his back, smoked thoughtfully. 

Helen's sleep was a sanctuary. He could not enter it. 

When she woke, they could begin again this business of reaching for each other, wanting, probing, feeling. 

But now, withdrawn in sleep, she was not his. He thought about it, lying beside her while her soft untroubled breathing assured him she was still there. 

Love and passion. Mating. It was both the thing that lifted man to divinity and that unified him with all animal life. The actual physical union of two bodies was a life process engendered by nature, in so far as it was a matter of nerves and the pounding of blood and the submission of the body to an elemental and instinctive sensation. And yet what was that unless the mind gave too?

He lit another cigarette. How close she was! The warmth of her sleeping body came to his, the soft fragrance of her hair. He was tempted to wake her at once to be sure she knew he loved her. 

There couldn't be complete love without passion, he decided, knocking the ash from his cigarette. You couldn't have a sound house without timbers. Deny the blind urge of two bodies to fuse and love was a pallid wraith, thin, unsubstantial. Because the physical had a meaning that couldn't be ignored. The men who denied that were as far off the track as the sensualists. 

Love was nothing much, if not everything. He might have loved Helen, it was true, and never known her tangible self, but it would have been the ghost of love. 

With last night, with live breathing passion, came reality.

Oh God, to be able to think it through clearly, to know, cried Oliver in himself. To say it, the whole of it, to her when she stirs and comes again from the fields of sleep. Another cigarette. Turn and watch the mist leveling the hollows. 

Go back to passion. That was a word to mean many things. Already he knew it as two selves, a coin with a rose on the face and a panther on the reverse. He held the coin in his hand. His imagination, long starved, glutted itself on dreams. With Helen, he might take a long journey into worlds of the mind. What did he mean by that? His thoughts were full of images. 

He would love her with a love like rich flaming flowers with long secret leaves unfolding in hot Eastern moonlight. Closed flowers shaped like her soft secret breasts, and moonlight white and hot over a world

heavy with scent. There was the sound of a fountain, a warm glimmering fountain, and music too, in it, but whether the music was only the beating of pulses, he could not say. He could see all whiteness and all desire walking in Helen and his own madness on quiet feet following. A black panther leaping in the night, crushing the flowers, drinking the blood of their stems and with his sleek coat dripping with sweet odors and with warm blood. 

Yes, he would love her like that-if he had her. "I'm glad she doesn't know how mad I am," thought

Oliver, with lazy satisfaction. "I wake up, rejecting the flesh-not wishing to be vulnerable. But when the mists come in, I am already in Asia or Ethiopia-licking my paws! God help me, I really am mad. But perhaps all of us are, and hide the madness." 

He crushed out the cigarette. He also would love her-she made a sweet little sound, a breath of sound.

Her hair was softer than a dream, and close to his hand, tumbled on the pillow, the color of sunlit leaves in October. Her mouth was the mouth of a child. 

He would love her like a lily. He saw her, no longer naked, but sweetly clad in flowered silk, sitting in a conventional garden, with a small puppy at her knees. 

Soft and gentle, his passion came with tenderness. His mouth was still, against her palm. All the quiet lavender and mignonette and stock and gillyflowers made a sunny peace in the garden. Very quiet, and in an aching tenderness he loved her with a bright open-air serenity. "Are you awake, darling?” her voice was drowsy, her

eyes smiled. They were the color of the mist now, where it darkened at the wood's edge. 

He kissed her. Dreams fled away, fantasy died. Here was his woman, warm with sleep. He tangled his fingers in her hair and kissed her sleepy mouth, her throat, and the warm hollow between her breasts. "Do you love me in the morning?" he asked. "I was dreaming," she said. "I couldn't find you,

Oliver. I walked and walked, and then I ran, and it was very dark. You were gone." "You mustn't dream such dreams. Wake up, darling. 

I'm too lonely." "But I'm right here!" She laughed, "Even on your pillow, instead of mine!" "No, you aren't here when you are asleep. You're away so far I can't shout at you." "I'm very hungry." coffee. Did she really love him like that? Or did she, after the manner of women, conceal the dark thoughts behind her serene manner?

The shift and variety of feeling, the bitter hunger, and the impact of body against body-who has told it? Or am I perhaps, queer? Decidedly queer, grinned Oliver, flopping the toast. Here I am, with no future and a barren past, with disaster upon my head, trying to carry a tray of food to the woman I love, and all that matters is that the bacon be unburnt and that I love her as she desires!

Helen was sitting up in bed, her hair tumbled, her eyes dreamy. She was smoking. A little dab of fallen ash lay on her pajamas and she leaned over to blow it off. "What are you thinking?" There never could be a mouth as desirable as hers. 

He was certain of it. The expression of a face was not contained in the cut of the features, it was intangible.

Their two faces, side by side in the mirror, looked out at them. His face was thinner than ever, the blue eyes darker, the fair hair bleached by the country sun to a winy yellow. They left the house and walked through the fields. 

The day was the very soul of summer, summer ripe and not yet falling from the lavish bough of August. The air was voluptuous, the clouds dreamed in a lazy sky, and all the little insects made a reedy background of noise to intensify the stillness. 

Where they walked, butterflies white and blue, flickered over the tawny fields, sudden grasshoppers in the cut hay, and bees sensuous with pollen, lived their little

day. The melancholy full note of a locust sounded, a distant hound dog bayed.

The hills of Connecticut stood deep with haze, blue and still. The trees in the nearer foreground were heavy and full, apples brightened in the sloping orchards. 

When they came out to the road, there was bittersweet faintly orange over the fences, goldenrod and elderberry, and a riot of lesser flowers, little nameless starry things in blue and gold, open to the sun.

It was as if there never would be any tomorrow. 

Today was the sum of growth, the fertile earth giving up her fulness to the sun, every leaf instinct with brief immortality. And over all, the dreamy rich haze of late summer.

Under an abandoned appletree they sat down. Helen leaned against the worn trunk, turning an apple in her fine fingers and smiling faintly. The sun made her hair the color of autumn leaves, her eyes were dreamy, still heavy-lidded from the night's fulfillment. Her mouth was soft. She looked down at the apple, turning it and turning it again. "It fell too soon," she said.

There was a little silence. Oliver stretched his long legs in the deep grass, crushing some wild purple flowers.

A storm was coming up, a quick August storm. Before they could get home, it broke with a surge of thunder and a jagged lightning stroke. The rain fell. 

The clouds darkened the sky, the rain was so heavy the hills vanished, leaving a wall of rain.

Oliver had a wild notion they had died, their bodies were propped against the dresser, but life had gone. "Please, Oliver," she said in that still voice, "if you love me, help me now." "If I love you!" He couldn't tell whether he shouted the words or whispered. He turned away and plunged from the room. The hall was dark. Who was he?

Judas. That was the man. Helen wanted him to be 

Judas. Betray love. 

Thirty pieces of silver.

He looked back. Helen stood motionless, her hands on the dresser. That rigid figure had no part in the woman he loved, the soft and passionate woman. She was turning again to stone to face Brant. "Judas!" he muttered thickly to himself, "Judas!"

The stairs were a deep well. Cold air came up to him. 

His head cleared. It was, for an instant, too clear. "The price we are paying tonight," he said suddenly, "is too high." 

No, he wouldn't be sold. He heard Gilda downstairs.

If she hadn't meddled-there'd be an end to lies. Better if Brant burst in and found Helen in his arms!

He felt the blood rise in a sullen tide. He couldn't be stopped! Let Brant come! Let him come at once. 

Oliver was ready.

The stairs seemed to fall away beneath him, he caught the rail, scraping the skin from his knuckles. 

Gilda was in the kitchen making a loud noise with glasses and bottles. "Come here," she said.

Oliver lunged out with the groceries. The rain thinned, he had no idea what time it was, but a late stain of red was splashed over Tranquillity Hill. By the time he turned into the yard the rain was mist, thick and difficult to the lungs. The house looked so peaceful, rising in the mist that he stood still. Had he dreamed? Was Helen waiting for him, sitting alone by the applewood fire, her hands clasped and her eyes

dreamy? Then he heard a burst of laughter and Gilda's voice. "How do you do," he said, walking into the livingroom. 

Faces and forms grew out of the mist in his head. 

The doctor sipping a highball. Elizabeth, very prim, rocking back and forth in the rocking chair. Joan wiping tears from her eyes with Helen's handkerchief. 

Brant, his eyes sharp and suspicious on Helen. Helen, very composed, a figure of ice. "Well, Heron, it seems you entertained some angels unawares," said Brant with a steely smile. “Youweren't expecting company, were you?"

You know, Brant, I can't make out what's got into you."

Helen's eyes met his. He couldn't withstand the look.
