Oliver got up the stairs and ran the hot water. Bathing and shaving and eating and dressing, these things went on, no matter what desolation walked within. The thing to do would be to scrape out the inside of a man, empty it like a gourd, let the outside go on. His head was full of hornets, he doused it with cold water, then got in the hot tubful. Oliver looked at himself in the mirror. His leaden face sneered at him, the mouth twisted in a savage grin. "You goddamned imbecile," he said between his teeth, "what gave you the idea she was yours?"

He sat on the top step, lighting a cigarette. He felt suddenly quiet. If his heart beat, it beat under water.

That woman down there wasn't his. Not so much as the shadow of her that fell across the fire. If she had beenwell, that was another thing. Pull yourself together, Oliver, and come downstairs and eat meat loaf and fried onions while Brant makes clever remarks about what the dinner lacks. Just an anchovy here-a touch of garlic rubbed on the bowl-a mere suggestion of mustard rubbed over the loaf.

In the blurred stillness of his mind, he was conscious of the dinner, of Joan's spilled plate, of Elizabeth's smug table manners. Mercifully any acute perception was ab-

sent. He didn't care. Ah, it was good to feel a paralysis creep up into his thighs and wind around his heart in cold tendrils.

The doctor reckoned the score, paid Brant what he owed. Gilda trailed out. "Call me in the morning, Oliver, and I'll fix an omelet."

The doctor wound his watch. "Time to hit the hay for me, too." 

Brant stood up. "Good night." He took Helen's arm. "Come along, we'll want to be up early in the morning." 

Oliver waited for her to speak. Surely it was imperative now for her to speak the truth. This couldn't go on. A fierce and quiet triumph filled him. She was his, the sense of their union flowed through him. "Come on," Brant said.

Helen stood up. She spoke. Her eyes looked past 

Oliver, her mouth twisted in a curious smile. "Good night, Oliver," she said. 

She went out with Brant. 

Oliver stumbled to the kitchen. He heard a door close upstairs. 

His blind hand found the whisky bottle. He poured it down his throat as fast as he could swallow. He drank it like water, all there was in the bottle, sending the fire down to his body. When his stomach revolted, he waited an instant, then drank again. He dropped the empty bottle.

Under his gripping hands, the worn drainboard splintered. A sluggish black swamp sucked his legs, he was sick, his stomach rejected the dulling of the whisky but he kept it down.

She accepted this. Dear God, she had climbed the stairs without screaming. Covering the lie of their life. Helen, what have you done to me, he cried. Helen, do you know what you have done? 

Prayed back to the fold, said Oliver, leaning drunk against the sink.

Drip, drip, went the faucets. A drop at a time, water fell to the drain. Every drop measured so much time that would not be again. 

All right, spill it! He turned both faucets on full head. Water rushed to the sink. One drunken hand shut them off again. Drop, drop, drop, they began. 

Do away with cab horses. Get along in streamlined limousines. Let all horses rot in beds of quicklime, bloated bellies thick with flies. Hard metal, this is the age of hard metal. Horses have beating hearts.

"Oliver, where are you?" She was there, her pale face glimmering in the dark. "Here," he said dully. 

She put out her hands. "I can't help it," she said. "He's put it on the basis of sleeping with him tonight or he'll know the whole thing! What can I say? What can I do?" 

A thin sound answered her. "He suspects something and he's simply mad with jealousy. You know how little I can say!" She beat her hands together. "Oliver, it won't be this way again!" 

He looked at her. "No, it won't," he said slowly. "Ah, you hurt me so, Oliver!" "I'm sorry. I don't mean to." His voice was dead. "Doesn't it mean anything that the real me is yours, only yours?" 

He tried to laugh, the sound bubbled in his throat. "Mine," he said. "Just what can that mean?"

She drew back as if he had struck her. Then she threw back her head, her hands at her throat. "Oh you!" she cried desperately, "you'll kill me! 

You'll kill me, between you! You're tearing me to pieces, you and Brant. What do you know of what I feel? Yes, you're as bad as he is!" She took a wild step toward him, her pale wild face strained through the darkness. "If I endure this for another night, it's all I can bear. But you strike at my soul!" 

He looked at her, saying nothing. "I hate you both," said Helen. Suddenly she sobbed, her hands over her face. 

From above came a voice. "Helen! Helen!"

She was in his arms, her face hard against him. "Kiss me, Oliver! Oh-darling "God!" said Oliver.

Their bodies crushed together, his cold mouth found her wet trembling one. Her shaken breath sounded loud.

She pulled away, stumbled to the door. She ran. At the stairs, she paused, then climbed with slower, labored steps.

A door closed. Opening the kitchen door, Oliver flung himself into the rainy darkness. 

His feet found the earth. 

Toward morning, when his limbs sagged under him he sat down on a rotting log in the meadow. Grey light crawled up the sky. The clouds were going, with a slow purposeful swing, they swept in beaten battalions from the sun. The birds came out, a drenched robin hopped after a secret worm. When he found it, he set his little beak in the worm and pulled, leaning back in a ridiculous fashion. It ended with the parting of the worm. The amazed robin fell back, then hastily made off with the piece he had riven from the whole. 

The night was over. There never would be a night like it. Only once ultimate death drank a man's veins. She could lie in his arms one night in rapture of Godhead, and go to Brant's bed the next, she could do that. 

Women were like that, they could accept any necessity with a fatal passiveness. 

He had lived too long.

He found an abandoned barn crumbling at the road's edge and crept in, lying down in the hay, stretching his numb legs on musty dead grasses. He dozed, he slept a

thick feverish sleep. His watch had stopped, and when he woke, he thought it must be late.

His wooden legs carried him stiffly to the house, which was empty. They had gone somewhere. There was a note, in Gilda's writing on the table. 

We've gone to the Berkshires-or somewhere else -for a look at the scenery. Coffee is made. Bacon all gone. Gilda.

He set the percolator on the stove. Once more it was day. Time to wash and shave and put on a clean shirt.

His face was rough. Sometimes hair grew on dead bodies, he had read that somewhere. They dug Lincoln up some thirty years after he was buried.

The face had darkened, and there was a spot of mould on the black wing tie. Oliver could see him, neat in the coffin with the growing mould alive there. Good job these undertakers make. What for? Dump the flesh in a box and let it rot, the quicker the better. At funerals, they hovered like buzzards, smiling and rubbing their hands and saying, "Doesn't he look natural?" 

Funny business. Funny world.

He drank three cups of coffee, muddy from standing in the pot. How long ago had he carried her breakfast to her, where she lay warm and serene? They had laughed. 

It was a good thing they had laughed. 

The thing to remember was hope. Something would happen. Brant might die. Helen would be free then, in spite of herself. You couldn't compromise with a corpse. 

And the children would grow up. His happiness and

Helen's might not always be turned under so two new lives could grow vigorous.

He went upstairs, his knee joints creaked from being wet all night. His head was light. His hands were a long way off at the end of knobbed sticks. He flexed the fingers.

In the narrow hall, he stood and stared through an open door. An unmade bed. The white intimacy of spread sheets and tossed blankets. On one side Helen's pajamas. On the other, Brant's, a leg trailing limply off. On the dresser, Brant's brushes were wedded to her slim ivory comb. The imprint of a masculine thumb pressed the toothpaste, a soapy snake had oozed on the cover.

Day spread its obvious light over everything. There was a button missing on Brant's pajama coat. Sew it on, Helen. Yes, dear.

He moved with effort toward his own room, lifting his feet with care, setting them down with difficulty. 

There is something the matter with me, thought 

Oliver coolly. Something gravely wrong in my head.

There is undoubtedly something growing in there, a dark, twisting vine. Blood-red flowers pressing behind the eye-balls. Tendrils, cold and strong push down in my lungs, running against the membrane.

I'll put an end to it. This has something to do with blue dishes. And Laurie said, "you can't burn the memory-you can't burn" No, you couldn't attack the material things. Only this dark vine. Tear it out and let the blackened juice drip in the heart. Never mind the pajamas, tear them up and they can be used for dust cloths.

Flesh is more resistant. But it yields. It yields. Come back, Helen, I want to talk to you. Before the snaky vine throttles the pulses in my wrists. Come back, Helen, you have a rendezvous. I'll call you, not with a brandied voice, not with a wedding ring-come back, Helen!

Stay with me, Helen, if not above the ground, then beneath it, at the dark and secret roots. 

The other coat must be dry. Get it on. Think of a grown man wandering all night in the rain for desire's sake! The shoulders are tight. Well, the vine takes up room, it grows so fast. Even in my ears, the damned thing pushes its shoots. The sap makes a hard sound. 

What's this manilla envelope? The abstract! How far back does it go? Take a look, there's plenty of time. 

Get out of this room, sit on the maple bed and open the thing. Perfectly sane, why not? There's her name. There it is!

Diana G. Heron, of Cambridge, Connecticut.

He must be mad. Or they were mad. People didn't toss property around like a game of battledore and shuttlecock. What did it signify?

All right, he gave it back. Oliver got up and went to the bathroom and put his head under the tap. Cold water trickled down his face. She gave it to him, and he gave it to her, and then-in God's name what story lay buried in this legal paper?

The papers fell to the floor. There was a horrid unnatural sound in the room. Oliver got up to see what it was. It was his breathing. His lungs were thick with warm blood, the air couldn't get through. He opened his mouth, dry and full of sand, and forced more air in. His hands made a weaving motion, fanning a stir of stale air. When he went to the window, he waded in a cold salty tide that sucked at his knees. There was no air outside either. He stumbled and eased himself to the floor. A splinter in the wide boards scratched his cheek, a thin cool trickle of blood made its way to his mouth. 

He licked it off. There was an old wide-spread stain dark in the grain of the wood, somebody had spilled something over the edge of the bed. 

A fly buzzed, landed on his forehead, trailing its legs in the cold sweat.

Laurie, I have something to say to you. Laurie, I want to talk to you 

Shot and killed his wife, and then shot himself. And the baby in her womb. That was my father. That was my mother. He loved her and he killed her. She was going to leave him, as Helen-no, never mind that. He burned the blue dishes.

Diana Heron am and have always been the ownercurious legal lie. Who ever was and had been the owner of anything? Lease for one year. No lease on life went

with that page. A warranty deed to death, made out, sealed with the witnesses. 

His mind fumbled back to the past. A little boy. 

A woman in a blue dress walking in the meadow. Rockinghorse. Oh eternal wooden horse, rocking away while a man kills his wife and a little boy goes away to school. 

What day was this? Where was he? What time was it? Better get away and think. This house was haunted. 

Not the dead, but the living, well, the dead and the living. No place for him now, who knew not whether he lived or died.

He got up and stood erect. The curtains swayed in a little wind, a fresh wind, cooler than summer, a wind tainted already with the frost. Summer was over, Autumn walked in the air. 

Oliver got down the stairs and went out to the woods.

* * ¶ His feet took him to the place he and Helen had been, a lifetime ago. The grass was yet crushed a trifle, the brown daisy stalks broken where she had sat. He sat there now, his head on his knees, and his mouth pinched in. There was no blood in him, he felt his bloodlessness. He sat and looked at the ground while sight continued to register the small things it fed on. 

There was a tiny close-reefed mullein, with furred leaves. It was ready for winter, resistant, secure. 

Far off, a train whistled and his ears took in sound. 

A minute puff of smoke blew in the air. People were travelling on that train, live people with bags and wraps and destinations. Ah, that was it, they were bound somewhere! He licked his lips.

The air was as bright as if it had been varnished. A new clump of nameless little flowers had come up, in- 270

sisting on growth, on flowering, on seeding. Life was very strong, it took a lot to snuff it out. 

Bullets could do it. Laurie had ordered flowers, perennials, and they had never come to bloom, but one of those dark vines like the vine in him had flowered and borne fruit.

His father, that was his father. He, Oliver, was his father's son. Was he not part of Laurie also? This devouring love for Helen, it was a child of an earlier passion, born of travail, born of his father and Laurie and their passion. Out of the broken body of a woman and the imperative loins of a madman had come this 

Oliver Heron with his love, his devouring love. 

Was his father mad? An hour ago-centuries ago, had he not suffered the same madness? He had to remember whose child he was, now that he knew. 

He had to check up on his heritage. 

A white butterfly flickered past on swansdown wings. 

Little lovely thing, born to die in an hour. Its wings beat, and beat again, the light air supported it. It had life. It had the same inexplicable thing that beat in 

Laurie once. Where did dead butterflies go? This one was so alive. Movement, beating, beating in the air. 

But the shot jackrabbit twitched no longer. Its eyes glazed, the limbs stiffened, blood clotted on soft fur, it was dead. Laurie's eyes glazed also. Blood clotting on her white breast. The moving life within her slowly suffocating in the tide of her dark spilled blood. 

His hands were numb. He beat them together with a kind of mechanical anxiety until blood stung them again. His father had seen her lying there before he died. Certainly it was a death of passion. He took them

both out of life under some mad compulsion which meant love, in its own way.

An ant crawled up his shoe. He saw it laboring over the difficult slope. He shook it off and watched it stagger away, waving its feelers in a wild surprise. Not for him to crush it. No, not for him, crawling like an ant up his own black and inaccessible hill. 

Dimly he was aware that time passed. The shadows moved over him. His own shadow fell crookedly on the mullein, and he shifted his position, not wishing to shut the light from anything so sturdy and growing. 

Life was a succession of moments. It hadn't any flowing continuity. But it moved on to something, and he must consider what it moved on to. Nothing would ever be the same again for him, he had come fully into his heritage, he was a man, and he must act accordingly.

He looked up. Helen was running over the grass, her smooth long limbs flying, her hair blown. "Oh, Oliver, I was frightened!" 

He looked up, his eyes stone. "Where's Brant?" "I sent him back to the village to do errands. We have an hour. Darling, where have you been?"

He laughed. The laugh changed to a dry hard sob. 

He rose to his knees, pulling her down. "Oh, Oliver-not here-not now-wait 

He fastened his hands in her hair. He smelled her hair. He buried his face in it. The strong fine strands stung his eyeballs. He kept his eyes open in the darkness of her hair. "Helen! Helen!" "Darling-not here —

Her body pressed down the short grass, the new grass after the first hay had gone. His hands went, hungry, over her throat, he pressed her breasts, he moved his mouth until it found hers, he tasted in his dry and sandy mouth the sweet wet salty tears from her eyes. He drank her tears. He drank the breath from her mouth. "Oliver-they will find us!" her pupils were distended, her cheeks burned.

There was a thicket, a little removed. He got up, and pulled her to it. His hands clamped on her flesh like steel. He was strong, stronger than death or life. He pulled her down, crushed her down. Her body now lay in a softer grass, under the thicket. A green stain from broken blades of grass colored her white dress. A white virginal silk dress. 

He bent above her. "Did he touch you?" "No. No. Never again. I swear it." "But you won't-you can't go away from menow?" 

She made a pitiful sound.

His body came down to hers. She was alive, there was no clotted blood in her. Her breath warmed him. 

Her mouth was immortal. "Oliver-let me breathe!" "Yes, my darling, breathe."

She moved a little. Her hair spread over a clump of myrtle purple flowers, tiny and intricate shone between the strands. Oliver plucked one, dropped it.

She spread out her skirt, smoothed her belt. "Oh darling," she said helplessly.

"You'd better go back." His voice trembled. “I'llcome along presently." "The doctor says Switzerland isn't necessary for 

Joan," she said breathlessly. "No?" "No. I'm only going to take her West-just for the hard months. Two or three. Arizona. That's not so bad, is it?" "Not nearly. You'll enjoy Arizona." He twisted up a handful of grass. "And then when I come back, I'm sure Brant will let me go there'll be a settlement. You see if he gets the idea gradually" "There's a great deal to be said for getting used to things gradually. Compromise is the thing." He smiled, an odd dry smile. "I'm so glad you understand. I was afraid—_—_—___” "Don't be afraid," he said gently. "I understand a good many things I never thought I should." He flung the grass away. "What do you mean?" "Nothing for you to worry your head over. You," said Oliver, "attend to your sins and I'll handle mine."

She stood up, tall, pale, lovely in the clear sunlight. 

The line of her wrists as she moved her hands to fasten her blouse, was achingly lovely. The clear level eyes were no longer chalcedony. There were lavender shadows under them, they were the color of lake water on a dark day, a grey, greyer than grey. Her mouth was soft and beautiful, and the shadowy smile was a place for beauty to be lost in. There was a little quick pulse in her throat, the tide of passion receding

left the beat of waves on the shore. Her bosom moved lightly, evenly, but rapid with the quick breath. A man could drown in the sweetness of her. Sink and be lost, and let the clenched hands be lax forever. A man could die for an hour with beauty. She was the meaning of life, all little rivers running swift in the sun, all appleblossoms leaning in spring air, all ripe fruit heavy on the bough. 

She was Helen. "Will you come in soon? We didn't know__” "I'm going to talk to Tessie," said Oliver. "I was just out and around," he added vaguely. "But you're all right?” "Yes, all right." 

She hesitated, ready to go, reluctant. "I-hate to leave you." 

He spread his hands. "You'd better be engaged in a domestic occupation. Reading to Joan." "You will come in-soon?" "Why not?"

She took his hand and laid her mouth on the palm. "I love you," she said softly. 

He watched her go, his eyes narrowed, his lips still.

He lost no least footfall on the grass. She walked like beauty. With her head up, and her hands quiet. The sun went with her.

Oliver stood above the place where they had lain. 

The grass was flat. Another rain would bring it up again, vigorous as ever, the blades erect, the roots secure. Winter would kill it, but spring would be a renascence of green, soft and subtle. What a good thing grass was, covering the bare bones of earth. Grass

endured. It had strength in it. Even bullets wouldn't strangle it. A man could shoot into the earth all day and the earth would receive the hot metal, and keep on growing grass, and that was a good thing. 

He knelt and put his face against the crushed grass. 

Either it was still warm from the heat of their love, or he dreamed it was. It didn't really matter, either. 

Vaguely he wondered what time it was. He took out his useless timepiece and looked at the dial as if he had never seen a stopped watch before. Acting on a sudden impulse, he scooped a little hole in the earth and thrust the watch in, covered it with broken sod and stamped it down. Let it lie there and rust. It marked the end of an epoch.

He smoked a cigarette, waiting for Helen to get out of sight. Her figure, diminished like a figure in porcelain, vanished in the green distance. Then he followed, walking evenly, his legs making long shadows behind him. Tessie could tell him what time it was. In any case, there was no hurry.

He came to the Pauls' place and crossed the yard. 

Tessie was inside, in the hot kitchen, counting eggs. "Hello, Tessie," said Oliver, "come outside, will you? 

I want to speak with you. It's important."

Lennie.' 

It was a world of smoke and warmth and the steam of bodies. Lennie, stripped to the waist, had his head buried in soapy water. The muscles stood out on his arms and chest, honest muscles rolling under sunned flesh. Mamma Paul, with a glass of sour cider, waited for Lennie to give up the sink so she could wash the milk bottles, in the same pan. Tessie, who had been out in the world, wore a clean print thing cut on a magazine pattern. She wrapped the plump hen she had plucked in a ragged flour sack, pushed Lennie aside while she washed her firm hands. Lennie threw a blob of soap at her. "You Tessie!" he said.

The sense of vigorous life overflowed the room, even 277

the kitten poking a damp nose up through a hole in the floor, was compact with life. Oliver, in his curious state, estranged from life, wished he could plunge in it, get a peasant strength for existence. The world would go on, when he and his like were vanished, by means of people such as these. 

He smiled at Mamma Paul; her warm steamy face set with the bright eyes widened generously in return. "Nize day, I think like rain is gone," she offered as her bit of small talk. He found it difficult to begin. Tessie did not help him, she sat very still, looking across the yard at the harvested field above the barn. She smiled. Her lips moved a little, she was saying something to herself. He couldn't penetrate her silence this way. 

He got up, lit a cigarette, and faced her standing. "Tessie, you have to tell me," he said. "I don't mean to argue with you, and I don't mean to be put off any more. There are some things that have come to me, that 

I shall be doing something about. And so" he sat down again and leaned forward, his gaze hard on her eyes. "You mean you-well, as to that, I know about you and her. She told me," Tessie said.

A scattering of chickens fled suddenly across the grass, small important bodies the color of unripe peaches. An old turkey mounted the back fence and bobbled there in an absurd manner, defying the world with a hoarse noise. A cow, heavy with calf, came to the edge of the yard and mooed. "Tessie, why did my father murder my mother?" "Ah," said Tessie. 

She stared away from him at the hill. One hand smoothed her dress, gathered it in a fold, smoothed it again. "So that's the way it is," she said to herself. 

Oliver lit a cigarette. He sat very still, blowing the smoke softly into soft air. "He was drunk," she said. "Tessie, men get drunk often. It doesn't follow that they shoot their wives. So why was it?" "Nobody will ever know." "What do you think? You must think something." "All I know is she was thinking of leaving him.”

"Was she going away with the other man?" "Oh, no. Oh, no." Tessie shook her head. "There was something—” "What was it?" 

Tessie spread her hands. "They weren't like other people," she said. "He was jealous, anyway." "Yes. He had a fear of her going. Maybe he was too crazy about her. You see," she struggled for words, "they weren't settled people. Not settled people." 

Oliver considered that. "He was queer. He did things. Not like most men.” "What?" "Oh—just different," said Tessie vaguely. "So he went off sometimes and got drunk. She used to come over for Papa to find him. Papa went off and came back sometimes two-three o'clock." 

Oliver sighed. "She said once she might go away. But she wouldn't.

The matter with her was she wasn't afraid," Tessie went on. "She wasn't afraid."

There was a long pause. A door slammed across the road. Voices came out, died away. Oliver waited. He had his teeth in it now. He held himself still. April. He had Papa to put in some new fence posts that morning. She was ordering some flowers. We had dinner early so he could go to town. We had chicken and peas and mashed potatoes and dried apple pie and some funny kind of cheese, running all over the plate.

Running through wet grass, crying. So that was the way it was. He shut his teeth on it. "They couldn't ever get off the blood," said Tessie. 

He saw the dark stain. Blood soaked in, the grain of the wood took it up. That was how it was. "Thank you, Tessie," he said, getting down off the well house. He held out his hand. "I really had to know." "People shouldn't know too much." "That's as may be. It's been-good for me."

He went away, crossing the road slowly. It was in his mind, it filled his mind. He had no way of analyzing the things in him. Not for a long time. The phantasy

of the vine, cut at the root, withered. The pattern of emotions, born in him, realized and looked at, was a thing to free him.

Laurie had courage. The rest was trivial compared to that. Somewhere in him survived that courage, or a part of it. Love destroyed her, might have destroyed him.

He looked up at the house. The doctor was in the side yard, smoking a thin cigar. Oliver went to him and pulled out the abstract. "I wonder if I ought to buy this place," said the doctor, thoughtfully. "By all means," answered Oliver. "It's high time you took it. It's got strong timbers." Oliver leaned forward, trying to see her face, the long lovely curve of throat, the shadowy still hands.

Difficult to speak. So many dark leaves had been torn down in his mind, it stood gaunt, bare, but there was solidity in it. "What are you thinking?" "About you and me, Helen. What life does to us.

Oh God, to work! And find Helen.

Life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness. What a vision for a dreamer! "Oliver, you won't give up hope?" "Why should you love me, Helen? After all, what am I?" Words strove for utterance. So much he must tell her, so much he must leave unsaid. Choosing the right thing "I do love you, you know that. Oh, darling, it's so unfair!" She beat her hands softly together.

He loved her. There it was, simple and immensely complicated. She ought to know about Laurie and 

Bunny, those two parts of himself, his shadowy heritage. But not now. It would trouble her. His father murdered his mother, a murderer, and a suicide. Out of the past a darkness had reached to him, but it was

his own. Profoundly it altered his life but it would be a long time before Helen need know it. He had become illumined to himself, he knew from whence he had sprung. Under the obvious Oliver, ran the secret writing that made him plain. "Well, the world isn't noted for fairness," he said, answering her. "Since the days of Christ." "Oliver, you sound strange. What is it?" "I love you." He held her hand, palm up in his hand. "You must believe that. Whatever meaning there is, lies in you. There's nothing else." 

Her tears fell on both hands, closed together. "Why do we have to be apart? Such a small thing to askjust to be with you all the time there is. It's not as if it would be asking anything from Brant, that he's ever had! Oliver, it rises in me, the will to live before I die!" 

He kissed the hand, tasting her tears on the cool flesh. "I know," he said, very low. "After this winter," he said. "I am sure I can manage things. Some way." 

Hope, thought Oliver. The real Siren, with a voice of honey and a smile to drive men mad. What deaths men undergo for her, how soft she speaks! Tomorrow, she says, and therefore today endure a little more. One more twist of the wheel, because perhaps, tomorrow, she whispers sweetly ——— "Yes, after this winter," assented Oliver gently.

Brant would buy a place, too. A bigger, more expensive place. For Helen and the children. To make them happy. You couldn't get away from the effect of owning property. It did something, made things tangible. If he had this house now-he shook the thought away. That was done with.

They stood in a circle, the oddly assorted group, lifting their glasses. Details struck Oliver with painful brilliance, he would never be able to forget this scene, even if after a time, he should forget the anguish. Always they would stand there, like this, with raised glasses, drinking to the new, while the old things faded in the shadow of the fire.

Helen. Her fingers curved on the frosted glass. Her face was still, he saw the clarity of her eyes, level under the long lashes. She had a funny old-fashioned pin at her throat, little blue forget-me-nots painted on ivory.

She had worn it that summer so many life-times ago, pinning up her jersey blouse, that dark thin navy blouse. He had kissed her then, the kiss was forever.

Oliver kept thinking, there is something in this evening for me, at least. A meaning beyond anything. I look at her now, I remember her as she wasmy eyes love her secretly as the tricks are counted.

A heart really makes no sound at all when it ceases to beat. It is, and then it is not. Very simple. Helen my heart is stopped. What are you thinking as you bend your head over the cards? Then I'll be tired of you

When winds are tired of blowing, thought Oliver. 

Winds go a long way. He leaned against the wall, his hand touching John Gilpin's faded red coat. 

And you'll go on galloping, he thought, and that is a good thing too. Suddenly he thought, the doctor will tear off this paper and put on modern black and silver. 

Oh God, even the house will suffer change. Laurie is gone and her lover and murderer, and the house will suffer change. But the timbers defy it. He found a kind of comfort in the thought that the timbers were steady. 

He stretched out on the sofa. He wouldn't go to bed upstairs where they lay together. The moon came in, moonlight brushed his face like a silver mothwing. He watched the fire die. Fire dies. All fire dies, he thought.

But now I know myself. I know what I am, lying here with my heart burning to ashes also.

He dozed, heavy with fatigue and comforted by the sly warmth of whisky.

Tomorrow You are the desire of my body, and the faith of my

soul. I love you, and since I cannot give you the stars,

I give you the life of a man. My mind thirsts for you, death inhabits my flesh because I shall not have you, and yet-I know myself, I am Oliver Heron, a man, to live out my destiny with courage, as Laurie lived out hers. 

Laurie and Helen. Unknown and known. But I have been part of both bodies, I have been in beauty. I am myself, Oliver Heron. 

He woke, and found the sun up. Moving without sound, he crossed the room, his feet falling for the last time on the mellowed boards those others had trod. In his room, he lifted up his bag. In the bathroom he plunged his head in cold water, and cupping his hand, drank some. The coolness was grateful to his burned throat.

A last look at the nursery with the little brave soldiers and the rockinghorse. Good-bye, old fellow. Good-bye little boy I never knew. Time for Laurie's child to face the world.

Before their closed door, he stopped. His hands suddenly clenched, he felt the palms sweating. Not to be endured the agony and yet to be endured because he was 

Oliver Heron. Behind that blank shut door lay Helen beside Brant, her hair on the pillow, her hands open, in the way he knew she slept. Her mouth was the mouth of a child when she slept. And her tumbled hair made a cloud of brightness on the pillow. Remembered ecstasy drained his blood. Between him and this ecstasy was a closed door, blank and wooden.

He stood there a long moment, motionless, cold sweat on his forehead and the roof of his mouth dry. "Helen," his lips formed her name, "Helen, Helen!"

There was no sound at all. 

Taking a step forward he closed one hand on the chill metal latch. His sweat moistened the iron. "Dear-dear

Rigid, his fingers locked on the latch. He took his free hand and unclosed them. Slowly he crept down the stairs, the breath rattling in his throat. Who was it said a man could die but once?

With every descending step something inside him seemed to scream with agony. He endured, he got down the stairs, he left the house, he endured, the breath still fought in his lungs, his heart rolled a sullen thunder in his temples.

Outside, Oliver straightened. He wiped the sweat from his face. The sun stood clear above the hills and the meadow mist was rising. Never again would he lie beside 

Helen while she slept, watching the silver mist rise over that meadow.

The air was like glass, every leaf glittered, the grass shone with dew. The windows caught the first flush of the sun, and shone too, silver.

When he reached the rise of road and looked back, he saw the morning sun full on every window of the little white house. Serene and steadfast it stood, as it had always stood, and time was no more to it than the falling of leaves in the yard. 

The birds were awake now, singing tentative morning songs. Mr. Paul rattled out of his yard on the farm wagon and Tessie came to the back door with a pan of chicken feed. Life was going on, the earth yielding her harvest, and life was a good thing. On the main highway, out of sight, the early bus roared by.

Squaring his shoulders, Oliver went down the rutted winding road. His hand, in his pocket, closed on the list of agencies. The hand was damp with sweat but his fingers closed over the papers with strength. 

In the swamp, a little maple lifted a solitary branch of flame.
